Weekly Insights | Feb 12, 2026

Executive Summary

Corn – WASDE Tightens Marginally as Exports Keep
the Front End Supported
Corn drifted modestly lower through the week, with March
futures settling at $4.28, down roughly five cents week over
week and briefly testing the $4.30 area midweek. Despite
the softer flat price, spreads continued to reflect a market
anchored more by execution than by storage economics.
The March–May spread held near an eight-cent carry, still
short of full carry and consistent with limited commercial
incentive to widen structure aggressively.

February WASDE delivered a targeted but constructive

demand-side adjustment, raising exports and trimming ending stocks in recognition of a shipment pace
that remains well ahead of last year. For the week ending February 6, export inspections totaled
approximately 1.308 million metric tons, lifting cumulative shipments to near 34 million metric tons and
reinforcing a supportive export trajectory. Cash markets remained firm across key corridors, with Gulf
values steady and interior bids showing only marginal softness despite incremental improvements in
winter logistics. Managed money remains net short, but the position has continued to unwind, suggesting
reduced conviction to press the downside absent deterioration in physical flow. Flat price remains
rangebound, yet the combination of incremental balance-sheet tightening, strong shipment execution, and
stable front-end structure continues to limit downside risk.

Wheat – Inspections Improve, but WASDE Keeps the Broader Tone Supply-Heavy
Wheat futures were steady to slightly weaker, with March Chicago SRW settling near $5.33, essentially
unchanged on the week. The February WASDE delivered only modest adjustments, leaving the global
balance sheet broadly intact and reinforcing a market that remains structurally well supplied. Export
inspections improved to approximately 0.58 million metric tons, pushing cumulative shipments near 17.3
million metric tons and keeping wheat shipments modestly ahead of last year’s pace. However, the
improvement remains insufficient to materially shift sentiment within an otherwise heavy supply
framework. Cash markets showed little movement, with Gulf values broadly steady and interior bids
reflecting comfortable coverage. Managed money continues to hold a sizable net short, and with Black
Sea supply still defining the global benchmark, rallies remain vulnerable without a clearer catalyst tied to
weather, logistics, or policy disruption.

Soybeans – WASDE Adds South American Weight as Domestic Demand Remains the
Anchor

Cash markets, however, remain firm, supported by strong processor bids and sustained crush pull.
Managed money expanded net length to roughly 28,800 contracts, increasing downside sensitivity should

Macro / Policy – WASDE Week Dominates as Biofuels and Trade Headlines Stay in the Mix

Macro drivers remained secondary to WASDE positioning this week, though biofuel and trade narratives
influenced sentiment at the margin. Ethanol fundamentals softened in the latest weekly print, with
production declining to 0.956 million barrels per day and stocks at 25.136 million barrels for the week
ending January 30. The decline appears weather-driven rather than indicative of structural demand
erosion, interrupting but not reversing the broader stabilization trend. Within the soy complex, renewable
diesel policy continues to shape the tone of the market more than daily export flow, with attention
centered on how federal credit structures translate into tangible margin support for soybean oil demand.
Trade headlines circulated but lacked confirmation, and the market remains reluctant to price in policy-
driven demand until transactions appear in the export data. With spreads and basis continuing to provide
clearer real-time signals across grains, macro remains a backdrop rather than a driver, even as
participants monitor policy follow-through following WASDE adjustments.

Weather – US Logistics Improve; Argentina Remains the Volatility Point

In the United States, near-term weather remains operationally supportive. Warmer temperatures have
improved winter logistics and reduced freeze-related constraints, while wheat dormancy conditions
remain stable with adequate snow cover across portions of the Plains and no meaningful winterkill
headlines emerging.

In South America, Brazil continues to experience broadly supportive conditions, with harvest accelerating
in Mato Grosso and safrinha planting advancing under generally favorable moisture profiles. Localized
excess rainfall in southern states has slowed fieldwork intermittently but has not introduced systemic risk.
Argentina remains the focal point for volatility, with moisture deficits persisting across portions of Buenos
Aires, La Pampa, and Córdoba as corn enters pollination and soybeans move through pod-setting.
Forecast models indicate scattered 10–20 mm rainfall over the next 7–10 days, offering partial relief but
not a full recharge of soil moisture. No confirmed large-scale yield losses have been embedded into
official estimates, yet the coming two weeks remain critical in determining whether stress translates into
measurable production impact. Weather therefore represents a modest and conditional upside risk
layered on top of an otherwise supply-heavy baseline.

What’s Next (Feb 11 – Feb 18):

Export inspections: confirm corn shipment strength

Export sales: post-WASDE follow-through and any daily flashes

Argentina: rainfall coverage and crop stress signal

Brazil: harvest pace and safrinha planting progress

EIA ethanol: production and stocks after the weather dip

Positioning: fund short covering vs renewed selling

Corn Market Deep Dive

Post-WASDE Balance Sheet: February WASDE introduced a targeted demand-side adjustment to the
US corn balance sheet while leaving production unchanged at a record 17.021 bbu on 186.5 bpa across
91.3 million harvested acres. The meaningful revision came on exports, which USDA raised 100 mbu to
3.300 bbu in response to sustained shipment strength through the first half of the marketing year. Feed
and residual use remained at 6.200 bbu, and the export increase reduced ending stocks to 2.127 bbu
from 2.227 bbu in January, trimming stocks-to-use to roughly 14 percent while keeping the balance sheet
firmly in surplus territory.

February did not reverse January’s supply reset. It acknowledged that demand, particularly for export
execution, has been stronger than initially assumed. The balance sheet remains loose by historical
standards, but the adjustment shifts the focus squarely toward whether shipment pace can remain strong
enough to prevent renewed stock accumulation into the back half of the year.

Futures & Spreads: March corn settled at $4.28/bu, down roughly five cents on the week following the
WASDE release and briefly testing the $4.30 area midweek. Despite softer flat price, structure remained
composed. The March–May spread held near an eight-cent carry, roughly half of full commercial carry
under standard cost assumptions, signaling recognition of surplus without forcing a full storage incentive.
The curve is paying carry, but it is not being forced wider. That stability suggests commercials remain
comfortable owning nearby coverage at current replacement values. The market is acknowledging
surplus, but it is not pricing distress

Funds & Positioning: According to the latest CFTC Commitments of Traders report (week ending
February 3), managed money holds a net short of 68,786 contracts in corn futures, marking a meaningful
reduction from prior extremes and reinforcing the ongoing short-covering dynamic rather than renewed
bearish expansion. Gross short exposure remains elevated relative to gross longs, yet February WASDE
did not trigger renewed bearish expansion. Positioning now reflects reduced downside conviction rather
than active bullish reallocation, leaving corn less vulnerable to disorderly fund-driven pressure than during
January’s supply repricing phase.

Cash & Basis: Cash markets continue to reinforce front-end stability. CIF Gulf corn barges were bid in
the 109–117¢ over March range, broadly steady versus prior prints and consistent with stable exporter
coverage. FOB Gulf export values remained near the low-$220s per metric ton equivalent, preserving
competitive positioning into key destinations. Interior river bids softened only marginally despite improved
winter logistics and more fluid movement. Freight conditions have stabilized, although residual low-water
constraints continue to limit full normalization in barge economics. Basis behavior suggests supply is
available but not burdensome, with cash absorbing futures weakness rather than amplifying it.

Export Sales & Inspections: Weekly export inspections totaled approximately 1.308 mmt for the week
ending February 5, lifting cumulative shipments to near 34 mmt and keeping pace materially ahead of last
year. Execution remains the most constructive component of the balance sheet and directly supported
USDA’s February export increase to 3.300 bbu.

For the week ending January 29, weekly export sales exceeded 1.0 mmt, with cumulative commitments
near 58.7 mmt, roughly 72 percent of USDA’s full-year forecast. That level aligns with seasonal norms but
remains supportive given the earlier shipment strength. Demand continues to surface on price breaks
rather than retreat from them, reinforcing execution as the market’s primary stabilizer.

Global Context: Globally, February WASDE left the corn balance sheet broadly comfortable, with world
ending stocks near 289 mmt. Large crops in the United States and China continue to anchor supply, and
no material downward revisions were introduced among major exporters. Brazil’s second-crop planting
remains on pace following early soybean harvest, and Argentine production expectations are materially
improved relative to last year despite localized dryness. In the absence of supply deterioration abroad,
global stocks continue to function as a ceiling on balance-sheet-driven rallies. Corn remains a market
trading execution rather than scarcity.

So What? February refined the corn narrative without altering its foundation. Supply is known, stocks
remain elevated, and surplus conditions persist. However, export strength, firm Gulf basis, and gradual
short covering continue to define the lower boundary of price discovery. Corn is no longer pricing
tightening risk, but neither is it trading as distressed surplus. With January’s supply reset largely
absorbed, the next directional move depends on whether shipment pace and ethanol grind sustain
enough momentum to prevent renewed stock rebuilding later in the marketing year.

Soybean Market Deep Dive

Post-WASDE Balance Sheet: February WASDE left the US soybean balance sheet largely unchanged
while lifting global supply expectations. US production was maintained at 4.262 bbu on 53.0 bpa across
80.4 million harvested acres, confirming that domestic supply assumptions are settled. Exports were left
at 1.575 bbu despite continued lagging shipment pace, while crush was maintained at 2.570 bbu,
underscoring USDA’s confidence in domestic processing demand. Ending stocks held at 350 mbu,
keeping stocks-to-use near 8 percent and confirming that January’s loosening remains intact.

The more meaningful adjustment occurred globally. USDA raised Brazilian production again, lifting world
ending stocks to roughly 125 mmt and reinforcing expanding South American availability entering peak
export season. February did not tighten the balance sheet. It reinforced that domestic crush must
continue to offset export underperformance.

Futures & Spreads: March soybeans settled at $11.20/bu, down roughly ten cents on the week as global
supply revisions limited follow-through buying after January’s weather-driven rally. The March–May
spread held in carry near 12–13 cents, a little over half of full commercial carry under standard cost
assumptions. The forward curve remains composed. Carry persists, but the market has not moved toward
a full storage incentive. With Brazil’s export program ramping into Q1, coverage looks adequate and the
market is not being pulled into inverse.

Products & Crush: NOPA January crush, released February 3, totaled approximately 225 million
bushels, one of the strongest January volumes on record and consistent with near-capacity processing
rates. Soybean oil stocks remain relatively tight relative to crush volume, reinforcing renewable diesel
demand as a durable absorber of incremental supply. USDA’s decision to maintain crush at 2.570 bbu in
February underscores domestic processing as the most stable demand component within the balance
sheet. Despite softer export performance, positive crush margins continue to support interior processor
bids and provide structural stability to flat price behavior.

Cash & Basis: CIF NOLA soybean barges were bid around 95–97¢ over March, softening through the

week as Brazilian shipments expanded and exporter urgency diminished. FOB Gulf soybean offers
remained pressured relative to South American origin, reflecting competitive Brazilian FOB values and
narrowing arbitrage windows. In contrast, interior processor bids remained firm, supported by sustained
crush margins and consistent plant demand. The widening divergence between export basis and
processor basis reinforces that US soybeans are currently valued more through domestic consumption
channels than export replacement demand during Brazil’s dominant shipment window.

Funds & Positioning: According to the latest CFTC Commitments of Traders report (week ending
February 3), managed money holds a net long of approximately +28,832 contracts in soybean futures.
Gross length expanded following the late-January rally, while short positions were trimmed modestly.
Positioning is not extreme by historical standards, but the expansion in net length increases downside
sensitivity. Should Argentine rainfall improve or export data fail to accelerate, the market becomes more
vulnerable to long liquidation rather than fresh buying.

Export Sales & Inspections: Weekly export inspections totaled approximately 1.14 mmt for the week
ending February 5, keeping cumulative shipments sharply below last year’s pace at this stage of the
marketing year. Weekly export sales slowed meaningfully, and cumulative commitments stand near 34.3
mmt, roughly 80 percent of USDA’s full-year forecast and slightly below the historical seasonal pace.
Intermittent daily flash sales have surfaced, but they have not translated into sustained shipment
acceleration. USDA’s decision to leave exports unchanged in February reflects caution rather than
validation. Any future upward revision depends on measurable improvement in execution later in the
marketing year.

Global Context: February WASDE raised world soybean ending stocks to approximately 125 mmt,
driven primarily by a higher Brazilian production estimate near 176 mmt. Argentina’s crop outlook remains
weather-sensitive but materially improved relative to last year’s drought-reduced baseline. As Brazilian
harvest advances and export capacity ramps up, global availability expands into Q1 and maintains
competitive pressure on US export channels. Without disruption in South America, global supply functions
as a structural cap on sustained rallies.

So What? February confirmed that soybeans remain internally supported by crush but externally
constrained by global supply expansion. The US balance sheet is stable but not tightening, export flow
remains under pressure, and structure reflects adequate coverage rather than scarcity. Soybeans are not
pricing shortage. They are balancing resilient domestic processing against expanding South American
availability. Absent a material shift in export execution or a weather-driven supply disruption, price
discovery is likely to remain rangebound and increasingly sensitive to positioning rather than balance-
sheet tightening.

Wheat Market Deep Dive

Post-WASDE Balance Sheet: February WASDE delivered only marginal adjustments to the US wheat
balance sheet, reinforcing an already heavy supply structure. US production was left unchanged at 1.985
bbu on 53.3 bpa across 37.2 million harvested acres, confirming that supply assumptions remain settled.
Demand-side changes were minimal. Exports were maintained at 900 mbu and food use held steady,
leaving US ending stocks at 931 mbu, up slightly from January and keeping stocks-to-use near 45
percent. February did not introduce tightening. It reaffirmed that domestic supply continues to exceed
demand requirements comfortably.

Futures & Spreads: March Chicago SRW wheat settled at $5.33/bu, finishing essentially unchanged on

the week and being held within established trading ranges. Structure remained firmly in carry, with the
March–May spread near a 9¢ carry, roughly mid-50 percent of full commercial carry under standard
assumptions, reflecting ample supply but not a curve fully incentivizing storage. The market looks
balanced rather than distressed, but it is still pricing storage economics, not scarcity.

Cash & Basis: FOB Gulf SRW values hovered near $235/mt, while FOB Black Sea offers near $228–
230/mt continued to cap US competitiveness. Interior basis across SRW and HRW regions remained soft,
generally 50–60¢ under March futures, with mills well covered and showing little urgency to extend bids.
Basis continues to respond more to global competition than to domestic availability. Physical markets
remain adequately supplied, with no evidence of emerging tightness.

Export Sales & Inspections: Weekly export inspections totaled approximately 0.58 mmt for the week
ending February 5, lifting cumulative shipments to near 17.3 mmt. Shipments are modestly ahead of last
year’s pace but not at a rate sufficient to materially alter balance-sheet tone. Weekly export sales were
seasonally constructive but unexceptional, consistent with maintaining USDA’s current export forecast
rather than signaling acceleration. Flow remains steady into traditional destinations, yet volumes are
insufficient to challenge the broader supply-heavy narrative.

Global Context: February WASDE left the global wheat balance sheet broadly unchanged, with world
ending stocks near 278 mmt, elevated relative to historical norms. Russian exportable supply remains
ample, and Black Sea FOB values continue to anchor global pricing and define the marginal export
benchmark. Argentina’s production recovery adds incremental exportable volume, while EU supplies
remain adequate. With no meaningful reductions to global output or inventories, international supply
continues to act as a ceiling on sustained rallies, positioning US wheat firmly as a price-taker in global
trade rather than a price-setter.

Funds & Positioning: According to the latest CFTC report (week ending February 3), managed money
holds a net short of approximately –81,755 contracts in CBOT SRW wheat. The heavy short leaves the
market vulnerable to episodic short-covering rallies on weather or geopolitical headlines. However,
positioning alone has not altered the prevailing trend. Absent fundamental tightening, rallies driven by
fund covering have remained corrective rather than structural.

So What? February reaffirmed wheat as the loosest balance sheet within the grain complex. Domestic
stocks remain elevated, global inventories are comfortable, and structure continues to reward storage
over urgency. Export demand is steady but insufficient to tighten conditions, and cash markets show little
stress. Without disruption in a major exporting region or a material shift in global demand, wheat is likely
to remain rangebound, driven more by positioning and spread behavior than by fundamental tightening.

US Cash Market & Basis

Corn: Corn basis remained firm relative to futures, reflecting steady export replacement demand even as
the board consolidated following February WASDE. CIF Gulf corn barges were bid in the 109–117¢/bu
over March range, broadly unchanged versus prior prints and consistent with stable exporter coverage.
FOB Gulf export offers held in the low-100s over March, reinforcing that nearby demand remains
engaged despite confirmation of ample supply. Interior bids were largely unchanged to slightly softer,
consistent with improved winter logistics and more fluid movement, but not indicative of burdensome
availability. River conditions have stabilized and freight has normalized modestly, yet basis behavior
continues to signal active commercial ownership rather than defensive coverage. As in recent weeks, the
cash market absorbed futures weakness rather than amplifying it, reinforcing corn’s position as the most
resilient leg of the grain complex on a replacement-value basis.

Soybeans: Soybean basis softened in export channels while remaining comparatively supported in
processing regions. CIF Gulf soybean barges were bid near 95–97¢/bu over March futures, and FOB Gulf
offers hovered around 115–118¢/bu over March, reflecting reduced urgency from exporters as Brazilian
shipments accelerated. In contrast, interior processor bids remained firm, supported by strong crush
margins and sustained run rates following January’s robust NOPA crush. The widening divergence
between export basis and processor basis underscores that US soybeans are currently priced more
through domestic consumption than through export replacement demand during Brazil’s peak shipment
window. Crushers are providing the floor. Export channels remain price-takers in a competitive global
environment.

Wheat: Wheat basis remained soft and largely directionless, consistent with a market anchored by carry
and global competition. FOB Gulf SRW wheat values hovered near $235/mt, while Black Sea origin
remained competitive near $228–230/mt FOB, limiting US export pricing flexibility and reinforcing the
external ceiling. Interior basis across SRW and HRW regions held generally 50–60¢ under March futures,
with mills well covered and showing little urgency to extend bids. Lower-priced Black Sea and Argentine
supplies continue to pressure US competitiveness, reinforcing the storage-oriented signal embedded in
futures structure. Cash markets offer little indication of tightening.

So What? Cash markets continue to absorb balance-sheet adjustments without signaling stress, but tone
remains selective rather than uniformly supportive.Corn basis stands out as the most constructive, with
export replacement values holding in the 109–117¢ range even as futures remain rangebound. In
soybeans, the split between weaker export bids and firm processor bids reinforces crush, not exports, as
the primary near-term anchor. Wheat remains the weakest component, with basis offering little resistance
amid ample global supply and carry-driven structure. With logistics improving and no immediate demand
shock, nearby cash strength reflects end-user coverage rather than scarcity. Basis can remain stable, but
absent a new catalyst, sustained upside appears limited.

International Cash & Trade Flows

Brazil: Brazil’s role as the dominant near-term supplier continues to solidify as soybean harvest
accelerates and export capacity expands. ANEC estimates January soybean exports near 3.23 mmt, with
corn loadings around 3.39 mmt, both running ahead of last year’s pace and reinforcing Brazil’s early-year
export leadership. FOB Paranaguá soybean values continue to trade at a meaningful discount to US Gulf
origin, with US soybeans priced roughly $50/mt above Brazilian FOB on recent comparisons. That
differential preserves Brazil’s pricing advantage into Asia and the Middle East and continues to pressure
US export competitiveness during peak shipment season.

Corn shipments have slowed seasonally following the late-2025 safrinha surge, yet Brazilian corn FOB
Santos values near $220–223/mt remain competitive relative to US Gulf and Argentine origin. Brazil
remains firmly embedded in global feed grain channels despite the seasonal transition. Harvest progress
across Mato Grosso and Goiás has improved amid favorable weather windows, and port operations
remain fluid with no widespread congestion. Earlier-season moisture continues to anchor expectations for
a large soybean crop, reinforcing Brazil’s position as the preferred origin into Q1.

Argentina: Argentina remains an active and reliable participant in global trade, particularly in corn and
soybean meal. Up-River corn offers remain competitively priced relative to US Gulf origin, while soymeal
pricing continues to anchor Argentina’s role as a primary supplier of global protein meal.

Despite persistent dryness across parts of Buenos Aires and La Pampa, export channels have not been
materially disrupted. Currency dynamics continue to support exporter margins and farmer marketing
flexibility, sustaining shipment flow. Weather remains a monitored variable for corn and soybeans in

reproductive stages, but from a trade perspective Argentina continues to function as a dependable
supplier rather than a source of disruption.

China: Chinese participation remains tactical rather than structural. Confirmed US soybean purchases
have occurred intermittently but appear state-directed and opportunistic rather than reflective of broad
commercial restocking. With US soybeans priced roughly $50/mt above Brazilian origin FOB, private
crushers continue to favor South American supply. No sustained arbitrage window has emerged to
materially accelerate US corn or wheat imports. China’s engagement remains episodic and price-
sensitive, providing marginal support rather than altering global flow dynamics.

EU / Black Sea: Black Sea origins continue to anchor global price ceilings. Russian and Ukrainian wheat
FOB values cluster near $228–235/mt, preserving a competitive advantage in major import destinations
and limiting upside for higher-cost exporters. Ukrainian corn remains competitively priced into
Mediterranean and EU markets, reinforcing feed grain competition. With export corridors functioning and
supplies ample, Black Sea flows continue to define the marginal clearing price rather than respond to US
movements. The ceiling effect remains intact.

Other Regions: Australia continues to move wheat into Asian markets following a solid harvest, while
Canadian spring wheat exports proceed in line with seasonal norms. No material trade restrictions, quota
shifts, or policy disruptions have emerged across secondary exporters. The broader international
environment remains stable and well supplied.

So What? Global trade flows remain firmly dictated by price leadership. Brazil continues to anchor
soybean and corn exports, Russia dominates wheat, and Argentina’s role is increasingly contingent on
weather and producer selling behavior. China’s presence provides occasional demand support but lacks
consistency. Against this backdrop, US export competitiveness remains opportunistic rather than
structural, dependent on timing, logistics, or short-term disruptions elsewhere. Until global FOB spreads
narrow or supply risks materially reshape availability, the US remains a secondary origin in the global
trade hierarchy.

Key Fundamentals & Reports (Feb 05 – Feb 12)

USDA WASDE – February 2026 (released Feb 10): February WASDE delivered targeted demand-side
adjustments rather than another broad supply reset. For corn, USDA held production steady at 17.021
bbu but raised exports by 100 mbu to 3.300 bbu, trimming US ending stocks to 2.127 bbu from 2.227 bbu
in January and tightening stocks-to-use modestly into the mid-14 percent range. The revision formalized
shipment strength that had already been evident in weekly inspections rather than introducing a new
tightening impulse.

Soybean balance sheets were largely unchanged domestically, with US production held at 4.262 bbu,
crush maintained at 2.570 bbu, exports steady at 1.575 bbu, and ending stocks left at 350 mbu. The more
meaningful adjustment came globally, where USDA raised Brazilian production again, lifting world ending
stocks and reinforcing a supply-heavy international backdrop. Wheat adjustments were minimal, with US
ending stocks near 931 mbu and stocks-to-use holding around 45 percent. Globally, wheat supply
remains ample, keeping export competition intense and limiting balance-sheet-driven upside.

Export Inspections (USDA FGIS – week ending Feb 5): Export inspections continued to differentiate
demand strength across the complex. Corn inspections totaled 1.308 mmt, lifting cumulative shipments to
roughly 34 mmt and keeping exports well ahead of last year’s pace, validating USDA’s February export
increase. Soybean inspections reached 1.14 mmt, with cumulative shipments still sharply below last year,
underscoring continued export underperformance during Brazil’s peak shipment window. Wheat

inspections improved to 0.58 mmt, bringing cumulative shipments near 17.3 mmt and keeping exports
modestly ahead of last year, though not at a pace sufficient to materially alter the broader supply-heavy
tone.

Export Sales (USDA FAS – latest through week ending Jan 29): Weekly export sales reflected a
mixed but stable demand profile. Corn net sales exceeded 1.0 mmt, with cumulative commitments near
58.7 mmt, roughly 72 percent of USDA’s current export forecast and consistent with strong execution
despite seasonal moderation. Soybean net sales slowed meaningfully, with cumulative commitments near
34.3 mmt, approximately 80 percent of the annual projection but trailing the historical seasonal pace,
reinforcing that exports remain the weakest leg of the demand complex.

Wheat sales were constructive but unexceptional, aligning with inspection data that suggest steady rather
than accelerating flow. Daily sales announcements during the period included corn and soybean
transactions to China and unknown destinations, reinforcing that demand continues to surface
opportunistically on price breaks even as aggregate weekly totals fluctuate.

NOPA Soybean Crush – January (released Feb 3): NOPA crush data reinforced the strength of
domestic soybean processing. January crush volumes reached approximately 225 million bushels, one of
the strongest January runs on record, confirming sustained high utilization amid favorable margins.
Soybean oil stocks remain relatively tight relative to crush volume, supporting the view that renewable
diesel demand continues to absorb incremental supply. The persistence of strong crush justifies USDA’s
decision to maintain elevated domestic demand assumptions despite ongoing export weakness.

CFTC Positioning (latest available – week ending Feb 3): Speculative positioning remains uneven
across markets. In corn, managed money holds a net short of –68,786 contracts, reflecting gradual short
covering and diminished downside conviction rather than outright bullish reallocation. Soybeans carry a
net long of 28,832 contracts following the late-January rally, increasing downside sensitivity should South
American risk ease or export flow fail to improve. Wheat remains the most asymmetric market, with
managed money net short 81,755 SRW contracts, leaving the board vulnerable to short-covering rallies
even as underlying fundamentals remain loose.

So What? February’s data flow reinforced a market increasingly driven by execution rather than
estimation. Corn remains the most defensible balance sheet, with exports and ethanol providing tangible
support despite ample supply. Soybeans are anchored by domestic crush but expanding global
availability and weak export flow continue to cap rallies. Wheat fundamentals remain the heaviest, with
global supply and positioning dictating price behavior more than demand acceleration. With WASDE
revisions largely absorbed, inspections, sales, basis, spreads, and positioning remain the clearest real-
time signals for price discovery as the market transitions into the back half of February.

International Reports

Brazil: CONAB’s February survey maintained Brazil’s soybean production near 176 mmt, unchanged
from January, reinforcing that the “big crop” narrative is now institutionally embedded rather than weather-
contingent. Total grain output remains projected near 353 mmt, keeping 2025/26 positioned as a record
season in aggregate. Harvest continues to advance across the Center-West, and safrinha planting is
unfolding behind combines within seasonal norms. The institutional signal is one of confirmation rather
than adjustment. No downward revision has been introduced, and official confidence in production
remains intact. Brazil’s supply profile is therefore institutionally validated and embedded in global balance
sheets.

Argentina: Argentina’s position in global trade is being shaped more by crop recovery and currency
dynamics than by short-term export competitiveness. MAGyP projections continue to indicate materially
improved production prospects versus last year’s drought-reduced baseline, particularly in soybeans and
corn, reinforcing normalization across the oilseed and feedgrain complex. Localized dryness remains
under monitoring across portions of Buenos Aires, La Pampa, and Córdoba, yet national production
expectations remain far above 2023/24 levels. Forecast models suggest scattered 10–20 mm rainfall
across key areas, potentially stabilizing conditions but not fully replenishing uneven soil moisture profiles.

China: China’s grain demand profile remains measured and policy-guided rather than market-driven.
Domestic corn and soybean prices remain elevated relative to global benchmarks, yet import behavior
continues to be tactical and price sensitive. US soybeans remain priced roughly $50/mt above Brazilian
origin on an FOB basis, reinforcing why private crushers have favored South American supply during
Brazil’s peak export window. State-directed purchases appear opportunistic, with no evidence of broad
commercial restocking.

EU / Black Sea: Black Sea supply continues to anchor the global wheat balance sheet structurally.
Russian export flows remain steady under existing policy frameworks, and export taxes have not
materially constrained shipment pace. FOB Black Sea wheat values clustered near $228–$235/mt
maintain a competitive edge in key import destinations and reinforce the region’s role as marginal price-
setter. Ukraine’s export corridors remain functional despite ongoing geopolitical tension, preserving corn
and wheat flows into EU and Mediterranean destinations. Western Europe remains comparatively less
competitive in export tenders, further solidifying the Black Sea’s structural pricing influence. Rather than
responding to US price moves, Black Sea origins continue to define the global ceiling.

So What? International developments continue to reinforce a structurally comfortable global supply
environment. Brazil’s crop size is increasingly validated and embedded into balance sheets, Argentina
represents recovery rather than risk, and Black Sea flows remain uninterrupted and price-defining.
China’s participation remains tactical and policy-managed rather than demand-led. Unlike prior years
where global trade disruptions created asymmetric price risk, February’s international backdrop is
characterized by stability and competitive pricing. In this environment, upside requires a disruption that is
not currently visible, while downside remains governed by execution and demand follow-through rather
than structural tightening

Weather & Crop Conditions

United States
Weather remains a secondary driver for US grain markets, though short-term patterns have been
operationally supportive. Temperatures across much of the Midwest and Plains have trended near to
slightly above seasonal norms, limiting freeze-related logistics disruptions and allowing river systems to
function more consistently following earlier winter constraints. Snow cover across key HRW wheat areas
in Kansas, Oklahoma, and Colorado remains adequate, reducing immediate winterkill risk despite
intermittent cold shots. NOAA’s 6–10 and 8–14 day outlooks continue to favor moderation rather than
extreme cold, reinforcing dormancy stability for winter wheat and limiting headline-driven volatility. With
harvest complete and no active fieldwork underway, US weather remains neutral from a supply
standpoint, functioning primarily as a logistics variable rather than a production catalyst at this stage of
the calendar.

Brazil
Brazilian weather continues to validate large production expectations, with harvest progress accelerating
during recent drier windows across the Center-West. Mato Grosso soybean harvest has moved into the
30–40 percent range, recovering from early-season delays and aligning more closely with seasonal
norms, while safrinha corn planting has expanded into the 20–30 percent range behind combines.

Rainfall earlier in the season maintained favorable soil moisture profiles across Goiás and Mato Grosso
do Sul, anchoring yield potential and reinforcing CONAB’s expectation for a soybean crop near 176 mmt.
In southern states, including Paraná and Rio Grande do Sul, localized excess moisture has slowed
fieldwork intermittently, yet no widespread crop loss or systemic quality deterioration has been confirmed.

Forecast models indicate continued periodic rainfall across central Brazil, supporting late soybean
development and early safrinha establishment without introducing systemic risk. The broader weather
narrative remains stabilizing rather than deteriorating, reinforcing production confidence rather than
injecting new uncertainty.

Argentina
Argentina remains the key weather-sensitive region within the global complex. Dryness persists across
portions of Buenos Aires, La Pampa, and Córdoba as corn enters pollination and soybeans progress
through pod-setting stages. Rainfall coverage over the past week has been uneven, with scattered totals
generally in the 10–20 mm range in some areas, insufficient to fully replenish soil moisture deficits in the
most stressed zones.

The next 10–14 days remain critical, particularly for first-crop corn and early soybeans, where yield
potential is most sensitive to moisture availability. While crop ratings have softened in certain regions, no
confirmed large-scale production loss has yet been embedded into official estimates. Forecast guidance
continues to suggest periodic precipitation opportunities, though coverage and intensity remain uncertain.
Weather in Argentina therefore introduces asymmetric risk, not confirmed loss, but elevated sensitivity
relative to Brazil and the United States.

Global
Outside South America, crop conditions remain broadly stable. Northern Hemisphere winter wheat across
the Black Sea, EU, and US Plains remains largely dormant without widespread stress reports. Russia and
Ukraine have not reported meaningful winter damage, and Australia’s harvest has concluded without
weather-related logistical disruption. Across Canada and Western Europe, conditions remain seasonally
normal. At the global level, weather outside Argentina remains a background variable rather than an
active driver of price formation.

So What?
Weather influence remains differentiated rather than uniform. Brazil reinforces supply confidence as
harvest and safrinha planting advance under generally supportive conditions. The United States remains
operationally neutral, with dormancy stability and improving logistics limiting friction. Argentina stands
alone as the primary volatility source, where reproductive-stage crops face uneven moisture profiles
during a critical window. For now, weather represents conditional upside risk rather than confirmed supply
loss, meaning markets remain anchored by February’s supply-heavy baseline unless Argentine stress
intensifies measurably in the coming weeks.

Synthesis & Outlook

February’s data flow confirmed that the grain complex has transitioned from balance-sheet discovery into
a phase defined by confirmation and execution. January delivered the supply reset. February validated it.
USDA revisions were incremental rather than structural, and international institutional updates reinforced
stability rather than introducing new tightening impulses. Prices have stabilized into defined ranges,
spreads remain orderly, and basis continues to carry disproportionate signaling weight. The market is no
longer repricing supply; it is testing whether demand can consistently absorb it without erosion.

Corn remains the most balanced leg of the complex. Record production and a still-elevated carryout cap
structural upside, yet February’s export increase formalized what shipment data had already been
validated. Export execution continues to outperform seasonal norms, Gulf replacement values remain

firm, and managed money has reduced short exposure without aggressively re-adding length. Corn has
repriced abundance without transitioning into full storage-driven stress. As long as inspections and
ethanol grind avoid deterioration, downside risk appears controlled rather than disorderly.

Soybeans remain internally supported but externally constrained. Domestic crush continues to operate at
historically strong levels, reinforcing processors as the primary stabilizing force. At the same time, Brazil’s
officially confirmed 176 mmt crop and accelerating export window maintain structural pressure on US
export channels. Argentina introduces weather sensitivity, but no institutional downgrade has yet been
embedded into balance sheets. February did not materially alter that equilibrium. The result remains a
range-bound structure shaped by resilient domestic demand on one side and expanding South American
availability on the other.

Wheat continues to carry the heaviest balance sheet profile. February introduced no tightening impulse
domestically or globally. Russian export duties remain at zero, Black Sea FOB values continue to anchor
the global ceiling, and US stocks-to-use remains elevated. Export execution has stabilized but not
accelerated. With supply comfort validated across official channels, wheat remains a price-taker rather
than a price-setter, and rallies remain positioning-driven rather than fundamentally reinforced.

Bullish triggers: Upside risk remains conditional rather than embedded. For corn, sustained inspections
combined with normalization in ethanol output would further compress the effective nearby balance
despite ample stocks. In soybeans, a measurable weather-driven production loss in Argentina or a
structural acceleration in Chinese buying beyond tactical procurement would materially shift the demand
calculus. Wheat upside remains the most disruption-dependent, requiring either export surprises or
supply friction in a major exporting region rather than incremental domestic improvement alone.

Bearish triggers: Downside risk depends on erosion rather than revelation. For corn, a measurable
slowdown in export execution following February’s upward revision would reopen the path toward wider
carries and deeper storage economics. Soybeans remain vulnerable to uninterrupted Brazilian export
pressure if logistics remain fluid and US shipments fail to improve later in the marketing year. Wheat
continues to face structural headwinds from aggressive Black Sea competition, with elevated global
stocks limiting the market’s ability to absorb incremental supply without price concession.

Watchlist (Feb 12–Feb 19):

Argentina: Do forecasted rains meaningfully improve soil moisture, or does stress begin to translate into
condition downgrades?

Corn exports: Does shipment pace remain strong enough to justify February’s upward revision?

Soybeans: Any measurable improvement in US export execution during Brazil’s peak window?

Ethanol: Signs of production normalization following the late-January weather dip?

Positioning: Does fund short covering extend in corn and wheat, or stall without a new catalyst?

In summary: With February’s WASDE and international releases confirming rather than redefining the
supply landscape, grain markets are operating inside a validated baseline of global supply comfort.
Brazil’s crop is institutionally embedded, Russia’s export regime remains open, China’s policy stance is
stable, and Argentina has not yet introduced official downgrades. Physical signals, basis behavior, spread
structure, shipment pace, and positioning, now serve as the clearest guideposts for price discovery. Risk
remains asymmetrical. Upside requires disruption. Downside requires demand erosion. Until either

Executive Summary

Corn – Supply Reset Pressures Futures, but Export
Flow Continues to Anchor the Front End
March corn settled at $4.22/bu on January 13, down nearly
25¢ on Monday, as the January USDA reports finalized a
record 2025 crop and lifted it expectation for new-crop US
ending stocks to 2.227 bbu. The scale of the production
revision (+269 mbu) forced futures sharply lower, but nearby
structure softened only modestly. The Mar/May spread held
near an eight-cent carry (about 40 percent of full carry),
signaling that commercial interest in nearby ownership
remains intact despite a looser balance sheet. Export
execution continues to validate that signal, with inspections

confirming strong early-season movement and cumulative shipments running well ahead of last year.
January data effectively removed the pre-report tightening narrative, yet steady export flow and firm
replacement values continue to limit downside follow-through.

Wheat – Global Supply Overhang Dominates, While US Movement Remains Orderly

March SRW wheat closed at $5.13/bu, down six cents w/w, with structure reflecting a storage-driven
market rather than nearby stress. The Mar/May spread held near a 10¢ carry (roughly 35 percent of full
carry), consistent with ample supply and limited urgency for immediate ownership. USDA’s January
WASDE lifted US ending stocks to 926 mbu and raised global ending stocks to 278.3 mmt, driven
primarily by larger crops in Argentina and Russia. Despite the heavier balance sheet, export execution
remains supportive at the margin, with weekly inspections at 0.32 mmt and cumulative shipments running
about eight percent ahead of last year. Wheat continues to trade the contrast between burdensome
global availability and a physical market that has not materially weakened.

Soybeans – Export Downgrades Shift the Burden to Domestic Crush as Brazil Advances

March soybeans settled at $10.43/bu on January 13, down 13¢ on the week, as January WASDE
reduced US exports to 1.575 bbu and raised ending stocks to 350 mbu. At the same time, domestic crush
was increased to 2.57 bbu, partially offsetting weaker export demand. Structure reflects that balance, with
the Mar/May spread holding near a 10¢ carry (about 45 percent of full carry), signaling adequate nearby
supply without forced liquidation. While weekly inspections showed episodic strength, cumulative
shipments remain roughly 40 percent behind last year, reinforcing that domestic crush, not exports, now
carries the balance sheet as Brazil’s record 178 mmt crop advances.

Macro / Policy – Dollar Slightly Firmer, BRL Weaker; Energy Quiet

Macro conditions remained largely neutral over the past week, allowing grain markets to focus squarely
on the January USDA data. The US dollar index held near 98.95, while the Brazilian real weakened
modestly toward 5.37 per dollar, supporting Brazilian farmer sales at the margin without materially altering
global trade flows. Energy markets were range-bound, with WTI crude trading near $60 per barrel,
stabilizing biofuel economics but not generating new demand signals for corn or soybean oil. With no
material policy shifts or macro shocks during the period, price discovery across grains was driven
primarily by balance sheet adjustments rather than cross-asset influences.

Weather – Warm/Dry US Pattern; Argentina Dryness vs Brazil’s Generally Favorable Setup

In the United States, a warm and generally dry pattern persisted across key growing regions. NOAA’s 6–
10 day outlook continued to show above-normal temperatures and below-normal precipitation across the
Plains and Midwest, minimizing winterkill risk for winter wheat but limiting soil moisture recharge in parts of
the HRW belt. River conditions remained stable, with no major weather-related disruptions to grain
movement.

In South America, conditions remained favorable for Brazil, reinforcing expectations for a record soybean
crop. Core growing areas continued to receive adequate rainfall, supporting crop development as harvest
advances, with Mato Grosso harvest estimated near eight percent complete. In contrast, dryness in parts
of Argentina intensified, particularly across southern production areas, raising localized concerns for
soybeans during the critical pod-setting stage of development. While wheat production is already locked in
at a record 27.5 mmt, Argentine weather remains a key variable for oilseed and corn yield risk moving
forward.

What’s Next (Jan 15 – Jan 22):

USDA Reports Reaction: Monitor basis/spreads for carryout implications; looser sheets may widen
carries unless exports accelerate.

Corn Stocks Math: Higher production/ending stocks (2.227 bbu) likely caps rallies; watch if sub-2.0 bbu
talk revives on feed/residual.

Soybean Demand Split: Recent China flashes tested, but export cut (1.575 bbu) shifts reliance to crush;
Brazil harvest pressure key

Wheat Global Flows: Argentina's record (27.5 mmt) and Russia (89.5 mmt) reinforce heavy tone; fund
shorts may extend without disruptions.

Argentina Weather: Mid-January dryness swing factor; persistent deficits could reprice soy risk,
offsetting US surplus.
