Executive Summary

Corn – Exports Now Running Ahead of What the
Balance Sheet Requires
Corn firmed into early March, with May settling near $4.49,
up about six cents on the week, as shipments continue to
run ahead of seasonal requirements. Weekly inspections
moderated to 1.86 MMT but pushed cumulative shipments
to 39.6 MMT, now 45 percent ahead of last year, while
roughly 75 percent of USDA’s 83.83 MMT export forecast is
already sold. With the four-week shipment average near
1.54 MMT versus a required 0.80 MMT pace through
August, demand is beating what the balance sheet needs at
this point in the calendar. Even so, the March–May spread

holds near a 12-cent carry, still signaling comfortable nearby coverage. CIF Gulf barge basis eased to
+85H from +92H last week, ethanol bids remain firm, and managed money stays meaningfully net short,
supportive flow underneath a curve that still pays storage.

Wheat – Front-End Flips as Funds Cover into Persistent Black Sea Pressure
Chicago SRW’s front end tightened sharply, with the March–May spread flipping into roughly a 4-cent
inversion after carrying near 10 cents last week, even as May settled near $5.80 and held only marginal
gains week over week. Weekly inspections slowed to 0.34 MMT, yet cumulative shipments remain nearly
19 percent ahead of last year, and 93 percent of USDA’s export projection is already committed. Russian
12.5 percent FOB wheat near $234/MT continues to anchor global pricing at a meaningful discount to
U.S. Gulf equivalents, limiting sustained competitiveness. Managed money moved from a net short into a
modest net long position, suggesting short covering contributed to the structural tightening. While exports
remain steady, the inversion appears more positioning-driven than fundamentally restrictive, and absent a
shift in global supply or pricing spreads, the broader balance sheet remains heavy.

Soybeans – Structure Firms Slightly as Brazil Expands Its Window
Soybeans recovered, with May settling near $11.73, up roughly 15 cents on the week, though export flow
remains seasonally subdued as Brazil accelerates shipments. Weekly inspections improved to 1.14 MMT,
but cumulative shipments at 26.18 MMT remain 30 percent below last year, consistent with Brazil’s
expanding export program, with ANEC projecting March shipments near 15.7 MMT. The March–May
spread narrowed to roughly a 10-cent carry, about 46 percent of full carry, down from 70 percent last
week, suggesting stabilization in nearby structure rather than expanding storage incentives. CIF Gulf
barge basis firmed modestly to +90H, and interior processor bids remain steady, reflecting continued
domestic pull. Managed money remains net short, and while domestic demand continues to anchor the
complex, Brazil’s seasonal pricing ceiling remains firmly in place.

Macro / Policy – Ethanol Softens Slightly as Dollar Remains a Headwind

The U.S. dollar remained firm through the week, preserving a modest competitiveness headwind across
export channels. Crude oil held relatively stable in the low-to-mid $60s per barrel range, offering limited
directional influence on biofuel margins. Ethanol production for the week ending February 27 declined to
1.095 million barrels per day from 1.113 previously, while stocks increased to 26.34 million barrels from
25.40. The combination points to steady grind activity but limited margin expansion, particularly as cash
corn premiums remain firm across parts of the Midwest. Macro flows continue to play a secondary role
relative to physical demand signals, with grain markets trading primarily on export execution and balance
sheet mechanics rather than policy catalysts.

Weather – Argentina Stress Persists as U.S. Warms

NOAA’s 6–10 day outlook continues to favor above-normal temperatures across much of the central
United States, easing winter logistics friction and improving transportation conditions along major river
corridors. Brazil’s soybean harvest has reached roughly 45 percent completion, with safrinha planting
progressing near 47 percent despite localized rainfall interruptions across parts of the north. Argentina
remains the primary weather-sensitive variable, with soybean good-to-excellent ratings near 18 percent
and corn near 26 percent. While scattered showers have provided partial relief, roughly one-quarter of the
crop remains rated poor. Official production estimates remain unchanged near 47.5 MMT for soybeans
and 55.0 MMT for corn, but yield sensitivity remains elevated as crops advance through key reproductive
stages.

What’s Next (Mar 5 – Mar 12):

Whether corn shipment pace continues to exceed required levels

Whether wheat inversion holds or relaxes back into carry

Whether soybean exports confirm demand despite Brazil’s record March lineup

Argentina rainfall coverage during grain-fill

Corn Market Deep Dive
Futures & Spreads: Corn futures advanced modestly on the week, with May settling near $4.49 versus
roughly $4.43 last Wednesday, extending the recovery that began in late February. The more important
signal remains in the curve: the March–May spread holds near a 12-cent carry, roughly 80 percent of full
carry under the two-month storage framework. Carry widened slightly from last week’s 11 cents,
reinforcing that the market continues to reward storage and signal comfortable nearby coverage rather
than front-end stress, even as the board firms. Realized volatility remains moderate near 14 percent
annualized, consistent with a market grinding higher rather than breaking into a higher-beta trend, and
price action continues to reflect execution-driven support more than a structural tightening of supplies.

Funds & Positioning: Managed money remains net short in corn near -286,685 contracts as of the
February 24 report, but the week featured meaningful short covering with a +103,702 contract
improvement from the prior week. That shift matters because it confirms the rally is no longer only
commercial-led; some speculative pressure is being relieved as execution stays strong. At the same time,
open interest in May declined roughly 9,000 contracts from Feb 25 to Mar 3, suggesting the recent move
has leaned more toward liquidation than aggressive new length. In other words, positioning remains
defensive, but the market is starting to force risk reduction. Importantly, spreads have not collapsed
despite the large remaining net short, indicating the market is absorbing speculative flow without signaling

tightening physical availability.

Cash & Basis: Cash markets cooled modestly after last week’s strength but still read as constructive. CIF
Gulf barge basis eased to roughly +85H from +92H, suggesting demand remains active but nearby offers
are less urgent. Interior river bids are still running around 20 cents under May, and ethanol plants
continue to post supportive bids to maintain nearby coverage. Basis is neither flashing scarcity nor rolling
over; movement looks orderly, with barge economics still the main swing variable capable of quickly
resetting Gulf values if river conditions tighten again.

Export Sales & Inspections: Export execution remains the anchor. Weekly inspections eased from last
week’s surge but stayed strong at 1.86 MMT for the week ending Feb 26, lifting cumulative shipments to
39.6 MMT, now 45 percent ahead of last year. Commitments stand near 62.96 MMT, equivalent to
roughly 75 percent of USDA’s 83.83 MMT export forecast already sold. Just as important, the four-week
average shipment pace near 1.54 MMT sits well above the roughly 0.80 MMT required weekly pace to
meet USDA’s projection through August. That gap suggests the balance sheet has room for additional
tightening if execution persists, even if weekly sales remain choppy.

Global Context: Global supply remains ample, but U.S. origin continues to hold competitive footing in
core channels. Black Sea offers remain steady, and Argentine FOB values are currently cheaper into
some destinations, but U.S. execution continues to be supported by dependable Western Hemisphere
pull, particularly Mexico. Brazil remains focused on soybean logistics and safrinha development, limiting
immediate corn export pressure in the near term. The global environment still caps runaway rallies, but
the U.S. program is performing well enough that competition has not yet forced a structural deterioration
in spreads or basis.

So What? Corn is firming while still trading like a carry market. The curve continues to reward storage,
but export execution is running above what the balance sheet requires, and speculative shorts are being
reduced rather than added. With May open interest drifting lower and managed money still meaningfully
net short, the market retains a defensive overlay, yet downside pressure remains contained by physical
flow and shipment momentum. Unless inspections materially cool or global competition sharpens, corn is
increasingly set up to trade as a stable carry market with tightening risk skewed toward the USDA export
line rather than toward a collapse in structure.

Soybean Market Deep Dive

Futures & Spreads: Soybean futures moved higher this week, with May settling near $11.73 compared
with roughly $11.58 last Wednesday. The more notable shift occurred in the structure of the curve: the
March–May spread tightened to roughly a 10 cent carry, equivalent to about 45 percent of full carry under
the two-month storage framework. Last week’s structure had widened toward 16 cents, so the
compression signals that the market is no longer expanding storage incentives in response to Brazilian
harvest pressure. Nearby coverage remains comfortable and the market is still clearly in contango, but
the tightening spread suggests that the board is beginning to absorb seasonal supply rather than discount
it further. Price action therefore reflects stabilization within a carry structure rather than a directional
breakout.

Products & Crush: Domestic processing remains the primary source of underlying demand support for
the soybean complex. Crushers remain well incentivized to operate at high utilization rates, though the
margin structure is increasingly concentrated on the meal side of the balance sheet rather than on oil.
Soybean oil inventories remain comfortable, limiting the contribution from the energy side of the complex,
while steady feed demand continues to support meal pricing. The result is a processing sector that is
maintaining throughput rather than accelerating it. In effect, crush demand is acting as a stabilizing force

that prevents Brazilian export pressure from translating into deeper weakness in the nearby market.

Cash & Basis: Cash markets remain stable and largely transactional rather than urgent. CIF Gulf barge
basis is holding near +90H, firmer than recent lows but still consistent with routine export loading rather
than a scramble for nearby coverage. Interior processor bids generally sit around 40 cents under May
futures, reflecting balanced crusher coverage as plants maintain solid run rates without needing to step
bids higher. With Brazil supplying the bulk of export demand during this window, basis behavior continues
to read as “steady domestic pull” instead of tightening pipeline signals.

Funds & Positioning: Speculative positioning has shifted toward a more defensive posture. Managed
Money is now net short roughly 171,000 contracts according to the February 24 CFTC report, a
significant reversal from the constructive long positioning seen earlier in the winter. The build in short
exposure reflects the market’s adjustment to Brazil’s dominant export window and the broader seasonal
increase in global supply availability. Importantly, spreads have tightened despite the heavier speculative
short exposure, suggesting that the market is absorbing positioning pressure without structural
deterioration. The current configuration indicates a market that is defensively positioned but not collapsing
under speculative weight.

Export Sales & Inspections: Export activity remains secondary while South America dominates global
shipments. Weekly U.S. soybean inspections reached approximately 1.14 MMT for the week ending
February 26, an improvement from earlier weeks but still leaving cumulative shipments near 26.18 MMT,
roughly 30 percent below last year’s pace. Total export commitments stand around 35.65 MMT, or
approximately 83 percent of USDA’s 42.87 MMT export projection. The remaining shipment requirement
is manageable, and the recent pace suggests the United States can still meet the current forecast, but the
trade rotation clearly favors Brazil during this portion of the marketing year. Export demand therefore
remains functional, though it is no longer the primary driver of price formation.

Global Context: Brazil remains the defining force in the global soybean balance sheet. CONAB’s latest
estimate places production near 178 MMT, confirming another record crop as harvest progresses across
the central producing states. ANEC projects March soybean exports near 15.7 MMT, underscoring the
scale of Brazilian logistics as export flows accelerate. FOB Santos offers remain competitively priced into
Asia relative to U.S. Gulf values, reinforcing Brazil’s dominance during the peak shipping window.
Argentina’s crop outlook remains closer to 47–48 MMT, though crop ratings remain historically weak after
earlier heat stress and keep the market sensitive to late-season weather developments. For now, Brazil
defines supply while Argentina represents the remaining weather variable.

So What? Soybeans appear to be transitioning from seasonal pressure toward stabilization. The board
moved higher on the week and the March–May spread tightened materially, signaling that the market is
beginning to absorb Brazilian harvest flow rather than expanding storage incentives. Domestic processing
continues to provide the structural demand floor, while export activity remains active but secondary during
South America’s peak shipping window. With managed money now positioned net short rather than long,
the market carries less liquidation risk but remains sensitive to shifts in global supply expectations. Unless
South American production expectations change materially or U.S. export competitiveness improves,
soybean price discovery is likely to remain anchored by steady domestic crush demand and the pace of
Brazilian export flow.

Wheat Market Deep Dive

Futures & Spreads: Chicago SRW wheat futures edged higher during the week, with May settling near
$5.80 compared with roughly $5.79 last Wednesday. The more meaningful development occurred in the
nearby structure: the March–May spread flipped from carry into a modest inversion near four cents. That
shift represents a notable change from the carry structure observed in recent weeks and indicates
tightening nearby availability rather than an expansion of storage incentives. Volatility also moved higher
relative to corn and soybeans, reflecting the market’s sensitivity to shifting trade flows and speculative
repositioning. While the flat price move itself was limited, the spread inversion signals that the front end of
the curve has become more responsive to short-term supply and logistics dynamics.

Cash & Basis: Cash markets showed a firmer tone across export corridors. CIF Gulf SRW values are
currently near +110H, indicating steady demand for nearby export slots despite limited improvement in
global competitiveness. Plains HRW basis remains defensive near 40 cents under May futures, reflecting
adequate regional coverage rather than aggressive commercial bidding. Pacific Northwest values were
largely unchanged, with export programs continuing to operate at routine levels. Overall basis behavior
suggests that the nearby inversion is being driven more by short-term positioning and logistics dynamics
than by a widespread tightening in underlying physical supply.

Export Sales & Inspections: U.S. wheat export activity slowed somewhat during the latest reporting
week. Export inspections for the week ending February 26 totaled roughly 0.34 MMT, below the prior
week’s pace but still leaving cumulative shipments near 18.62 MMT, roughly 19 percent ahead of last
year. Export commitments now stand near 93 percent of USDA’s marketing-year projection, meaning only
modest additional sales are required to reach the forecast before the May marketing-year close. While the
export program remains steady, shipment volumes alone are unlikely to materially tighten the U.S.
balance sheet without an improvement in global competitiveness.

Global Context: International wheat pricing continues to be shaped by Black Sea supply. Russian 12.5
percent FOB offers are currently near $234 per metric ton, maintaining a roughly $29 per ton discount to
comparable U.S. Gulf SRW values. That pricing advantage continues to anchor global trade flows into
traditional import markets across North Africa and the Middle East. European wheat values have firmed
modestly alongside the broader complex, but Black Sea origin remains the dominant pricing reference. As
long as Russian exports continue moving without disruption, the global wheat market remains structurally
well supplied despite short-term price fluctuations.

Funds & Positioning: Speculative positioning has shifted notably. Managed Money moved into a modest
net long position near 17,700 contracts in Chicago SRW wheat following several weeks of short covering.
The adjustment reflects the market’s sensitivity to spread tightening and volatility rather than a wholesale
shift in the global supply narrative. Even with the move into net long territory, speculative exposure
remains relatively small compared with prior cycles. The current positioning suggests that funds have
reduced downside conviction but have not yet established aggressive bullish exposure.

So What? Wheat’s tone has improved modestly, but the broader balance sheet remains heavy. The shift
of the March–May spread into inversion highlights tightening in the nearby structure and greater
sensitivity to short-term positioning and logistics flows. At the same time, export competitiveness remains
constrained by the persistent Black Sea pricing advantage, and global supply availability continues to limit
sustained upside. With speculative positioning shifting away from outright shorts and volatility elevated
relative to corn and soybeans, wheat may experience sharper short-term price swings. However, without
a meaningful disruption to global export flows or a production shock in a major origin, the market remains
fundamentally constrained by ample world supply.

US Cash Market & Basis

Corn: Corn basis held relatively firm across key export corridors even as futures edged higher during the
week. CIF Gulf barge values remained near +85H, reflecting consistent export loading and active river
movement. Interior river bids were roughly 20 cents under May futures, suggesting that elevators
continue to source grain without needing to aggressively lift bids. Ethanol plants also maintained
supportive bids as processors compete locally to secure supplies for steady grind rates. While basis has
cooled slightly from the stronger levels seen earlier in the winter, export shipments and ethanol demand
continue to provide the underlying support for the nearby cash market.

Soybeans: Soybean basis showed little directional change as global trade flows continue to rotate toward
South American origin. CIF Gulf barge bids are currently near +90H, indicating ongoing export movement
but below the stronger premiums observed earlier in the season. Interior processor bids around 40 cents
under May futures suggest crushers have sufficient nearby coverage while maintaining strong run rates.
With Brazilian exports ramping up rapidly during the harvest window, U.S. soybeans are moving into a
more secondary role in international trade, leaving the domestic cash market stable but lacking strong
upward pressure.

Wheat: Wheat cash markets leaned slightly firmer along export channels. CIF SRW basis near +110H
reflects consistent vessel demand at the Gulf despite the persistent price advantage held by Black Sea
suppliers. In the Plains, HRW basis remains near 40 cents under May futures, indicating comfortable
regional supply levels and routine commercial activity. Pacific Northwest values were largely unchanged
as export programs continue to operate at typical capacity. Basis strength at the Gulf therefore appears
linked more to shipping schedules than to tightening inventories across the broader wheat market.

So What? Cash market signals across the grain complex continue to point toward active commercial
movement rather than tightening availability. Corn basis remains comparatively supported by export flow
and ethanol demand, soybeans are steady but constrained by Brazil’s expanding export presence, and
wheat basis reflects normal export scheduling despite intense global competition. Overall, basis behavior
suggests that grain is moving through the system without difficulty, with demand channels operating
steadily but not forcing a repricing of nearby supply.

International Cash & Trade Flows

Brazil: Brazil’s soybean export pipeline is now shifting from harvest progress to shipment scale. ANEC
projects March exports near 15.7 MMT, indicating that port logistics are moving into the highest loading
period of the marketing year as freshly harvested supplies reach terminals. FOB Santos values around
$429/MT remain competitive relative to U.S. Gulf offers and continue to attract Asian demand during the
seasonal export rotation. Corn exports remain minimal for now, with ANEC projecting roughly 0.47 MMT
in March as exporters wait for the much larger safrinha crop later in the year. For the moment, Brazil’s
influence on global pricing is coming less from crop size and more from the speed at which soybeans are
entering the export pipeline.

Argentina: Argentina’s production outlook continues to carry uncertainty as crops move through late
development stages. Current estimates place soybean production near 47.5 MMT and corn near 55
MMT, though crop conditions remain uneven following earlier heat stress. Recent rainfall has improved
moisture levels in parts of the Pampas, but yield potential remains variable across regions. Export offers
are present but relatively limited compared with Brazil’s shipment volume, leaving Argentina’s role in
global markets tied more to potential supply revisions than to current trade flow.

China: Chinese import demand remains aligned with seasonal pricing incentives. Brazilian soybeans
continue to hold a cost advantage over U.S. Gulf offers, leading Chinese buyers to concentrate forward
purchases on South American origin during the harvest window. Dalian futures have traded within a
relatively narrow range, reflecting stable feed demand and steady crushing margins rather than an
expansion in consumption. Without new policy signals or large U.S. purchases, China’s import program
continues to rotate primarily through Brazil.

EU / Black Sea: Global wheat trade remains highly sensitive to price spreads between Black Sea origin
and competing exporters. Russian 12.5% FOB wheat near $234/MT maintains a sizeable discount to U.S.
Gulf SRW values around $263/MT, allowing Russia to retain a strong position in price-sensitive import
markets. Ukrainian corn around $232.50/MT also remains competitive into Mediterranean destinations.
European wheat futures have firmed modestly alongside the broader complex, but global supply
availability remains ample as long as Black Sea shipments continue moving without disruption.

Other Regions: Secondary exporters continue to supply grain through established trade routes.
Australian wheat shipments remain active into Southeast Asia following a solid harvest, while Canadian
spring wheat exports continue at a steady pace into traditional markets. No significant policy changes or
logistical disruptions have emerged across these regions, leaving the broader international trade
environment stable.

So What? The current global trade pattern reflects seasonal logistics more than structural supply shifts.
Brazil’s soybean shipments are expanding rapidly as harvest moves through the system, while Argentina
remains the key production uncertainty that could influence later-season balances. In wheat, price
spreads between Black Sea origin and other exporters continue to determine competitiveness across
import markets. For now, global grain flows remain fluid and well supplied, with pricing differences rather
than supply shortages directing trade.

Key Fundamentals & Reports (Feb 26 – Mar 04)

Export Inspections (USDA FGIS – week ending Feb 26): Inspection volume stayed active but the
leadership remains clearly corn-led. Corn inspections totaled 1.86 MMT, pushing cumulative shipments to
roughly 39.6 MMT and keeping the program about 45 percent ahead of last year’s pace. Soybean
inspections improved to 1.14 MMT, a healthier weekly print, but cumulative shipments near 26.2 MMT
remain roughly 30 percent behind last year as trade continues to rotate through Brazil. Wheat inspections
slowed to 0.34 MMT, leaving cumulative shipments around 18.6 MMT, still modestly ahead of last season
but not at a pace that materially tightens the domestic outlook. Net-net, the complex continues to trade
three different demand profiles: corn executing, soybeans functional but seasonally displaced, and wheat
dependent on price spread competitiveness.

Export Sales (USDA FAS – latest through week ending Feb 19): Forward commitments continue to
provide a useful gauge of export demand through the remainder of the marketing year. Corn export
commitments now total roughly 62.96 MMT, representing about 75 percent of USDA’s 83.83 MMT export
projection. With roughly 20.9 MMT still required over the remaining 26 weeks, the implied shipment pace
is near 0.80 MMT per week, well below the current four-week shipment average near 1.54 MMT,
suggesting corn exports remain comfortably ahead of schedule. Soybean commitments stand around
35.65 MMT, equivalent to 83 percent of USDA’s export forecast, leaving a relatively modest shipment
requirement for the balance of the marketing year. Wheat commitments have reached approximately 93
percent of USDA’s projection, meaning the export program is largely sold but dependent on steady
shipment execution through the marketing year close. Overall, forward sales remain adequate across the
complex, though the composition of demand continues to favor corn.

Ethanol Production & Stocks: Ethanol production eased slightly during the latest reporting week.
Output declined to 1.095 million barrels per day, down roughly 18,000 barrels per day from the previous
week. Ethanol inventories increased to approximately 26.34 million barrels (from 25.40 million), reflecting
steady output alongside demand that remains stable but not accelerating. Margin proxies for Midwest
plants remain positive but compressed near $0.18 per gallon, largely due to firm cash corn premiums.
The ethanol sector continues to provide consistent demand for corn, though the build in inventories
suggests the industry is operating at a steady equilibrium rather than expanding grind rates further.

South American Production Updates: South American supply remains the dominant global variable.
Brazil’s CONAB raised its soybean production estimate to 177.98 MMT, confirming another record crop
as harvest advances across the main producing regions. Corn production was revised slightly lower to
138.44 MMT, reflecting localized adjustments in early crop performance ahead of the safrinha cycle. In
Argentina, soybean production estimates remain near 47.5 MMT, while corn production is projected
around 55 MMT, though crop condition ratings remain historically weak following periods of heat stress
earlier in the season. While Brazil’s record crop continues to expand global supply availability, Argentina
remains the key weather-sensitive variable capable of introducing volatility later in the season.

NOPA Soybean Crush: January U.S. soybean crush totaled 221.56 million bushels, beating
expectations near 218.5 million and confirming that processors continue to run at high utilization rates.
Strong soybean meal demand is sustaining crush margins even as soybean oil inventories remain
comfortable, limiting the contribution from the energy side of the complex. The result is a domestic
processing sector that continues to absorb U.S. soybeans steadily, providing balance sheet support while
global export demand shifts toward Brazil during the peak harvest shipping window.

CFTC Positioning (as of February 24, released February 28): Speculative positioning shifted notably
across the grain complex. Managed Money expanded its net short in corn to roughly 286,685 contracts,
reflecting continued skepticism toward sustained rallies despite strong export execution. In soybeans,
funds also moved into a deeper net short position near 171,392 contracts, reversing earlier constructive
positioning and highlighting the market’s adjustment to Brazil’s harvest-driven export window. Wheat
positioning moved in the opposite direction, with funds shifting to a modest net long near 17,758 contracts
after several weeks of short covering. The repositioning suggests that speculative flows are becoming
increasingly differentiated across the complex rather than uniformly defensive.

So What? Fundamental signals across the grain complex are becoming increasingly uneven. Corn
continues to lead on export execution, with shipment pace comfortably exceeding the level required to
meet USDA’s forecast. Soybeans remain supported by steady inspections but continue to operate under
the shadow of Brazil’s expanding export flow. Wheat fundamentals remain comparatively neutral, with
adequate export coverage but persistent global competition. Speculative positioning is also diverging
across crops, with funds heavily short corn and soybeans while shifting toward a modest long in wheat.
The result is a market structure where demand performance supports corn, seasonal supply caps
soybeans, and wheat remains primarily influenced by global pricing dynamics rather than domestic
fundamentals.

International Reports

Brazil: Brazil is shifting from harvest progress to export logistics as the central driver of market attention.
Soybean harvest continues advancing across key producing regions, and the immediate focus has
moved to the speed at which new-crop supplies are reaching export channels. ANEC projects March
soybean exports near 15.7 MMT, indicating that port systems are entering their peak loading window.
FOB Santos values around $429/MT remain competitive into Asian destinations and continue to anchor
global soybean price discovery during the current shipping cycle. Corn shipments remain limited for now,

with ANEC projecting roughly 0.47 MMT in March as exporters await the larger safrinha crop later in the
year. While domestic pricing and currency dynamics continue to influence farmer selling, Brazil’s primary
influence on global markets at this stage lies in the scale and pace of soybean exports entering
international trade.

Argentina: Argentina continues to represent the primary source of uncertainty within the South American
supply outlook. Current estimates place soybean production near 47.5 MMT and corn near 55 MMT, but
crop conditions remain fragile after earlier heat and moisture stress. Recent rainfall provided some
localized improvement, yet yield potential varies widely across producing regions as crops move through
late developmental stages. Export flows remain modest relative to Brazil’s scale, meaning Argentina’s
influence on global markets is driven less by current shipments and more by the potential for further
production adjustments. If additional weather stress develops during the final stages of the growing
season, the country’s supply outlook could still introduce volatility into global corn and soybean balances.

China: China’s import behavior continues to align with seasonal price signals rather than aggressive
inventory accumulation. Brazilian soybeans maintain a clear pricing advantage during the harvest
window, keeping South American origin at the center of China’s procurement strategy. U.S. shipments
remain present but largely opportunistic within the current trade rotation. Domestic futures markets in
Dalian have moved within a relatively narrow range, reflecting stable feed demand and steady crushing
activity rather than a significant expansion in margins. No new policy measures or reserve interventions
were announced during the reporting period, reinforcing the view that Chinese demand remains steady
but tactical in its purchasing approach.

EU / Black Sea: Global wheat pricing continues to revolve around Black Sea export offers. Russian
12.5% protein wheat is currently quoted near $234/MT FOB, maintaining a significant discount to U.S.
Gulf values and preserving Russia’s competitiveness in price-sensitive import markets. Ukrainian grain
exports remain active through established shipping channels, and no disruptions to regional logistics have
emerged. European wheat futures have firmed modestly alongside broader commodity markets, but the
region’s export pace still trails last year’s level. As long as Black Sea supplies continue moving freely into
global trade channels, the international wheat market remains anchored by abundant availability rather
than supply constraint.

So What? Global trade is still being dictated by export price spreads and logistics timing, not by a supply
shock. Brazil’s March soybean lineup is now the dominant flow story as harvest volume reaches ports,
while Argentina remains the swing variable mainly through potential production revisions rather than
current shipment scale. In wheat, Black Sea offers continue to set the reference price into major import
markets, keeping alternative exporters reactive. Until either South American weather forces a material
estimate change or logistics bottlenecks become disruptive, international markets are likely to stay
“competitive and well supplied” rather than structurally tight.

Weather & Crop Conditions

United States
U.S. crop weather remains largely uneventful as the market moves through the late-winter transition
period. Winter wheat across the Plains continues under adequate snow cover and soil moisture
conditions, limiting near-term freeze concerns. Forecast models point to a warmer pattern across much of
the central U.S., which should help river logistics and freight movement normalize after earlier winter
disruptions. With spring fieldwork still several weeks away and crops largely dormant, weather
developments remain more relevant for transportation and monitoring potential winterkill than for
immediate production expectations.

Brazil

Brazil’s supply outlook remains historically large as harvest activity accelerates across the main producing
regions. CONAB’s latest estimate places soybean production near 177.98 MMT, confirming another
record crop as harvesting progresses through central Brazil. Total corn production is projected near
138.44 MMT, with most expected output tied to the upcoming safrinha crop cycle. With harvest now well
underway, market focus is gradually shifting from production estimates toward the pace at which newly
harvested soybeans are moving through domestic logistics and into export channels.

Argentina
Argentina’s production outlook remains stable but continues to carry elevated agronomic risk. Current
estimates place soybean output near 47.5 MMT and corn production near 55 MMT, though crop condition
ratings remain relatively weak following earlier periods of heat and dryness. Recent rainfall has provided
partial relief in some Pampas regions, yet uneven moisture distribution continues to generate yield
variability. As crops approach the final stages of development, the country remains a key weather-
sensitive component of the global corn and soybean outlook.

China
China’s domestic grain environment appears steady, with no major shifts in policy or reserve
management reported during the week. Feed demand and crushing activity remain broadly stable, while
Dalian futures have traded within a relatively narrow range. Import activity continues to favor Brazilian
soybeans during the current harvest window, reflecting both price competitiveness and seasonal supply
availability rather than a change in underlying demand.

EU / Black Sea
Weather conditions across Europe and the Black Sea region remain broadly stable as winter crops move
through dormancy. No significant winterkill concerns have been reported in Russia, Ukraine, or the
European Union, and export logistics from Black Sea ports continue to operate normally. With Russian
wheat still priced competitively in global markets, the region continues to play a central role in setting
international wheat benchmarks.

So What?
Weather conditions across major producing regions currently present few immediate supply threats.
Brazil’s harvest continues to expand global soybean availability, Argentina remains the primary source of
potential late-season weather risk, and Northern Hemisphere crops are still early enough in the seasonal
cycle that weather developments remain largely observational. For now, grain markets appear more
responsive to export flows and logistics dynamics than to weather-driven production risks.

Synthesis & Outlook

This week’s tape was driven less by the closes and more by what the forward curve signaled about near-
term comfort. Corn and soybeans pushed higher on the board while wheat was mostly unchanged, but
structure moved in three distinct directions. Corn remains in a clean carry with the March–May spread
near 12 cents, consistent with storage economics staying intact and nearby coverage remaining
comfortable. Soybeans tightened as the March–May carry compressed toward 10 cents, implying the
market is absorbing harvest pressure rather than expanding incentives to store more. Wheat was the
outlier, with the front end flipping to a modest inversion that looks more like a positioning/logistics
squeeze than a sudden change in the global supply picture.

Corn remains the demand leader of the complex. Export inspections near 1.86 MMT keep the U.S.
shipment program running well ahead of last year, and cumulative exports now sit roughly 45 percent
above the prior season’s pace. Gulf basis around +85H and steady ethanol grind reinforce the idea that
the physical pipeline continues to move grain efficiently. What makes the setup unusual is speculative
positioning: managed money remains heavily net short, holding nearly 287,000 contracts against a

market that continues to execute on the demand side. The result is a corn market where the balance
sheet remains comfortable, but the flow of grain through the system is strong enough to prevent
meaningful downside momentum.

Soybeans are balancing a steady domestic floor against a global export window that still runs through
Brazil. U.S. inspections improved week over week, but cumulative shipments remain materially behind
last year as South American origin dominates nearby coverage. At the same time, crush demand
continues to do its job underneath the board, and the tighter March–May carry suggests the market is no
longer widening storage incentives just to clear Brazilian flow. The key point is timing: ANEC’s roughly
15.7 MMT March shipment projection means the “Brazil supply wave” is shifting from expectation to
physical pipeline reality, keeping rallies hard to sustain even as domestic disappearance helps prevent a
deeper break.

Wheat remains the most structurally capped leg despite the front-end signal. The March–May inversion
reflects a tighter nearby setup, but global price leadership still sits in the Black Sea. Russian 12.5 percent
FOB near $234/MT continues to undercut U.S. Gulf values by a meaningful margin, so the export story
remains “steady but not transformative.” Inspections slowed on the week, and while funds have shifted
into a modest net long after covering, sustaining upside still requires the international spread to narrow or
a disruption that forces importers away from Black Sea origin.

Weather remains the only meaningful wild card in an otherwise well-supplied system. Brazil’s harvest is
advancing rapidly and export infrastructure is beginning to absorb the flow of newly harvested soybeans.
U.S. conditions remain quiet ahead of the spring planting season. Argentina continues to sit in the middle,
with production estimates near 47.5 MMT for soybeans and 55 MMT for corn, but crop conditions
remain fragile following earlier heat stress. The market is watching Argentina closely, yet for now it
remains a potential risk rather than a realized production shock.

Bullish triggers: Bullish catalysts would likely require confirmation that Argentine yield losses become
large enough to tighten global balance sheets, or that Brazil’s export logistics struggle to move the full
volume of its crop during peak shipment months. Continued strength in U.S. corn exports could also
tighten the domestic outlook if shipment pace remains well above the level required to meet USDA’s
forecast.

Bearish triggers: On the downside, a smooth Brazilian harvest and export campaign would reinforce
South America as the seasonal pricing ceiling for soybeans and keep U.S. exports in a secondary role.
Stabilization in Argentine weather would remove the primary risk premium currently embedded in the
market. In wheat, the persistence of cheap Black Sea supply would continue to cap rallies unless
geopolitical or logistical disruptions intervene.

In summary: Taken together, the week points to a market adjusting internally even as the broader supply
picture remains intact. Corn continues to move grain efficiently through the export channel, and that
steady flow is quietly supporting the balance sheet despite heavy speculative short exposure. Soybeans
are entering the phase where Brazil’s crop is no longer just an estimate but a logistical reality, and the
tightening of nearby spreads suggests the market is beginning to absorb that volume rather than pushing
it deeper into storage. Wheat is showing signs of short-term sensitivity through the nearby inversion, yet
global trade remains anchored by competitively priced Black Sea supply. The result is not a decisive
directional trend but a market increasingly influenced by structure, logistics, and positioning. Until a
clearer disruption emerges, whether through weather, export competition, or policy, the grain complex is
likely to remain driven by these incremental shifts rather than a single dominant catalyst.

Ethanol for America Act

The major barrier to a higher Renewable Fuel Standard (RFS) is the fuel versus food debate. Now is the
perfect time to move to E15. Corn prices are extremely low, and the latest USDA WASDE reported a record
crop and a record crop expansion.

USDA WASDE forecast corn production at 16.7 billion bushels, with the yield at 188.8 bushels per acre and
ending stocks at 2.1 billion. The season-average corn price dropped 30 cents to $3.90 a bushel. Most
importantly, 202526 crop year is expected to have 1.8 billion more bushels than 2024/25.

With current ethanol production consuming 5.6 billion bushels, a 10 percent increase in consumption would
consume an additional 560 million bushels. The reality is that year around E15 would add approximately
50 million bushels and regulatory streamlining would add another 50 million bushels. At some point over
the next ten years, the additional corn consumption could be 2.8 billion bushels minus lower fuel
consumption resulting from electric vehicles. The ethanol corn consumption increase is likely to be more
of a S curve as new compacity comes online.

With Brazil and the US corn and soybean production increasing, 100 million bushels of ethanol corn
consumption will barely move the needle. The ethanol production capacity expansion that would be three
to four years from now is when the corn consumption impact to the corn market would be felt.

US Corn Production (Billion Bushels)

Source: USDA

For example:

The National Corn Growers Association (NCGA) is intensifying its call for action from Congress
and the administration after the US Department of Agriculture’s latest World Agriculture and
Supply and Demand Estimates report projected a record 16.7-billion-bUShel corn crop this year,
further threatening the livelihood of farmers already facing record-low corn prices.

“Corn growers are already marketing their corn for extremely low corn prices, and this massive
projected corn supply without market-based solutions to increasing corn demand is already
caUSing corn prices to fall further,” said Illinois farmer and NCGA President Kenneth Hartman Jr.
“BecaUSe we need markets fast for this supply, we are redoubling and intensifying our call for
Congress to pass pending E15 legislation that will allow for year-round consumer access to higher
blends of ethanol and for the Trump administration to quickly broker deals that will open new
foreign markets for corn.”

Today’s WASDE report projects average corn yields of 188.8 bUShels per acre for 2025, for an
overall crop of 16.7 billion bUShels. If the projections prove accurate, this year’s crop will be the
largest on record by far: 1.4 billion bUShels above the current production record set in 2023, a
9.1 percent increase.

An immediate boost to demand would be the passage of legislation authorizing year-round
consumer access to 15 percent ethanol blend, or E15. This solution comes at no cost to
consumers, requires no additional infrastructure developments and would generate demand for
an additional 457 million bUShels of corn, according to NCGA estimates.

NCGA is also pUShing the administration to quickly broker additional deals with other countries
and finalize details on deals already announced. For example, India, Vietnam and Kenya are all
strategically important markets to US corn growers.

Sen. Joni Ernst, R-Iowa, on July 31 introduced the Ethanol for America Act, a bill that aims to make it easier
for Americans to fuel their vehicles with domestically produced E15. Companion legislation was introduced
the following day by Rep. Adrian Smith, R-Neb.

The legislation would eliminate unnecessary barriers to E15 by requiring the US EPA to finalize rulemaking
from 2021 to make the E15 fuel label clearer and more consumer oriented. It would also confirm the
compatibility of E15 with existing underground fuel storage and dispensing equipment.

Currently, fuel dispensers offering E15 must feature a label designed to alert customers to the appropriate
and lawful use of the fuel. The proposed rule, issued in January 2021, co-proposes to either modify the E15
label or remove the label requirement entirely. The EPA opened a 90-day public comment period on the
proposal but has failed to take final action on the rulemaking.

Following its introduction, the Senate bill, S. 2591, was referred to by the Senate Committee on
Environment and Public Works. To date, Sens. Amy Klobuchar, D-Minn.; Deb Fischer, R-Neb.; Chuck
Grassley, R-Iowa; Tammy Duckworth, D-Ill.; and Richard Durbin, D-Ill., have signed on to cosponsor the
bill. The House bill, H.R. 4864, was referred to the House Committee on Energy and Commerce. Reps.
Nikki Budzinski, D-Ill.; Dusty Johnson, R-S.D.; Tracey Mann, R-Kan.; Derek Schmidt, R-Kan.; Mike Flood,
R-Neb.; Randy Feenstra, R-Iowa; and Brad Finstad, R-Minn., are cosponsoring the House bill.

Vietnam Opening Market to Ethanol

To boost ethanol imports from the US tenfold to approximately 290 million gallons, the Vietnam Biofuel
Association has proposed that the government swiftly implement a nationwide E10 biofuel gasoline
program.

The Vietnam Biofuel Association recently submitted a document to the Domestic Market Management and
Development Department (under the Ministry of Industry and Trade), addressing several issues related to
the import tax on 99 percent ethanol used for biofuel blending, as well as the broader context of tariff
negotiations and trade relations between Vietnam and the United States.

According to the association, the import tax on 99 percent ethanol - a type specifically used in biofuel
gasoline blending - has continuously decreased from 15 percent to 12 percent, then 10 percent, and most
recently to 5 percent. These reductions fall within the broader trade policy framework between Vietnam and
the US

Petrolimex and PVOIL, Vietnam’s two largest fuel distributors and retailers, launched the country’s first
commercial trials of gasoline blended with 10 percent ethanol (E10) in three major cities last week. A total
of 42 fuel stations in Hanoi, Ho Chi Minh City and Hai Phong began selling E10 RON95 gasoline as part of
the trial that follows the Government of Vietnam’s recent announcement of a nationwide E10 mandate to
begin in January.

“The US Grains & BioProducts Council (USGBC) and its members and partners commend Petrolimex,
PVOIL and the Government of Vietnam for advancing the use of fuel ethanol in Vietnam as a means to
reduce harmful emissions, bolster fuel security and further stimulate rural economies,” said Ryan LeGrand,
USGBC President and CEO. “This month’s rollout of E10 gasoline at retail stations is a massive step
toward decarbonizing Vietnam’s transportation sector and a new potential partner for the US ethanol
industry and the American farmer.”

In 2018, Vietnam implemented an E5 mandate for its RON92 grade of gasoline, one of two gasoline grades
widely used throughout the country. The Government of Vietnam’s decision to expand and migrate the
country’s ethanol policy in 2026 to E10 and all grades of gasoline, including the more widely used RON95
grade, will help the Vietnamese fuel industry amplify the environmental and economic benefits of fuel
ethanol use which augment Vietnam’s policy objectives.

Vietnam’s gasoline consumption surpassed 2.8 billion gallons in 2024, increasing 8.1 percent from 2023
consumption levels. A nationwide E10 mandate will create a total fuel ethanol demand of 280 to 300 million
gallons per year, depending on gasoline demand.

USDA Land Value Report

The United States farm real estate value, a measurement of the value of all land and buildings on farms,
averaged $4,350 per acre for 2025, up $180 per acre (4.3 percent) from 2024. The United States cropland
value averaged $5,830 per acre, an increase of $260 per acre (4.7 percent) from the previous year. The
United States pasture value averaged $1,920 per acre, an increase of $90 per acre (4.9 percent) from 2024.

Average Farm Real Estate Value (Dollars)

Source: USDA NASS

Farm Real Estate Value by State

Like overall farm real estate values, average US cropland values rose to record levels in 2025, reaching
$5,830 per acre. This marks a 4.7 percent increase over 2024, nearly identical to the previous year’s growth
but slower than the sharper increases earlier in the decade. In dollar terms, cropland values rose by $260
per acre year over year. Regional patterns remain consistent with broader farmland trends, with California
and Northeastern states reporting the highest average cropland values. These are followed by much of the
Midwest and Florida, the latter reflecting strong specialty crop production and ongoing residential
development pressures. The top three states in percentage growth were Utah (9.7 percent), Michigan (8.2
percent) and Tennessee (7.8 percent). Notably, no states recorded a decline in cropland values.

Average Cropland Value (Dollars)

Source: USDA NASS

Cropland Value by State

US pastureland values continued their upward trend in 2025, rising to $1,920 per acre, a 5 percent increase
over 2024. This growth is nearly in line with last year’s 5.2 percent gain but remains below the 6.7 percent
increase in 2023 and the sharp 11 percent jump in 2022, which followed nearly a decade of stagnant values.

Unlike cropland and overall farm real estate, pastureland values are highest along the East Coast and mid-
South, where development pressures, land scarcity and lifestyle demand contribute to elevated prices. In
contrast, Western and Plains states report lower average values, partly due to lower land productivity and
the prevalence of federally owned grazeable land accessed through grazing permits, which reduces market-
based competition. In densely populated Eastern states, fragmentation of land and rising competition for
open space (whether for grazing, recreation or rural living) continue to drive up per-acre costs for the limited
private pastureland available.

Average Pasture Value (Dollars)

Source: USDA NASS

Pasture Value by State

Cash Rents

American Farm Bureau summarized the USDA NASS cash rent survey.

NASS also released data on cash rents, which tend to lag changes in land values and commodity
prices due to early-season lease negotiations. While the reported figures reflect past agreements,
they often serve as important benchmarks for future producer-landlord negotiations, especially in
areas with variable rent structures. Average US cropland rent increased to $161 per acre in 2025,
a modest 0.6 percent rise over 2024 , jUSt $1 per acre. Irrigated cropland rents declined by 0.4
percent to $244 per acre, while non-irrigated rents rose 0.7 percent to $147 per acre. Pastureland
cash rents remained unchanged at $15.50 per acre.

Despite minimal national movement, pressures on open land for solar development, suburban
expansion and recreation continue to support rental rate growth in select areas, particularly where
demand for working land is layered with lifestyle or energy investment interest. Increased telework
continues to pull buyers and lessees into rural areas, intensifying local competition for land in
certain high-amenity or fast-growing regions.

While earlier rent increases were tied closely to the 2021–2022 commodity price boom, today’s
changes are more uneven, shaped by regional water constraints and shifting cropping patterns.
In some areas, recent federal disaster assistance may have tempered pressure to renegotiate
downward, even as commodity prices weaken and input costs remain high.

Cropland Cash Rent Value by State (Percent Change)

Source: American Farm Bureau, USDA NASS

Washington and Montana led the country in cropland cash rent growth, up 10.7 percent and 8.2
percent, respectively, followed by Alabama at 8.1 percent. Meanwhile, cropland rents in Hawaii,
New Mexico, Wyoming, West Virginia and MassachUSetts declined by more than 5 percent, likely
due to a mix of stabilized drought conditions, changing land USe priorities and rebalancing after
years of sharp growth.

By value, the most expensive cropland rents remain in states with high-value specialty crops,
such as California ($346/acre), Arizona ($334/acre) and Hawaii ($295/acre), often driven by
contracts tied to irrigation infrastructure and water rights. Iowa, Illinois, Indiana and Washington
round out the next tier of high-rent states, reflecting both row crop concentration and specialty
crop presence. Water access and cost continue to be major drivers of both land value and rental
rates across the Western US

Margins for producers who rely heavily on rented land remain precarioUS, especially as revenue
potentially slips while fixed costs (including rents) remain elevated or inflexible. Beginning farmers
and those without land equity are especially exposed, facing steep barriers to accessing operating
credit. In a year where net farm income is forecasted to decline without ad hoc support, even
modest increases in rental rates can have outsized financial consequences.

Conclusion

Rural land values are experiencing a price bubble. In many of the Corn Belt states the cropland value
continues to increase while the cash rent decreases. The values represent the impact of government land-
based farm programs and ad hoc payment that could go to landowners. The government incentives to buy
land have diminished the link between land values and real agricultural value. The result is farmers who
rent land are increasingly in financial distress. As farmers exit the market, the disconnect between the land
prices and cash rents will increase.

What the government gives it can take away. The Democrat party can win without the farm vote. Their
messaging is trending away from “preserving family farmers” to “bashing Big Ag.” If rural land prices start
declining, the floor is real agricultural value. For example, Illinois cash rent for cropland is $264 per acre.
Farmdoc crop budget indicates that if a cash rent farmer needs $100 profit per acre to survive, the cash
rent should be approximately $133 per acre or a decrease of approximately half. Assuming a 5 percent
rate of return, the real agricultural value for cropland in Illinois is $2,660 per acre versus $9,850 per acre.

The other concern is that many of the new land buyers are investors. The investors are helping to drive
land prices higher. When land values decrease, will the investors sit tight or take a profit? The investors
do not have the same emotional attachment to the land. Farmers who want to own their own land act as a
“put” under the land market. The good news is the farmers currently have low debt levels. This means the
farmers have access to cash to buy land. The unknown is if the farmers are making a good investment or
trying to catch a falling knife.

Cropland Cash Rent Value by State

Source: American Farm Bureau, USDA NASS

Farmer Sediment

The Purdue University-CME Group Ag Economy Barometer index declined for the second month in a row.
The July index fell 11 points below June’s reading and was fueled by US farmers’ weaker perceptions
regarding both current conditions and their expectations for the future. The July Current Conditions Index
fell 17 points while the Future Expectations Index declined 7 points, both compared to a month earlier.
Weaker income prospects in 2025 were largely responsible for farmers’ weaker appraisal of current
conditions. Although all three principal farmer sentiment indices were lower in July than a month earlier,
producer sentiment remains much more positive than at this time last year, with nearly three-quarters of
July’s survey respondents reporting that the US is headed “in the right direction.” The July barometer survey
took place from July 7-11, 2025.

Concerns about weak income prospects in 2025 were evident in the July survey as the Farm Financial
Performance Index fell 14 points compared to a month earlier. This month’s decline left the index at a
reading of 90, indicating that more farmers expect weaker rather than stronger income in 2025 compared
to 2024. Weakening crop prices are eroding income prospects. For example, in the Eastern Corn Belt, bids
for fall harvest delivery of corn and soybeans were 7 percent and 3 percent lower during the July survey
week, respectively, than just 4 weeks earlier. In turn, the decline in farmers’ income prospects helped push
the Farm Capital Investment Index down 7 points in July to a reading of 53.

Farm Financial Performance Index

Source: Purdue/CME Group Ag Economy Barometer

Unsurprisingly, a weaker income outlook led to a softer outlook for farmland values in the upcoming year.
The Short-Term Farmland Value Expectations Index dipped 5 points below a month earlier. This month’s
reading of 115 also left the index 3 points lower than a year earlier and 10 points below two years ago. The
farmland index’s weakness was attributable to a small shift among respondents away from expecting values
to rise to looking for weakening values in the upcoming year, while the percentage expecting values to hold
steady rose just one point to 57 percent. With farmland leasing discussions for next year underway between
farmers and landowners, this month’s survey included a question regarding crop producers’ expectations
for farmland cash rental rates in 2026. Despite weakening crop income prospects, nearly three-fourths (73
percent) of respondents said they expect cash rental rates in 2026 to remain about the same, with just 11
percent of crop producers indicating they expect rental rates to decline.

One supporting factor for both farmland values and farmland cash rental rates could be producers’
expectations regarding the farm income safety net provided by US farm programs. In this month’s survey,
31 percent of respondents said they expect the safety net in the 2025 Farm Bill to be stronger than in the
previous Farm Bill.

US farmers in July were somewhat more optimistic about future agricultural trade prospects than a month
earlier. This month, 43 percent of respondents said they expect agricultural exports to increase in the
upcoming five years, up from 41 percent a month earlier. Fewer producers said they are looking for exports
to decline, dropping to 13 percent of respondents, down from 16 percent who felt that way in June. In a
related question, 64 percent of this month’s survey respondents said they think it likely that new foreign
export markets will open to American agricultural goods in the next five years. To further gauge US farmers’
outlook regarding trade and policy, this month’s survey included the following question: “Would you say
things in the United States today are generally headed in the right direction or on the wrong track?” Seventy-
four percent of respondents said the US is headed in the “right direction.”

US farmers’ sentiment declined in July as weaker farm income prospects dimmed producers’ view of current
conditions and their future expectations. Despite a weaker crop income outlook, just 11 percent of crop
producers said they expect farmland cash rental rates to decline, with a majority reporting that they expect
rental rates to stay about the same. Helping to support producers’ farmland value and rental rate outlook is
farmers’ expectation for a stronger farm income safety net, with nearly one-third (31 percent) of US
producers saying they expect the 2025 Farm Bill to provide a stronger safety net than the previous Farm
Bill. Producers in July were somewhat more optimistic about US agricultural trade prospects than in June,
while nearly three-fourths (74 percent) of US farmers responding to the survey reported that they feel that
things in the US are headed “in the right direction.”

Bunge Buys IFF Crush Division

As part of its second quarter results disclosure, International Flavors & Fragrances Inc. announced that it
has entered into a definitive agreement to divest its soybean crush, concentrates, and lecithin business to
Bunge Global S.A. The transaction includes operations that generated approximately $240 million in
revenue in 2024 and employs around 250 people globally. The sale aligns with IFF’s strategy to strengthen
its portfolio and supports the ongoing evaluation of strategic alternatives for its Food Ingredients segment,
with a focus on maximizing shareholder value.

Greenhouse Gas Phase 3 Standards do not Apply to Human Health

The industry has an aftertreatment system for EPA's 2007 and 2010 Heavy-Duty Highway Engines and
Vehicle emissions reduction regulations, which set standards for not only NOx but also particulate matter
(PM). It also mandated the use of advanced emission control technologies like selective catalytic reduction
(SCR) and diesel particulate filters. "Completely separate," said North American Council for Freight
Efficiency Executive Director Mike Roeth. "Aftertreatment is not a direct GHG solution. We tend to call NOx
and PM pollutant emissions, and they are directly attached to human health; breathing in these in the air...
Carbon/GHG is different in that it affects climate, encouraging our more frequent and harsher weather
events causing other health concerns."

Daimler Truck North America (DTNA) noted in a statement to Commercial Carrier Journal (CCJ) that the
proposal does not modify EPA's existing requirements for criteria pollutants, including NOx and PM. NOx
and PM standards remain in place, and they continue to be enforced independently of any changes to GHG
regulations. "Selective Catalytic Reduction (SCR) systems, Diesel Exhaust Fluid (DEF), and aftertreatment
technologies are essential components designed to reduce nitrogen oxides (NOx) regulated under the
EPA's Clean Air Act," added Paccar companies Kenworth and Peterbilt. "The current aftertreatment
systems related to NOx are still required to meet EPA and CARB standards and are independent of
greenhouse gas (GHG) specific mandates."

Covered in the Endangerment Finding is nitrous oxide (N2O). NOx is shorthand for nitric oxide (NO) and
nitrogen dioxide (NO2), the nitrogen oxides most relevant for air pollution. NOx itself is not considered a
greenhouse gas; rather it plays a role in the formation of ozone, a naturally occurring greenhouse gas.

Thus, NOx is not a greenhouse gas and the aftertreatment system, including DEF, would not be affected
by this pending regulatory change.

However, EPA Administrator Lee Zeldin said in March his office intends to revisit portions of the Biden-era
Clean Trucks Plan, which includes the 2022 Heavy-Duty NOx rule – a standard that includes new, more
stringent emissions standards that cover a wider range of heavy-duty engine operating conditions and
requires these more stringent emissions standards to be met for a longer period of time.

DEF Guidance Clarification

Starting in the early 2010s, regulations required all new on-road diesel vehicles, tractors, trucks, buses,
ships, locomotives, gas turbines and construction equipment, to install additional technology to reduce
nitrogen oxide emissions, a byproduct of diesel. Almost all commercial diesel transportation and equipment
today has Selective Catalytic Reduction (SCR) technology that runs on DEF.

According to an EPA release, "EPA issued clear guidance urging engine and equipment manufacturers to
revise DEF system software in existing vehicles and equipment to prevent these sudden shutdowns, giving
operators more time to repair faults without impacting productivity or safety."

The EPA provided a chart showing derate timing for heavy duty trucks will be extended from a 5-mph
limitation after just four hours' worth of a system fault to a three-stage derate:

 15 percent torque reduction at 650 miles / 10 hours
 30 percent torque reduction then at 4,200 miles / 80 hours
 and a final-stage derate to 25 mph at 8,400 miles / 160 hours

"In addition to providing certainty to manufacturers about how EPA wants this issue resolved, the agency
is not requiring separate approvals beyond that provided in EPA’s guidance," the release said. "This
ensures that bureaucratic steps do not delay manufacturers’ ability to put solutions into the field.”

Before the new EPA DEF guidance, a farmer had four hours before the vehicle or equipment stopped
operating, leaving the farmer stranded. EPA’s new guidance to engine manufacturers will reduce derating,
an automatic trigger forcing the engine to operate at a reduced capacity far below its capability and increase
the amount of time operators must secure DEF and refuel.

Previously, farmers and truckers faced rapid engine slowdown to 5 mph within just four hours of a sensor
alert. Under EPA’s new guidance for heavy-duty trucks, for example, after a fault is detected, only a warning
light appears for 650 miles or 10 hours. Most significantly, there is no performance impact for at least one
workday. Then, the engine only mildly derates by 15 percent, but this does not limit speed, allowing trucks
to operate normally for up to 4,200 miles or two work weeks. Only after about four work weeks does speed
drop to 25 mph until repairs are made. Most operators are expected to fix issues well before reaching this
limit. Moving forward for farmers, non-road equipment will not experience an impact for the first 36 hours.

The ruling represents a major step up from the status quo by giving operators significantly more time to
address faults before experiencing severe engine or speed limitations, which will improve their ability to
complete daily operations and reduce downtime.

The revised DEF guidance is estimated to reduce farming expenses by $727 million annually. With fewer
disruptions, reduced costs for emergency towing, repairs, rentals, DEF deliveries and inventories,
producers will have greater operational and financial control.

Truck Manufacturers Sue CARB/California

Four of America’s top truck makers have officially filed a lawsuit in the US District Court for the Eastern
District of California challenging the Clean Truck Partnership (CTP) agreement signed with the California
Air Resources Board in 2023. CARB has argued since the Congressional rollback of its Advanced Clean
Trucks and Omnibus Low-NOx rules earlier this summer that despite the federal disapproval of the
regulations, the CTP is still binding.

Cummins, Daimler Trucks North America, Hino, Isuzu, then-Navistar, Paccar, the Truck and Engine
Manufacturers Association, and Volvo Group North America in July 2023 were among 11 signers of the
CTP, an agreement with CARB that pledged truck and engine manufacturers will meet California’s vehicle
standards and required the sale and adoption of zero-emission vehicle (ZEV) technology.

The OEMs said in the lawsuit that despite the Congressional Review Acts passed this year preempting
California’s emissions standards, the state “continues to demand compliance with its heavy-duty emissions
standards, and it has threatened OEMs that design, develop, manufacture and sell heavy-duty vehicles and
engines ... with civil sanctions and unfavorable regulatory treatment if the OEMs refuse to comply with the
state’s unlawful standards, while also taking measures to insulate itself from lawful challenges to those
standards.”

Senior Clean Vehicles Advocate for the Natural Resources Defense Council Guillermo Ortiz called the
lawsuit, "a cynical reversal of course. These companies helped negotiate the Clean Truck Partnership to
secure regulatory certainty. Now they’re trying to dismantle the very deal they shaped -- injecting instability
into a market they claim to lead," he said. "This is bad faith, plain and simple, and it raises questions about
whether these manufacturers are serious about their ability to deliver clean trucks to the global stage."

The OEMs said the US Department of Justice has sent them Cease & Desist letters stating that California’s
enforcement of its regulations “is contrary to federal law” and directed the truck makers “to immediately
cease and desist compliance with California’s preempted and unlawful mandates.”

This has left the OEMs “caught in the crossfire,” they said, as “California demands that OEMs follow
preempted laws; the United States maintains such laws are illegal and orders OEMs to disregard them.
This situation is not tenable.”

As such, the lawsuit aims to clarify the OEMs’ legal obligations under federal and state law and to block
California from enforcing regulations that are preempted by federal law. Given the inconsistencies between
federal and California regulatory requirements for emissions, truck and engine makers and their trade
associations in 2023 petitioned CARB for relief from the state’s “infeasible regulatory requirements,” which
resulted in the CTP, the lawsuit added. Now, according to the OEMs, “California seeks to use the Clean
Truck Partnership, which was intended to harmonize compliance with state and federal law, to compel an
entire industry to follow California emissions standards that are now in conflict with federal law, and which
have been expressly preempted by federal law.” They added that the state is disregarding the CTP’s “plain
language,” which “states that manufacturers are obligated to follow CARB’s emissions regulations
‘regardless of the outcome of any litigation challenging … those regulations, or CARB’s overall authority to
implement those regulations,’ not federal legislative action taken to invalidate them.”

The regulatory back-and-forth between the federal government and California/CARB has put OEMs “in an
impossible position,” they said in the lawsuit. “As a result, the OEMs are subject to two sovereigns whose
regulatory requirements are irreconcilable and who are openly hostile to one another. Each wields a
hammer to enforce its will on industry, leaving OEMs -- who simply seek to sell heavy-duty trucks in
compliance with the law -- unable to plan with the necessary certainty and clarity where their products need
to be certified for sale and by which regulatory authority.”

California, citing the CTP, has contended that in order for OEMs to legally sell trucks in the state, they must
obtain certification from CARB. Yet the OEMs said this process “takes months,” adding that they “need to
know within a matter of weeks whether they must obtain a model year 2026 certification from CARB in order
to lawfully sell their products in California and other opt-in states, which together represent approximately
25 percent of the national market for new vehicle registrations for heavy-duty vehicles.”

In filing the lawsuit, the truck makers said they are looking for “declaratory and injunctive relief with the goal
of clarifying and establishing the regulatory obligations that they must follow to lawfully offer products for
sale in California and the various opt-in states, and to prevent California officials from violating their
constitutional rights,” adding that “an expeditious decision is essential.”

The OEMs’ lawsuit joins another suit brought by the Western States Trucking Association that challenged
CARB’s authority to enforce the CTP following the federal preemption of the regulations.

Ambassador Jamieson Greer: Why We Remade the Global Order

The following op-ed provides the foreign policy vision of the Trump Administration. On the 200-day mark
of the second Trump administration, United States Trade Representative Jamieson Greer published an op-
ed in The New York Times explaining how President Trump’s trade policy is remaking the international
economic order to reduce our trade deficit and deliver lasting benefits for American workers and industries.

The full text of the op-ed is below:

It mUSt be an unwritten rule that international economic orders emerge at stately hotels. In 1944,
as World War II raged, Allied representatives gathered at a picturesque New Hampshire resort
named Bretton Woods to discUSs how to establish a postwar economic order to restore the
sensible flow of trade in a fractured world.

Although the resulting Bretton Woods system ended in 1976, its legacy lives on. Our current,
nameless global order, which is dominated by the World Trade Organization and is notionally
designed to pursue economic efficiency and regulate the trade policies of its 166 member
countries, is untenable and unsUStainable. The United States has paid for the system with the
loss of indUStrial jobs and economic security, other countries have been unable to make needed
reforms, and the biggest winner has been China, with its state-owned enterprises and five-year
plans. Unsurprisingly, the past decade has seen significant international and bipartisan American
frUStration at the system’s failure to adapt to meet the essential interests of sovereign nations.

Now, reform is at hand. Last week at his Turnberry resort on the Scottish coast, President Trump
and the European Commission president, Ursula von der Leyen, concluded a historic agreement
, one that is fair, balanced and oriented toward serving concrete national interests rather than
vague aspirations of multilateral institutions. Indeed, by USing a mix of tariffs and deals for foreign
market access and investment, the United States has laid the foundation for a new global trading
order.

The previoUS system rejected tariffs as a legitimate tool of public policy, meaning that the United
States sacrificed tariff protection for critical manufacturing and other sectors. Over the past three
decades, the United States slashed barriers to our market to allow vast inflows of foreign goods,
services, labor and capital. At the same time, other countries kept their markets closed to our
goods and deployed a suite of policies , such as subsidies, wage suppression, lax labor and
environmental standards, regulatory distortions and currency manipulation , to artificially boost
exports to the United States. This approach made the United States and a handful of other
economies the consumers of last resort for countries pursuing beggar-thy-neighbor economic
policies.

Our trading partners were adept at this game, and elites on Wall Street and in Washington were
all too happy to cash in on the global arbitrage by moving production abroad. The net result? The
bulk of global manufacturing shifted to jurisdictions such as China, Vietnam and Mexico where

companies could exploit vulnerable workers or benefit from expansive state support while the
United States ran up what in absolute terms is the highest trade deficit in the history of the world.
This led to extensive and well-documented losses in US indUStrial capacity and employment as
well as reliance on our adversaries for critical supply chains.

We subordinated our country’s economic and national security imperatives to a lowest common
denominator of global consensUS. This approach harmed American workers, their families and
communities by undermining a manufacturing sector that creates high-wage jobs, fosters
innovation and catalyzes investment across the economy.

What began at Bretton Woods as a necessary effort to rebuild a global trade system shattered by
war evolved, over nine rounds of trade negotiations, into something unrecognizable. The
measured guidelines for commerce developed in the Kennedy and Tokyo Rounds gave way to
our recent experiment in global hyper-integration, embodied in the Uruguay Round, which
concluded in 1994 and established the W.T.O.

We are now witnessing the Trump Round. On April 2, President Trump announced tariffs to
address the national emergency posed by the trade deficit. The intense bilateral negotiations that
followed were held in diverse locations across the world: Washington, Geneva, Jeju Island, Paris,
London, Stockholm and, of course, Turnberry. Our trading partners had never before shown such
interest in opening their markets to the United States, aligning on matters of economic and
national security, and rebalancing trade in a more sUStainable direction. In a few short months,
the United States secured more foreign market access than it had in years of fruitless W.T.O
negotiations.

Reversing decades of harmful policy that weakened our manufacturing capacity and work force
will take time and coordinated effort across the public and private sectors. But keeping the statUS
quo would only accelerate the dangeroUS trajectory of deindUStrialization. We require a
generational project to re-indUStrialize America, and time is short.

When I joined a critical mass of my fellow trade ministers in June at a meeting of the Organization
for Economic Cooperation and Development in Paris, I was struck by how many voiced serioUS
concerns about the danger of macroeconomic imbalances, the threat of nonmarket practices and
the sclerotic state of the global trading system , the same issues President Trump has raised for
years and now taken emergency action to address. What was long dismissed as heresy by the
free-trade fundamentalists in BrUSsels, Geneva and Washington is now becoming conventional
wisdom.

In announcing the US-European Union deal last week, President von der Leyen echoed the call
to refashion global trade to adapt to economic and political realities. She explained to reporters
