that the trans-Atlantic economic relationship needed “rebalancing” to ensure that it could be “more
sUStainable.” Such recognition is reinforced by additional deals with Britain, Cambodia,
Indonesia, Japan, Malaysia, Pakistan, the Philippines, South Korea, Thailand and Vietnam, which
make up almost 40 percent of US trade, according to my office’s figures. Other countries with
large trade surplUSes with the United States are subject to generally higher tariffs. The new
economic order, solidified at Turnberry, is emerging in real time.

The results are astounding. Every year for 40 years, the Office of the US Trade Representative
has produced a detailed report called the National Trade Estimate chronicling varioUS barriers
faced by US companies, including high tariffs, requirements to produce goods in the countries
where the companies want to do bUSiness, and restrictions on agricultural products contrary to
scientific consensUS. In the past, the only significant way the United States could remove these
barriers (if at all) was by giving away the tariffs defending our manufacturing sector. President
Trump has flipped the script on this: Now, we are systematically eliminating these barriers abroad
while ensuring sufficient tariff protection at home.

Indonesia is lowering 99.3 percent of its tariffs on imports from the United States and eliminating
a slew of longstanding non-tariff barriers, while accepting a 19 percent tariff on exports to the
United States. South Korea is accepting US auto standards along with a 15 percent tariff. Vietnam

pledged to lower all of its tariffs and barriers in exchange for a 20 percent rate. Most countries we
are negotiating with have also agreed to cooperate on economic security to ensure the safety and
reliability of our critical supply chains.

Countries are also committing to upgrade and better enforce their labor standards, tackling
arbitrages that have put American workers and producers at a disadvantage. Multiple countries
will join the United States (along with the E.U., Mexico and Canada) in banning the importation of
goods made with forced labor. Eliminating global slavery was a longstanding goal of advocates
and policymakers, but it was the leverage of President Trump’s tariffs that finally made it possible
to unlock meaningful progress.

improve resource efficiency and enforcement of
Similarly, countries are agreeing
environmental laws, including in the most problematic sectors, such as illegal logging, illegal
fishing and the illegal wildlife trade. The international trade system should not force Americans to
compete with those who USe our responsible capitalism against US as a competitive advantage.

to

Importantly, these commitments are actionable, and the United States will enforce them. Rather
than the drawn-out dispute settlement process favored by the old guard of trade bureaucrats, the
new US approach is to closely monitor implementation of the deals and swiftly reimpose a higher
tariff rate for noncompliance if needed. President Trump uniquely recognizes that the privilege of
selling into the world’s most lucrative consumer market is a mighty carrot. And a tariff is a
formidable stick.

At the World Trade Organization, enacting changes to trade rules requires total consensUS
among nations. In fact, the last attempt at serioUS reform, known as the Doha Round, failed
becaUSe protectionist nations refUSed to take down their trade barriers to the United States.
Moreover, our adversaries relish blocking reform. They prefer a statUS quo that feeds an
exploding US trade deficit, sapping this nation of the indUStrial might that made it, and keeps it,
a superpower.

But the rules of international trade cannot be a suicide pact. By imposing tariffs to rebalance the
trade deficit and negotiating significant reforms that form the basis of a new international system,
the United States has shown bold leadership to address what policymakers long considered
intractable problems.

Many of these deals also come with significant investment commitments into US productive
capacity, such as $600 billion in the case of the European Union and $350 billion by South Korea.
These investments , 10 times larger than the inflation-adjUSted value of the Marshall Plan that
rebuilt Europe after World War II , will accelerate US reindUStrialization. South Korea will help
reinvigorate the American shipbuilding indUStry, which has atrophied in the face of nonmarket
competition. These types of investments are on top of purchase commitments that cumulatively
account for almost $1 trillion of American energy, agriculture, defense and indUStrial products.
This demand for American goods and ready access to capital will allow American manufacturing
to reassert leadership across strategic sectors where we have fallen behind.

Skeptics point out that tariffs, although once a fixture of American economic policy, have not been
USed this extensively for generations. But we now have data showing that the failure to USe
tariffs or similar protections created an economy that was heavy on finance and consulting fees
and low on the lasting wealth and security that come from making things. Even among those who
agree with that diagnosis, some say the president’s remedy is too strong or was administered too
hastily, or that the tariffs will be too disruptive in the short term. This is not the time to debate how
many angels can dance on the head of a pin. This is an emergency. We know what the problem
is and we know how to address it. There is no time to waste.

President Trump has already shown that he can implement tariffs and other economic tools to
reshape supply chains and reinvigorate manufacturing. When he deployed sweeping tariffs in his
first term, not only did the sky not fall as pundits predicted, but inflation actually went down. And
now that he is imposing tariffs even more broadly, inflation remains in check. A long-term problem

won’t be fixed overnight, and the process may not always be smooth, but the situation demands
strong and resolute action to strengthen the US indUStrial base.

It took over 50 years from that first meeting at Bretton Woods until the creation of the W.T.O. It
has been 30 years since. Fewer than 130 days from the beginning of the Trump Round, the
Turnberry system is by no means complete, but its construction is well underway.

Stagflation Discussion

Stagflation talk has taken center stage. It should be remembered that stagflation in the 1970s was driven
by crude oil prices increasing from $3.56 per barrel in August 1973 to $10.11 per barrel in January 1974 to
$14.85 per barrel in January 1979 to $39.50 per barrel in May 1980. In less than seven years crude oil
price increased by over 1,100 percent. By comparison, the current crude oil price is approximately half the
May 2022 price. With almost every country expanding energy production, lower energy prices could occur.

According to the prolonged price decrease in crude oil and natural gas, inflation numbers will be below
macro-economic models. This is because energy is incredibly important as it impacts inflation numbers
directly, such as fuel and as a feedstock in petrochemicals, and indirectly, such as an increased cost that
a manufacturer and business must recoup. Energy costs impact input prices for production, and every
other stage required to reach the consumer with each cost increase being pushed to the next level. The
cumulative impact of higher energy prices on the consumer tends to be understated by macro-economic
models.

One inflationary outcome from lower energy prices is discretionary income increasing, which allows for
more spending. The other outcome is the producer price index will show a decline with retail sales prices
staying firm and retailers performing better than if it was still a higher energy cost environment. Any
economic downturn will force retailers to lower prices to move products.

The inflationary impact of the massive increase in the money supply has worked its way through the
economy, resulting in a natural slowdown. It is difficult to have runaway inflation with lower energy prices,
and a weak housing market.

During the last trade war, the rate of inflation decreased. It is not a forgone conclusion that the rate of
inflation is going to increase. The consumer price index increased a seasonally adjusted 0.2 percent for
the month and 2.7 percent on a 12-month basis, the Bureau of Labor Statistics reported. That compared
with the respective Dow Jones estimates for 0.2 percent and 2.8 percent.

Excluding food and energy, the core CPI increased 0.3 percent for the month and 3.1 percent from a year
ago, compared with the forecasts for 0.3 percent and 3 percent. Federal Reserve officials generally consider
core inflation to be a better reading for longer-term trends. The monthly core rate was the biggest increase
since January while the annual rate was the highest since February.

Tariff-sensitive new vehicle prices also were unchanged. For now, automakers are choosing to keep
market share.

A 0.2 percent increase in shelter costs drove much of the rise in the index, while food prices were flat and
energy fell 1.1 percent, the BLS said. US housing market now 508,715 more home sellers than
homebuyers. With the current housing market experiencing pain, it is easier to envision lower prices not
higher prices.

Mid-America Manufacturing Slumps for July

After six straight months of above growth neutral readings, the Creighton University Mid-America Business
Conditions Index, a leading economic indicator for the nine-state region stretching from Minnesota to
Arkansas, sank below 50.0 growth neutral threshold for July.

Overall Index

The Business Conditions Index, which uses the identical methodology as the national Institute for Supply
Management (ISM) and ranges between 0 and 100 with 50.0 representing growth neutral, declined to 49.4
for July from 50.7 in June.

“Creighton’s latest survey continues to reflect job losses across the region, accompanied by elevated
wholesale inflation,” said Ernie Goss, PhD, Director of Creighton University’s Economic Forecasting Group
and the Jack A. MacAllister Chair in Regional Economics in the Heider College of Business. “Supply
managers expressed growing concern that rising tariffs could further accelerate inflationary pressures.
Despite these concerns, only 6.7 percent of surveyed supply managers reported shifting purchases from
international to domestic suppliers in response to the tariffs.”

Employment

The July employment index fell to 45.4 from 49.4 in June, marking the fourth consecutive month below the
growth-neutral threshold of 50. This sustained weakness reflects ongoing labor market challenges in the
regional manufacturing sector.

“The Creighton survey has now recorded six straight months of job losses across the nine-state Mid-
America region,” said Goss. “While employment rose in the first quarter, driven by increased production
ahead of anticipated tariff impacts, the last four months have signaled a return to regional manufacturing
job declines,” said Goss.

US Bureau of Labor Statistics data show that the region shed 12,900 (-0.9 percent) manufacturing jobs
over the past 12 months. The US lost 89,000 (-0.7 percent) manufacturing jobs over the same time period.

Comments from Supply Managers in July:













“Most of the suppliers I work with are dropping their production down to four-day work weeks.
Nothing like a 20 percent cut in pay.”
“I believe that the President's tariff changes are going to help our country to be better trading
partners. It will take a bit of time for it all to shake out though.”
“No changes to international purchases until a final tariff rate is in place. Certain products have
been put on hold.”
“We are not accepting pricing increases and have any tariffs added as a surcharge, so they are
easy to remove.”
“Long-term contracts have been key to holding pricing steady. Where there are extreme cost
pressures with suppliers, collaboration on a resolution is the method of choice, while rare.”
“While the Trump administration is doing everything possible to bring the economy back to its
former glory, the democrats continue their seditious acts to block any attempts at dismantling their
previous criminal maneuvers to destroy this great country! A reckoning is coming.”

Wholesale Prices

The July price gauge climbed to 69.7 from 67.9 in June. “The regional inflation yardstick has moved into a
range indicating that inflationary pressures are moving higher at the wholesale level. However, due to a

slowing regional and US economies, I expect the Federal Reserve to cut interest rates at its September 16-
17 meetings,” said Goss.

On average, supply managers expect tariffs to push input prices up by 7.8 percent over the next 12 months.
This is up from 7.5 percent recorded in June.

Confidence

Looking ahead at the next six months, economic optimism, as captured by the July Business Confidence
Index, dropped to 42.6 from June’s 50.0. “Concerns regarding tariffs and slowing new orders pushed supply
managers’ expectations lower. Only one in six supply managers expect rising economic conditions for their
firm over the next six months,” said Goss.

Inventories

The regional inventory index, reflecting levels of raw materials and supplies, increased to 49.1 in July from
June’s 46.9. “Rapid expansions in inventories in the first quarter are now being offset by monthly pullbacks
in buildups,” said Goss.

Trade

Recent uncertainty regarding tariffs and trade restrictions pushed new export orders lower for the last four
months. New export orders slumped to 38.3 from 43.4 in June. As a result of record imports for the first two
months of 2025, supply managers pulled back on purchasing from abroad in the last five months. The July
import index increased to a still weak reading of 36.3 from 30.0 in June.

Supply managers were asked about their firm’s reactions to tariffs and threats of tariffs. Only 6.7 percent of
manufacturing supply managers reported switching from international suppliers to domestic suppliers. More
than one-third, or 67.0 percent, reported making no changes of suppliers due to tariffs. The remaining 26.3
percent indicated that their firms had switched from one international supplier to another international input
supplier.

According to US International Trade Administration (ITA) data, the regional economy exported $38.9 billion
in manufactured goods for the first five months of 2025, compared to $40.8 billion for the same period in
2024, for a 4.7 percent decline. In terms of export gainers, North Dakota registered the top percentage gain
with a 42.6 percent addition, and South Dakota recorded the largest percentage loss with a 19.9 percent
reduction in the export of manufactured goods.

Other Survey Components of the July Business Conditions Inde

New orders dropped to 45.5 from 52.4 in June; the production or sales index increased slightly to 49.0 from
48.5 in June; and the speed of deliveries of raw materials and supplies rose to 57.8 from June’s 56.5.
Higher readings indicate slower deliveries and/or rising supply chain disruptions or delays.

Arkansas

The state’s July Business Conditions Index fell to 43.0 from 48.2 in June. Components from the July survey
of supply managers were new orders at 43.7, production or sales at 47.0; delivery lead time at 53.1;
inventories at 39.5; and employment at 31.8. According to ITA data, the Arkansas manufacturing sector
exported $2.3 billion in goods for the first five months of 2025, compared to $2.4 billion for the same period
in 2024, for a 5.6 percent decline.

Iowa

The state’s Business Conditions Index for July declined to 43.7 from 48.0 in June. Components of the
overall July index were new orders at 43.9; production or sales at 47.3; delivery lead time at 53.6;
employment at 33.2; and inventories at 40.6. According to ITA data, the Iowa manufacturing sector exported
$6.2 billion in goods for the first five months of 2025, compared to $6.9 billion for the same period in 2024,
for a 10.3 percent decline.

Kansas

The Kansas Business Conditions Index for July increased to 51.1 from 49.9 in June. Components of the
leading economic indicators from the monthly survey of supply managers for July were new orders at 45.5;
production or sales at 50.3; delivery lead time at 58.6; employment at 47.7; and inventories at 58.6.
According to ITA data, the Kansas manufacturing sector exported $5.0 billion in goods for the first five
months of 2025, compared to $5.2 billion for the same period in 2024, for a 3.5 percent decline.

Minnesota

The July Business Conditions Index for Minnesota dropped to 49.3 from 52.8 in June. Components of the
overall July index were new orders at 45.1; production or sales at 49.8; delivery lead time at 57.5;
inventories at 50.2; and employment at 44.3. According to ITA data, the Minnesota manufacturing sector
exported $9.5 billion in goods for the first five months of 2025, compared to $10.7 billion for the same period
in 2024, for an 11.4 percent decline.

Missouri

The state’s July Business Conditions Index rose to 51.8 from 50.8 in June. Components of the overall index
from the survey of supply managers for July were new orders at 45.6; production or sales at 50.6; delivery
lead time at 59.1; inventories at 54.4; and employment at 49.2. According to ITA data, the Missouri
manufacturing sector exported $7.0 billion in goods for the first five months of 2025, compared to $7.1
billion for the same period in 2024, for a 0.5 percent decline.

Nebraska

The state’s July Business Conditions Index climbed to 51.6 from June’s 51.3. Components of the index
from the monthly survey of supply managers for July were new orders at 45.5; production or sales at 50.4;
delivery lead time at 58.8; inventories at 53.6; and employment at 48.3. According to ITA data, the Nebraska
manufacturing sector exported $2.7 billion in goods for the first five months of 2025, compared to $3.1
billion for the same period in 2024, for a 10.8 percent decline.

North Dakota

The state’s Business Conditions Index advanced above growth neutral for a 13th consecutive month to
52.2 from 52.0 in June. Components of the overall index for July were new orders at 45.7; production or
sales at 50.8; delivery lead time at 59.4; employment at 50.0; and inventories at 55.0. According to ITA
data, the North Dakota manufacturing sector exported $2.5 billion in goods for the first five months of 2025,
compared to $1.8 billion for the same period in 2024, for a 42.6 percent gain.

Oklahoma

The state’s Business Conditions Index for July declined to 49.0 from 50.6 in June. Components of the
overall July index were new orders at 45.0; production or sales at 49.5; delivery lead time at 57.2;
inventories at 49.7; and employment at 43.7. According to ITA data, the Oklahoma manufacturing sector

exported $2.9 billion in goods for the first five months of 2025, compared to $2.6 billion for the same period
in 2024, for a 3.5 percent gain.

South Dakota

The July Business Conditions Index for South Dakota slumped to 44.5 from 48.5 in June. Components of
the overall July index were new orders at 44.1; production or sales at 47.6; delivery lead time at 54.1;
inventories at 42.1; and employment at 34.8. According to ITA data, the South Dakota manufacturing sector
exported $0.7 billion in goods for the first five months of 2025, compared to $0.9 billion for the same period
in 2024, for a 19.9 percent decline.

Fundamental Recommendation: Soybean oil futures remain near the upper end of modeled value for the
balance of 2026, with the forecast projecting a gradual decline from the mid-50s toward the mid-40s into
year-end. Current pricing continues to reflect biofuel-driven premium and cross-commodity support from
the broader soy complex rather than tightening oil fundamentals. Rallies into the upper-50s favor
disciplined hedge initiation or risk-defined short exposure, while outright buying should remain tactical and
limited to weather-driven volatility rather than structural strength.

Fundamental Bullish Factor – Renewable diesel and biodiesel demand continue to provide baseline
structural support, though margin volatility has prevented sustained upside
follow-through.

Fundamental Bullish Factor – Weather-sensitive palm oil production remains a watchpoint; any supply
disruptions could tighten the global veg oil balance and lift soybean oil
prices.

Fundamental Bearish Factor – A confirmed 176 mmt Brazilian soybean crop materially expands global
vegetable oil supply in 2025/26, reinforcing competitive pressure on US
soybean oil pricing.

Fundamental Bearish Factor – Depressed crude oil prices weigh on biofuel margins and typically
correspond with seasonally weaker biodiesel output, capping near-term
price potential.

Weekly Insights | Jan 29, 2026

Item

 Stories of the Week
 Corn
 Wheat
 Soybeans
 Soybean Meal
 Soybean Oil
 Weekly Prices

Executive Summary

Corn – Front End Holds as Exports Stay Strong
Corn traded slightly firmer through the week, with March
futures edging up to $4.28, while the March–May spread
held near a seven-cent carry, still short of full carry. Export
inspections totaled 1.12 million metric tons, bringing
cumulative shipments to around 33.7 million metric tons,
roughly 48 percent ahead of last year, reinforcing continued
strength in execution. CIF Gulf basis remained firm in a +98
to +105H range, and interior river bids held steady despite
improving winter logistics. Low water levels on the
Mississippi continued to inflate freight costs and complicate
movement, keeping nearby values supported. Managed

money further trimmed its net short to approximately 72,000 contracts (as of the January 27 CFTC
report), the smallest short position since mid-2025. While flat price lacks clear momentum, cash strength
and ongoing export demand continue to limit downside risk.

Wheat – Export Flow Steady as Funds Stay Heavily Short
Wheat futures firmed modestly, with March Chicago SRW settling near $5.32, up about four cents on the
week, while the March–May spread tightened slightly to roughly nine cents under carry. USDA reported
weekly export inspections of approximately 0.34 million metric tons, lifting cumulative shipments to around
16.5 million metric tons, or 22 percent ahead of last year. Gulf basis remained broadly steady in a +65 to
+115H range, and interior bids showed little movement, indicating adequate short-term coverage.
Managed money still holds a sizable net short near 94,700 contracts, and with global supply ample, rallies
remain vulnerable to producer selling and speculative pressure absent a clearer overseas supply
disruption.

Soybeans – Weather-Driven Rally Tests Resistance Amid Brazil Competition
Soybeans rallied sharply during the week, with March futures jumping to $10.92, a roughly 26–28 cent
gain from the previous week's close, fueled by intensifying crop stress in Argentina, record U.S. crush
demand, and speculative interest. The March–May spread held near minus 12 cents, still reflecting
nearby coverage but with stronger board pricing. Export inspections improved to 1.31 million metric tons,
yet cumulative shipments remain about 35 percent below last year as Brazil’s January exports are now
estimated near 3.23 million metric tons, extending its early-season dominance despite the downward
revision. Still, U.S. cash markets held firm, with CIF Gulf basis steady at +116 to +120H and interior
processor bids supported by strong crush demand. Managed money expanded its net long to
approximately 17,300 contracts, signaling a shift toward a more bullish stance. While South American
weather risks are lifting futures, abundant supply from Brazil continues to cap sustained upside unless
export flows shift decisively.

solely at the liability of and responsibility of the user.

interval.)

Macro / Policy – Freight Constraints Ease, Biofuel and Trade News Surface

Macro drivers remained secondary to logistics again this week. Ethanol production slipped to roughly 0.96
million barrels per day (week ending January 30), while stocks declined to about 25.14 million barrels,
and blending demand improved modestly to around 890,000 barrels per day, reinforcing stable corn grind
even with a softer weekly print. While energy markets were neutral, market attention shifted to U.S.
Treasury’s proposed biofuel tax credit (45Z), which boosted soyoil and supported soybean futures on
improved renewable diesel margins. On trade, President Trump’s comments around potential trade
discussions kept optimism circulating for agricultural purchases, though no official volumes or
commitments have been confirmed. Freight conditions showed modest improvement as ice cleared along
parts of the Mississippi, but low water levels still limited draft and tow sizes, keeping barge execution
uneven and supporting nearby basis.

Weather – Plains Freeze, South America Dries

In the U.S., the late-January cold snap impacted transportation more than crops. Snow cover across the
Plains remained adequate, minimizing winterkill risk, and no new winter wheat condition updates were
issued. In South America, central and northern Brazil continued receiving favorable rainfall, aiding
soybean development but slowing harvest progress. In the south, conditions have turned more variable,
with recent excess moisture in Paraná and Rio Grande do Sul delaying fieldwork and raising disease
pressure, even as pockets remain uneven in coverage. In Argentina, rainfall remained patchy and
insufficient across major growing areas, leading to further deterioration in crop ratings. With key
reproductive stages underway, February rainfall remains critical, and traders are increasingly weather-
sensitive as Argentine supply risk intensifies.

What’s Next (Feb 05 – Feb 12):

USDA export inspections and whether corn shipment pace remains well ahead of last year

Export sales data for confirmation of soybean demand amid rising Brazilian exports

Argentina rainfall outcomes following continued crop condition deterioration

EIA ethanol production and stocks for confirmation of steady biofuel demand

Treasury 45Z follow-through: Market response to U.S. biofuel credit proposal

Corn Market Deep Dive

Futures & Spreads: Corn remained rangebound through the week, with March futures settling near
$4.28, up slightly from the prior week, as trade continued to focus on structure rather than outright
direction. The March–May spread held around minus seven cents, still comfortably in carry but short of
full. On a full-carry framework, that spread is roughly 50 percent of full carry for the Mar–May window. The
curve continues to reflect steady nearby demand without signaling broader balance-sheet stress. Price
action stayed contained and volatility muted, reinforcing the sense that the market is waiting on a clearer
catalyst before committing to a directional move

Funds & Positioning: Speculative positioning continued to ease rather than intensify. Managed Money
trimmed the net short to approximately 72,000 contracts as of January 27, marking a further reduction
from recent highs. While the position remains sizable, the pace of short accumulation has clearly slowed.
With futures unable to extend losses and spreads holding their ground, funds appear increasingly
reluctant to press the downside absent a shift in physical flow or a macro trigger.

Cash & Basis: Cash markets remained firm despite weather-related disruption earlier in the period. CIF
Gulf basis held within a +98 to +105H range, while interior river bids were largely steady, signaling
consistent demand at the destination level. Freight conditions remained the binding constraint, as ice

damage and low-water levels on the Mississippi imposed draft and tow-size restrictions, keeping barge
movement uneven. Basis strength under these conditions continues to reflect execution and logistics
rather than speculative tightening.

Export Sales & Inspections: Export inspections reached 1.12 million metric tons for the week ending
February 1, bringing cumulative shipments to approximately 33.7 million metric tons, or roughly 48
percent ahead of last year. While weekly volumes moderated from the previous peak, the broader
shipment pace remains solid and aligned with USDA’s export expectations. Export sales for the same
reporting week totaled 1.62 million metric tons, with shipments of 1.65 million metric tons, reinforcing the
view that physical flow remains intact even as futures remain subdued.

Global Context: Brazil’s January corn export outlook remains near 3.45 million metric tons, according to
ANEC, reflecting continued late-season shipment strength. In Argentina, crop conditions remain under
pressure due to uneven rainfall, though export offers have yet to adjust meaningfully. Black Sea
availability remains seasonally light, limiting immediate competition into key destinations. U.S. origin
continues to maintain an advantage into Mexico, where execution has remained strong, even as Brazil’s
forward program tempers broader price sensitivity.

So What? The board remains subdued, but the underlying market continues to signal firmness. Spreads
are holding, basis remains supported, and execution is steady. While speculative positioning is still short,
funds are no longer adding aggressively. With logistics acting as a near-term constraint and South
American harvest still developing, the front end of the corn market remains supported, even as flat price
struggles to generate momentum.

Soybean Market Deep Dive

Futures & Spreads: Soybeans staged a modest rally during the week, with March futures settling near
$10.92, up roughly 26–28 cents on the period, as Argentine weather risks intensified and speculative
buying returned. The March–May spread held steady near a 12¼-cent carry, maintaining a contango
structure that continues to reflect adequate nearby supply rather than a breakdown in demand. While the
curve still signals that domestic pull is present but not tightening enough to reprice structure meaningfully,
flat price action gained traction amid rising weather concerns and supportive macro developments.
Trading ranges expanded slightly, reflecting a more directional tone.

Products & Crush: Crush fundamentals remain the stabilizing force within the complex. While no new
NOPA report was released during the window, the market continues to operate against a backdrop of
historically strong crush rates and resilient meal demand. Soybean oil fundamentals remain less
supportive, with ample stocks and policy-related uncertainty continuing to limit upside. The crush margin
remains meal-driven, reinforcing USDA’s emphasis on domestic use as the primary demand pillar for
soybeans in the current balance sheet.

Cash & Basis: Cash markets stayed firm despite softer futures. CIF Gulf basis held in a +116 to +120H
range, signaling that nearby export demand has not fully deteriorated even as Brazil accelerates
shipments. Interior processor bids remained supported across the Midwest, reflecting steady crush
demand and limited farmer selling. River logistics remained uneven due to lingering ice damage and low-
water constraints, reinforcing the firmness in basis despite the lack of price follow-through on the board.

Funds & Positioning: Speculative positioning tilted more bullish. Managed Money expanded its net long
to roughly 17,300 contracts as of January 27, reflecting increased optimism tied to domestic crush
demand and Argentine production risk. Gross exposure remains lighter than prior months, suggesting that
funds are positioning tactically. While not overly aggressive, the profile now leans constructively rather

than neutral.

Export Sales & Inspections: Export inspections improved to 1.31 million metric tons for the week ending
February 1, offering a modest pickup in physical execution. However, cumulative shipments remain
approximately 35 percent below last year, underscoring the ongoing impact of Brazil’s dominant early-
season export program. Export sales for the same week totaled 825,000 metric tons, with shipments of
1.3 million metric tons, signaling decent front-loaded movement but still lagging historical norms. The
export program remains functional but lacks the competitiveness to reshape the broader demand outlook.

Global Context: Brazil’s soybean export pace remained dominant, though ANEC’s January estimate has
been revised down to roughly 3.23 million metric tons following harvest delays. While harvest progress
remains uneven due to persistent rainfall in central and northern regions, logistics have not become
restrictive enough to slow export flow meaningfully. In Argentina, crop conditions deteriorated further amid
uneven rainfall, keeping weather risk elevated and more closely watched by the market. FOB values from
Brazil remain competitive into Asia, maintaining pressure on U.S. origin.

So What? Soybeans remain caught between firm domestic demand and expanding South American
supply. The board reflects caution, spreads remain comfortably in carry, and exports continue to lag.
Cash markets, particularly processors, are still anchoring the front end, while funds have shifted toward a
more balanced stance. Until either Brazil’s export program stumbles or US demand reaccelerates,
soybeans are likely to remain rangebound, with strength expressed more clearly in basis than in flat price.

Wheat Market Deep Dive

Futures & Spreads: Wheat futures posted modest gains during the week, with March Chicago SRW
settling near $5.32, up roughly four cents. The March–May spread tightened slightly to near an 8¾-cent
carry, maintaining a storage-led structure without signaling urgency from end users. Price action
responded briefly to weather-related headlines in the Black Sea but remained largely contained, reflecting
a market still anchored by ample global supply. Kansas City and Minneapolis contracts were
comparatively steady, lending limited support to the complex but failing to alter the broader tone.

Cash & Basis: Cash markets were largely unchanged. FOB Gulf SRW offers held across a wide range,
roughly +65 to +115H, while interior markets showed little movement, indicating adequate nearby
coverage. HRW basis remained steady across the Plains, with no meaningful tightening despite episodic
winter weather concerns. Pacific Northwest values also held flat. Overall, basis stability continues to
underscore a lack of urgency from export buyers, with U.S. wheat still struggling to regain
competitiveness.

Export Sales & Inspections: Weekly export inspections for the period ending February 1 totaled
approximately 0.34 million metric tons, pushing cumulative shipments to around 16.5 million metric tons,
or about 22 percent ahead of last year’s pace. While the weekly volume was routine, year-to-date
performance remains consistent with USDA projections. Export sales for the same reporting week totaled
560,000 metric tons, with shipments of 380,000 metric tons, steady but not dynamic. The export program
continues to function, but without momentum to reshape sentiment meaningfully.

Global Context: Global supply continues to dominate pricing. Russian wheat remains available at
competitive levels, maintaining pressure across major importing regions. Ukrainian shipments through
Danube channels continued without disruption, while European values remained elevated and largely
sidelined from price-sensitive tenders. Argentine wheat offers were steady, adding another layer of low-

cost supply into global channels. With no logistical or policy constraints emerging across major origins,
global availability continues to cap upside potential.

Funds & Positioning: Speculative positioning remains heavily skewed to the short side. Managed
money held a net short near 94,700 contracts as of January 27, still among the largest bearish bets
across the grain complex. While the pace of new short accumulation has slowed, there has been no
significant covering either. Without a clear catalyst, be it weather, freight, or geopolitics, funds appear
comfortable maintaining a defensive stance.

So What? Wheat remains defined by imbalance rather than momentum. Spreads remain in carry, basis is
flat, and global supply continues to dictate trade flows. Despite sizable speculative shorts, the absence of
a structural disruption has limited both downside acceleration and upside opportunity. Until a material shift
emerges, wheat is likely to remain rangebound, with risk skewed toward patience rather than price
discovery.

US Cash Market & Basis

Corn: Corn basis remained firm across key corridors, supported by steady demand and lingering
transportation constraints. CIF Gulf basis held within a +98 to +105H range for nearby positions, with
February values little changed, reflecting continued downstream pull despite uneven execution. Interior
river markets were largely steady, as draft and tow-size restrictions on the Mississippi limited barge
availability but did not materially disrupt coverage. USDA transportation data continued to flag freight
constraints tied to low water and prior ice damage, reinforcing basis resilience even as futures struggled
to gain traction. Interior processor bids held firm across major ethanol regions, underscoring consistent
spot demand and limited farmer selling.

Soybeans: Soybean basis remained supported alongside a stronger board. CIF Gulf basis held at +116
to +120H for nearby loadings, signaling that export channels remain functional even as Brazil accelerates
shipments. Interior markets continued to reflect strong crush-driven demand, with processors maintaining
competitive bids to secure ownership. River logistics remained uneven but showed some improvement,
helping keep basis firm even as barge traffic gradually recovers. The recent board rally, driven by
Argentine weather and speculative buying, has aligned more closely with cash firmness, reinforcing the
role of domestic demand in anchoring the front end.

Wheat: Wheat cash markets were largely unchanged. CIF Gulf basis for SRW held across a broad range,
roughly +65 to +115H, while HRW basis across the Plains showed minimal movement, indicating
adequate nearby supply. Pacific Northwest bids remained steady, and interior values lacked directional
energy. Despite steady basis, U.S. wheat remains challenged on the export front, with global buyers
continuing to favor lower-cost Black Sea supply. The absence of new logistical or policy disruptions has
kept basis stable but uninspired.

So What? Cash markets continue to send a firmer signal than futures. Corn basis remains supported by
strong export execution and steady ethanol demand, soybeans by resilient crush pull and weather-driven
board support, and wheat by stable interior structure rather than export urgency. While some
transportation constraints have begun to ease, low water conditions and logistics frictions still underpin
nearby values. Until interior demand softens meaningfully or global supply disrupts, physical markets are
likely to remain resilient, even as futures reflect broader balance sheet comfort.

International Cash & Trade Flows

Brazil: Brazil continued to dominate near-term global trade flows. ANEC now estimates January soybean
exports near 3.23 million metric tons following downward revisions tied to harvest delays, while corn
shipments remain projected around 3.45–3.5 million metric tons, reflecting continued late-season
availability. FOB soybean offers from Santos remained competitive into Asia, maintaining a clear discount
versus US Gulf equivalents, while corn out of Paranaguá held near $205 per metric ton, sustaining
Brazil’s edge into Mediterranean and select Asian destinations. Vessel lineups across Santos, Barcarena,
and the northern arc remained active, indicating that logistics are scaling alongside harvest, despite
intermittent weather-related delays in central regions.

Argentina: Argentina remains the key source of supply-side uncertainty. Crop conditions continued to
deteriorate, particularly for soybeans and late-planted corn, amid uneven and insufficient rainfall.
Sensitivity to February precipitation outcomes has intensified, as reproductive stages advance. Despite
agronomic stress, FOB offers have only modestly adjusted, with Up-River corn holding near $225 per
metric ton and soymeal offers elevated on tightening meal availability. Farmer selling remains cautious,
influenced by currency factors and policy uncertainty, keeping nearby export flow constrained relative to
capacity.

China: Chinese demand signals remained selective rather than expansive. No major new US grain
purchases were reported during the window, though routine soybean inflows continued. Market indicators
in China showed modest improvement in crush economics, with domestic futures firming slightly, but
downstream feed demand remains pressured by weak livestock margins. State involvement in domestic
corn markets persisted, supporting internal prices but limiting incremental import demand. Overall, China
continues to provide episodic support on breaks rather than sustained demand acceleration.

EU / Black Sea: Black Sea origins continued to set the tone for global wheat pricing. Russian 12.5
percent protein wheat held firm near $229 per metric ton FOB, with January shipment volumes broadly
in line with seasonal norms. Ukrainian corn offers remained available near $235 per metric ton, though
EU feed demand stayed subdued. French wheat values out of Rouen remained elevated near €240 per
metric ton, leaving EU origin priced out of recent tenders. European Union wheat export licenses
remained behind last year’s pace, reinforcing the competitive pressure exerted by lower-cost Black Sea
supply.

So What? Global trade flows remain firmly dictated by price leadership. Brazil continues to anchor
soybean and corn exports, Russia dominates wheat, and Argentina’s role is increasingly contingent on
weather and producer selling behavior. China’s presence provides occasional demand support but lacks
consistency. Against this backdrop, US export competitiveness remains opportunistic rather than
structural, dependent on timing, logistics, or short-term disruptions elsewhere. Until global FOB spreads
narrow or supply risks materially reshape availability, the US remains a secondary origin in the global
trade hierarchy.

Key Fundamentals & Reports (Jan 29 – Feb 05)

Export Inspections: For the week ending February 1 (published February 5), corn export inspections
totaled 1.12 million metric tons, slightly below the prior week but keeping cumulative shipments near
33.7 million metric tons, roughly 48 percent ahead of last year. Soybean inspections rose to 1.35
million metric tons, offering a modest pickup in loadings, though cumulative shipments remain
approximately 35 percent below last year amid Brazil’s accelerating program. Wheat inspections
totaled 0.34 million metric tons, keeping season-to-date exports near 16.5 million metric tons, or
about 22 percent ahead of last year, broadly in line with USDA’s annual outlook.

Export Sales: Weekly export sales for the week ending January 29 continued to show solid physical
movement. Corn net sales totaled 1.62 million metric tons, with shipments of 1.65 million metric tons,
confirming sustained pace. Soybean net sales reached 825,000 metric tons, while shipments climbed to
1.3 million metric tons, reflecting strong front-end execution. Wheat net sales totaled 560,000 metric tons,
with shipments of 380,000 metric tons, keeping commitments near 90 percent of USDA’s forecast. No
daily flash sales were reported during the window.

Ethanol Production & Stocks: EIA data for the week ending January 30 (published January 31) showed
ethanol production slipping to roughly 0.96 million barrels per day, while stocks declined to about 25.14
million barrels. Blending demand improved to approximately 890,000 barrels per day, supporting stable
grind levels even with a softer production print. While margins remain sensitive to energy volatility,
ethanol continues to provide a steady floor for corn demand into early February.

CFTC Positioning: Managed money positioning continued to unwind. As of January 27, corn’s net short
was reduced to approximately 72,000 contracts, the smallest bearish position since mid-2025. In
soybeans, funds increased their modest net long to around 17,300 contracts, supported by strong
domestic demand and Argentine weather concerns. Chicago SRW wheat remained the most bearish,
with a net short near 94,700 contracts, reflecting persistent global supply pressure.

So What? Core U.S. fundamentals remain constructive beneath the surface. Corn exports continue to run
well ahead of last year, ethanol grind remains steady, and speculative pressure is easing. Wheat
fundamentals are stable but overshadowed by global supply, keeping positioning defensive. Soybeans
remain mixed, with domestic demand offsetting weak exports. Across markets, basis and execution
continue to provide a clearer signal than futures, suggesting that physical markets remain firmer than the
board implies as February begins (no nopa data).

International Reports

Brazil: Brazil remains firmly in control of near-term global supply, though the January soybean export
estimate has been revised lower. ANEC now estimates January soybean exports near 3.23 million metric
tons, while corn shipments are projected around 3.45 million metric tons, underscoring strong execution
despite uneven harvest progress. Northern ports and carryover stocks continue to anchor early-season
loadings, allowing exporters to scale shipments even as rainfall slows fieldwork across central regions.
FOB soybean offers out of Santos remain competitive into Asia, while corn out of Paranaguá continues to
price attractively into Mediterranean and select Asian destinations. Attention is increasingly shifting
toward southern Brazil, where persistent moisture is delaying harvest and raising disease risks in Paraná
and Rio Grande do Sul, keeping yield visibility a key variable for March–April availability. For now,
logistics are functioning, but forward clarity will hinge on how quickly harvest accelerates once weather
normalizes.

Argentina: Argentina’s production outlook remains fragile. Crop conditions continue to reflect the impact
of uneven rainfall, particularly for early-planted soybeans and corn, keeping yield risk elevated. While no
new policy changes were announced during the window, farmer selling remains cautious, influenced by
currency dynamics and lingering uncertainty around production outcomes. FOB values have adjusted
only modestly, with Up-River corn holding near $225 per metric ton and soymeal offers remaining firm,
signaling that the market is beginning to price tighter availability without fully repricing volume risk.
February weather remains critical, but the focus is gradually shifting from crop stress to the question of
how much exportable supply will ultimately materialize.

China: Chinese demand signals remained measured. No large new U.S. purchase announcements were
reported during the period, though routine soybean flows continued. Domestic indicators showed slight
firmness in futures markets, suggesting marginal improvement in crush economics, but downstream feed
demand remains constrained by weak livestock margins. State support for domestic grain markets
persisted, limiting urgency for incremental imports. Overall, China continues to act as a stabilizing
presence on breaks rather than a driver of sustained upside.

EU / Black Sea: Black Sea origin continues to set the benchmark for global wheat pricing. Russian 12.5
percent protein wheat held near $229 per metric ton FOB, with January shipments broadly in line with
seasonal norms. Ukrainian corn offers remained available near $235 per metric ton via Danube channels,
though demand from EU buyers remained subdued. European wheat values stayed elevated, leaving EU
origin largely uncompetitive in recent tenders. With no new logistical or policy disruptions emerging, low-
cost Black Sea supply continues to cap upside and anchor global trade flows.

So What? International markets remain defined by execution rather than scarcity. Brazil continues to
supply aggressively, Argentina’s risk profile is rising but not yet fully priced, and Russia remains the
dominant force in wheat. China’s demand remains episodic, supportive on breaks but not
transformational. The global balance still looks ample on paper, but regional weather and producer selling
behavior in South America are becoming increasingly important in shaping forward availability. As
February unfolds, global price sensitivity will hinge less on headline supply and more on whether these
emerging cracks translate into measurable volume constraints.

Weather & Crop Conditions

United States
Weather impacts during the period remained more logistical than agronomic. Cold conditions persisted
across parts of the Midwest and Plains in late January, contributing to ice accumulation along inland
waterways and complicating river movement. Snow cover across the Central and Southern Plains
remained generally adequate, limiting winterkill risk for hard red winter wheat, while no new USDA winter
wheat condition updates were released during the window. Precipitation remained limited across much of
the Corn Belt, though no immediate crop stress concerns were identified. River operations continued
under draft and tow-size restrictions tied to low water and prior ice damage, while Gulf loading proceeded
steadily under ongoing limitations.

Brazil
Brazilian weather remained broadly favorable nationally but uneven across regions. Continued rainfall in
central and northern producing areas supported soybean development but further slowed early harvest
progress, particularly in Mato Grosso and Goiás. In contrast, southern Brazil continued to experience
excess moisture, with CONAB reporting increased disease pressure and lodging risks in parts of Paraná
and Rio Grande do Sul, where wet soils delayed fieldwork and clouded yield outlooks. While no major
national yield revision was issued, field-level crop ratings show increasing variability, with southern
conditions now a key factor for second-half supply visibility. So far, weather-related disruptions have not
materially impacted logistics, but regional divergence remains a growing concern.

Argentina
Argentina continued to face challenging conditions. MAGyP’s latest crop bulletin showed a further drop in
good-to-excellent ratings, particularly for soybeans, with only about 19% of the crop now rated G/E, down
from 25% a week earlier. Rainfall coverage remained uneven, offering scattered relief but failing to
reverse widespread moisture deficits. Late-planted corn is also under increasing stress. With crops in
critical reproductive stages, the next two weeks are pivotal for yield retention. Despite the deterioration,
FOB offers have yet to reflect the full extent of production risk, keeping attention focused on whether
exportable surplus will contract meaningfully.

Global
Outside South America, global weather risks remained limited. Winter wheat across Russia, Ukraine, and
the European Union stayed in dormancy under generally adequate snow cover, with no new winterkill
threats reported. Black Sea logistics continued without disruption, and weather conditions across other
major exporting regions, including Australia and Canada, remained stable. No new global production risks
emerged during the reporting window.

So What?
Weather risk is increasingly concentrated in South America. Southern Brazil’s excess rainfall and
Argentina’s entrenched drought have emerged as critical variables for soybean and corn yield potential.
Central Brazil continues to support strong overall production, but the southern impact could shape
March–April availability. In Argentina, the steep decline in crop ratings underscores rising supply risk,
though the market has yet to fully price it in. For the U.S. and other major exporters, weather remains
secondary. As February progresses, yield outcomes in southern Brazil and rainfall patterns in Argentina
will likely set the tone for market direction.

Synthesis & Outlook

Futures finished the week mixed, with soybeans rallying while corn and wheat held in tight ranges. March
corn settled near $4.28 per bushel, soybeans at $10.90, and soft red winter wheat around $5.32. Despite
comfortable balance sheets on paper, calendar spreads remain short of full carry, CIF and interior basis
levels are holding firm, and physical movement across both export and domestic channels continues to
outpace what the board implies. Managed money has reduced short exposure in corn and wheat, while
soybeans have shifted to a small net long, reflecting increasing weather sensitivity and crush strength.
The disconnect between paper and physical markets persists.

Corn continues to exhibit the clearest divergence between futures and fundamentals. Export inspections
remain well ahead of last year, ethanol grind is steady, and lingering logistics constraints are preventing
carry expansion despite ample supply. CIF Gulf basis remains supported near +100H, interior markets
are stable, and spreads continue to resist widening. Futures remain capped, but the front end is well
anchored by execution and downstream demand.

Soybeans are showing renewed strength. Domestic crush demand and firm processor bids continue to
anchor the front end, while the board rallied on Argentine weather risks, strong basis, and rising fund
interest. Exports still lag under the weight of Brazil’s aggressive early-season program, but speculative
momentum and biofuel policy developments have lifted sentiment. The balance between bullish U.S.
fundamentals and bearish global supply keeps structure firm, even as flat price rises

Bullish triggers: South American weather remains the primary upside risk. Southern Brazil’s persistent
rainfall and Argentina’s intensifying drought are raising production uncertainty, particularly with soybeans
in critical stages. In the U.S., ethanol production remains steady, blending demand is improving, and
basis strength suggests end users remain active. The Treasury’s 45Z biofuel credit proposal has also
added optimism to the soy complex. Any flash Chinese demand or renewed logistics bottlenecks could
tighten nearby spreads faster than the board implies.

Bearish triggers: Global supply remains ample. Brazil’s export program is scaling efficiently, and if
weather conditions stabilize in southern Brazil or meaningful rainfall materializes in Argentina, production
risk could quickly fade. Ethanol production, while stable, has not reaccelerated, and any sustained
softening in grind or basis would challenge corn’s front-end support. In wheat, aggressive Black Sea
pricing continues to limit upside, and without a clear export acceleration, rallies remain vulnerable to
producer selling and speculative pressure.

Watchlist (Feb 05–Feb 12):

Argentina: Do crop conditions stabilize or continue to deteriorate?

Brazil: Does harvest momentum accelerate, particularly in the south?

CIF and interior basis: Early signs of softening versus continued firmness

Ethanol: Can production and blending demand remain stable into February?

China: Any material shift from routine buying to multi-cargo demand

Funds: Further short covering in corn and wheat, or renewed selling pressure?

In summary: The grain complex remains structurally uneven. Futures still suggest theoretical
abundance, but spreads, basis, and physical execution point to localized tightness, particularly in corn
and soybeans. Wheat stays pressured by global supply, despite steady U.S. shipments. Until full carry
emerges or physical demand softens, price discovery will remain shaped more by logistics and cash flow
than by speculative sentiment.

Ethanol for America Act

The major barrier to a higher Renewable Fuel Standard (RFS) is the fuel versus food debate. Now is the
perfect time to move to E15. Corn prices are extremely low, and the latest USDA WASDE reported a record
crop and a record crop expansion.

USDA WASDE forecast corn production at 16.7 billion bushels, with the yield at 188.8 bushels per acre and
ending stocks at 2.1 billion. The season-average corn price dropped 30 cents to $3.90 a bushel. Most
importantly, 202526 crop year is expected to have 1.8 billion more bushels than 2024/25.

With current ethanol production consuming 5.6 billion bushels, a 10 percent increase in consumption would
consume an additional 560 million bushels. The reality is that year around E15 would add approximately
50 million bushels and regulatory streamlining would add another 50 million bushels. At some point over
the next ten years, the additional corn consumption could be 2.8 billion bushels minus lower fuel
consumption resulting from electric vehicles. The ethanol corn consumption increase is likely to be more
of a S curve as new compacity comes online.

With Brazil and the US corn and soybean production increasing, 100 million bushels of ethanol corn
consumption will barely move the needle. The ethanol production capacity expansion that would be three
to four years from now is when the corn consumption impact to the corn market would be felt.

Executive Summary

Corn – Export Flow Holds the Front End Together
Ahead of the Jan 12 Data Dump
March corn settled at $4.45/bu, up 5¢ w/w, with the Mar/May
spread at +7¢ carry (about 50 percent of full carry), keeping
structure relatively supported even with the market leaning
into a “big January” USDA event. Gulf export channels
remain the clearest near-term anchor: CIF Gulf corn bids
were +81¢ over March and FOB Gulf offers were +106¢
over March as of Jan 1, while weekly export inspections
printed 1.21 MMT (week ended Jan 1), taking 2025/26
cumulative exports to 885.5 mbu (+69 percent y/y). The
export program is also still showing up in USDA flashes,

including a 150,000 MT corn sale to Mexico (Jan 6), another reminder that the market is sensitive to any
January WASDE change that either validates (or pushes back on) the current “export upgrade / yield trim”
bias.

Wheat – Heavy Fund Shorts vs. a Seedings/Stocks Catalyst

March SRW wheat settled at $5.20/bu, up 14¢ w/w, while the Mar/May spread stayed near 11¢ of carry
(about 40 percent of full carry), still consistent with a market searching for direction rather than pricing
tightness. Export execution remains mixed: weekly wheat inspections were 183k MT (week ended Jan 1,
–27 percent w/w), but season-to-date shipments reached 15.263 MMT (+6 percent y/y), good enough to
keep “exports may be revised up” in play for January, especially if USDA leans into the recent
competitiveness narrative. Positioning is the other piece: spec funds remain meaningfully short (–71,000
CBOT wheat net) (week ended Dec 30, futures-only series referenced), which keeps the market
vulnerable to sharp, fast rallies if Jan 12 surprises lean bullish (seedings lower, exports higher, or stocks
tighter).

Soybeans – USDA Flash Sales to China Surface, but the Export Pace Problem Isn’t Solved
Yet
March soybeans settled at $10.67/bu, up 5¢ w/w, with the Mar/May spread at 11¢ carry (50 percent
carry). The curve is doing something slightly different further out, with Jul ’26/Nov ’26 at a +12¢ inverse,
hinting that the market still wants to keep some risk premium in the new-crop window even as South
America harvest pressure approaches. Cash/export indicators were steady: CIF NOLA soybean bids
were +100¢ over Jan and FOB Gulf offers were +102¢ over Jan (as of Jan 1). Demand signals are split:
USDA daily sales flashes included 336,000 MT to China (Jan 2) plus earlier China flashes (136,000 MT
on Dec 30; 100,000 MT on Dec 29), but shipments remain the constraint, weekly soybean inspections
were 0.98 MMT (week ended Jan 1) and 2025/26 cumulative inspections are 603 mbu (–45 percent y/y),

Macro / Policy – Dollar Slightly Firmer, BRL Stronger; Energy Quiet

Into Jan 12 Cross-market signals stayed mostly “background noise” this week. DXY traded around 98.6
(Mar), up 0.5 percent w/w, while USD/BRL fell to 5.37 (about a 1 percent real appreciation w/w), a
marginal shift that can slow Brazilian farmer selling at the margin, but not enough (by itself) to reprice
export competitiveness. Energy remains range-bound: WTI Feb near $56/bbl, down about $1 w/w, which
keeps biofuel economics stable rather than forcing a new demand narrative into corn/soyoil. Net: macro is
not doing the heavy lifting right now; it’s mostly clearing the lane for Jan 12 fundamentals and South
American weather to drive price discovery.

Weather – Warm/Dry US Pattern; Argentina Dryness vs Brazil’s Generally Favorable Setup

In the US, NOAA’s 6–10 day (Jan 12–16) calls for much above-normal temperatures across most of the
Plains/Midwest, while precipitation probabilities are broadly below normal across the primary grain belt.
The 8–14 day (Jan 14–20) maintains that warm/dry bias, which reduces winterkill risk but keeps attention
on HRW moisture recharge in the southern Plains and keeps river ice risk muted for logistics. In South
America, the market is increasingly treating January as a two-speed story: Brazil’s core areas are largely
in good shape (with some regions even flirting with “too wet” issues into early harvest logistics), while
Argentina is the standout risk, forecast models and reporting highlight a very dry mid-January window that
can start translating into measurable soybean/corn yield risk if it persists through pod-setting.

What’s Next (Jan 08 – Jan 15):

USDA January Reports: Focus on whether Crop Production and Grain Stocks force WASDE
adjustments rather than headline balance-sheet changes; reaction will hinge on implied feed/residual and
seedings signals.

Soybean Exports: Track whether recent China buying translates into sustained weekly sales and
inspections; without follow-through, USDA is likely to defend exports cautiously or lean more on crush.

Wheat Seedings & Stocks: Winter wheat acreage and Dec 1 stocks are the primary catalysts; with funds
short, even modest surprises could trigger sharp but potentially short-lived moves.

Argentina Weather: Mid-January rainfall remains the swing variable; persistent heat/dryness in the core
belt would reprice soy and meal risk quickly, while stabilization caps upside.

Gulf Basis & Logistics: Replacement values and barge availability will show up first in CIF and nearby
spreads as post-holiday trade normalizes.
