Corn Market Deep Dive

Pre-WASDE Positioning: Trade talk continues to center on whether final Crop Production trims USDA’s
current 2025 yield (186 bpa) and whether strong export execution gives USDA room to keep the balance
sheet tighter via higher exports. The market’s bias has leaned toward “modest tightening” (yield risk +
export-friendly math), but without a verified Reuters poll table in hand, the clean anchor remains
execution data, inspections, Gulf values, and whether the next weekly Export Sales report (week ended
Jan 1) shows meaningful catch-up post-holidays.

Futures & Spreads: March corn settled at $4.45/bu (+5¢ w/w) and continues to trade like a market that
isn’t short nearby ownership, even without a headline catalyst. Calendar structure is still carrying, but not

“max carry”: the Mar/May spread is 7¢ carry (about 50 percent of full carry), keeping the curve’s message
closer to steady demand underneath than burdensome supply. Technically, the market is hovering
around the 200-day moving average near $4.45, which traders have treated as an important “line in the
sand” going into Monday’s reports.

Funds & Positioning: Managed money remains net short -53,192 contracts in CBOT corn futures-only
as of Dec 30, 2025 (Long 309,183 vs Short 362,375). That keeps positioning from being outright bullish,
but it matters into Jan 12 because any surprise tightening in final yield or stocks can still force incremental
short covering, especially if spreads/basis refuse to break.

Cash & Basis: Gulf basis remains the anchor for the front end. In your cash set, CIF Gulf sits near +82¢
over March and FOB Gulf near +106¢ over March, a structure that still reads as export replacement
values holding together even as futures churn. Interior tone remains steady rather than defensive, which
fits the message from the board: carry is present, but it hasn’t been able to fully take control of nearby.

Export Sales & Inspections: The clean, verifiable signal this week is inspections. USDA’s export
inspections show corn inspections at 1.21 MMT for the week ended Jan 1, versus 877,219 MT the same
week last year. On the sales side, the market is also watching whether the next weekly report confirms
continued strength: USDA reported net corn sales of 756,400 MT for the week ended Dec 25, and
2025/26 commitments (sales + exports) at 1.99 bbu, up 30 percent y/y, important context for why the
curve has stayed supported even without a weather scare.

International Trade / Competition: Global price competition hasn’t disappeared, but near-term US
export execution is doing enough to keep the market from slipping into a purely storage-driven structure.
The other key international variable for January is less about “new” export business and more about what
South America weather implies for late-Q1 and Q2 supply, especially Argentina corn during critical
development stages. If South America stays broadly favorable, the market will likely need Monday’s US
reports to be the bullish catalyst; if Argentina turns meaningfully drier/hotter, that becomes a second path
to tightening spreads and keeping funds from pressing shorts.

So What? Corn is acting like a market with quiet demand gravity: futures are stable-to-firmer, spreads are
only paying partial carry, and inspections are still strong enough to keep Gulf values from cracking. The
Jan 12 reports are the hinge, if final yield or Dec 1 stocks tighten the implied balance sheet, the market
has room to react because managed money is still net short (futures-only) and the curve isn’t “fully
carried.” If the reports lean neutral-to-bearish, the path of least resistance is not necessarily a collapse,
it’s more likely a drift lower with spreads doing the real signaling day-to-day.

Soybean Market Deep Dive

Pre-WASDE Positioning: The soybean story into Jan 12 remains split between soft shipment execution
and renewed China headline demand. The base-case trade view is that USDA won’t need to reinvent the
US balance sheet unless it chooses to re-tilt demand between exports and crush in response to the
slower export trajectory versus last year and the steadier processor pull. What changed this week is the
tone around China: USDA daily flashes confirmed multiple soybean sales to China (including 336,000 MT
on Jan 2), and market chatter has focused on whether state buying (Sinograin) is re-entering in size for
spring shipment. The key pre-WASDE question remains the same, does USDA continue to defend
exports, or lean more heavily on crush to keep demand intact as the marketing-year pace becomes
harder to ignore?

Futures & Spreads: March ’26 soybeans settled at $10.67/bu, up +5¢ w/w, but the path was choppy,
more “headline-driven pop” than a clean trend. Structure is still doing most of the talking, the Mar/May

carry sits at +11¢, about 50 percent of full carry, which reads as adequate nearby coverage rather than
any front-end shortage. Farther out, the curve is sending a different signal: Jul ’26 vs Nov ’26 remains
inverted by 12¢, keeping a weather-premium feel embedded in summer ownership even as the nearby
stays commercially supplied

Products & Crush: Soybean products provided steady underlying support, with both meal and oil firming
modestly on the week. The latest available NOPA crush data (November) printed 216.0 million
bushels, exceeding last year’s pace and reinforcing the view that domestic processing demand remains
resilient despite slower export shipments. Crush margins remain positive and continue to incentivize run
rates near capacity, keeping processor bids engaged across the interior. Into the January WASDE, this
keeps crush as the most defensible demand line item, raising the question of whether USDA offsets any
export caution by leaning more heavily on domestic processing rather than reducing total use.

Cash & Basis: Gulf signals were steady-to-softer but not weak: CIF NOLA is around +100¢ over Jan,
while FOB Gulf is near +102¢ over Jan, consistent with a cash market that’s being supported by nearby
logistics/friction and steady processor demand, but still lacking the kind of relentless export pull that would
force a sharper basis surge. Brazil remains the reference competitor: Paranaguá Feb FOB indications
(12¢/bu over March equivalent) keep the export program price-sensitive, especially if buyers can wait for
South America.

Funds & Positioning: Managed money positioning remains a swing factor, but it’s not screaming
“crowded long.” On a futures-only basis, managed money is net long +121,811 as of Dec 30, 2025 (long
203,843 vs short 82,032), which leaves room for additional buying if China buying persists and/or South
American weather risk re-prices.

Export Sales & Inspections: Execution remains the constraint. US soybean export inspections totaled
0.98 MMT (36 mbu) for the latest week, bringing cumulative shipments to 16.40 MMT (603 mbu), still 45
percent below last year’s pace. Export sales have improved at the margin, net sales of 504k MT in the
most recent report, but commitments remain insufficient to materially close the shipment gap. That’s why
the Sinograin headline matters: it doesn’t fix execution overnight, but it reduces the “no-China” tail risk
and helps explain why futures can firm even as the export tape remains heavy.

Policy / China Demand Context: This week’s soybean support came less from broad macro and more
from a very specific demand narrative: state buying + a push to clear domestic inventory space (Sinograin
auctions) to accommodate incoming US cargoes. If that state-led buying continues, it’s the cleanest path
to a pre-WASDE repricing, because it attacks the market’s biggest doubt: whether US export demand can
stabilize at all this season.

So What? The soybean board is still being supported more by crush and curve structure than by clean
export execution. Nearby spreads only pay partial carry and the back end remains inverted, which keeps
ownership from getting too comfortable even as shipments lag. The Sinograin headline matters because
it reduces the “no-China” tail risk, but it does not repair the export math overnight, inspections remain well
behind last year on a cumulative basis, and sales need sustained follow-through to change that. Into Jan
12, the market is effectively asking whether USDA defends exports or leans harder on crush to keep the
balance sheet stable. Without repeatable China business or a weather premium, soybeans likely stay
range-bound, with structure doing most of the signaling.

Wheat Market Deep Dive

Pre-WASDE Positioning: Ahead of Jan 12, the trade expects only marginal adjustments to the US wheat
balance sheet, with attention centered on the “January bundle” reports (Grain Stocks + Winter Wheat
Seedings) more than routine WASDE line edits. Execution has been respectable (shipments running
ahead y/y), which keeps “exports could be revised up” on the list, but global competition remains the
limiting factor. With funds still meaningfully short in CBOT wheat (futures-only), the market is more
vulnerable to sharp rallies if seedings print lower or stocks come in tighter than expected, though any
bullish reaction still faces a ceiling from ample Black Sea and Southern Hemisphere supply.

Futures & Spreads: CBOT March SRW wheat settled at $5.20/bu, up roughly 14¢ w/w, remaining range-
bound despite recent short-covering attempts. The Mar/May SRW spread holds near +11¢ carry, roughly
40 percent of full commercial carry, signaling comfortable nearby availability without forcing full storage
economics. KC HRW March closed near $5.28/bu, maintaining an 18¢ premium to SRW, while
Minneapolis HRS March finished near $5.56/bu. Volatility remains muted, and open interest changes
suggest the price action continues to be positioning-driven rather than fundamentally led. The curve
structure still rewards storage and offers little indication of front-end tightness.

Cash & Basis: US wheat cash markets remain weak relative to global competition. SRW FOB Gulf
values near $6.23/bu and HRW FOB Gulf near $6.56/bu keep US origin priced $15–25/MT above Black
Sea offers, limiting competitiveness in major tenders. Interior basis has been largely unchanged, with
HRW bids in Kansas and Oklahoma around 20–40¢ under March, and SRW river bids holding near 15–
20¢ under. Protein premiums remain intact, but overall cash movement is slow as mills remain covered
and export demand favors cheaper origins.

Export Sales & Inspections: Weekly US wheat export inspections totaled 183,000 MT (6.7 mbu), down
w/w but still leaving marketing-year shipments near 15.263 MMT, roughly six percent ahead of last year’s
pace. While execution remains solid, new sales activity has slowed materially, and the US continues to
miss major North African and Middle Eastern tenders dominated by Russian and EU wheat. Mexico and
select Latin American destinations remain the most consistent buyers, but volumes are insufficient to
materially shift the global balance. The market continues to distinguish between decent execution and
weak forward demand.

Global Supply & Competition: Russia remains the dominant force in global wheat trade, with FOB
offers near $229–233/MT, supported by a weak ruble and aggressive export pace. Australia’s harvest has
added additional exportable supply, while Argentina’s record wheat crop (27–28 MMT) is flowing
aggressively into Brazil and Asia. EU exports remain steady but unremarkable. Despite dryness concerns
in parts of the US Plains, global supply remains ample, and no meaningful exporter is signaling restraint
at this stage.

Funds & Positioning: Managed money remains heavily net short -70K contracts, with only modest short-
covering observed recently. The size of the short leaves wheat vulnerable to brief rallies on headline risk,
but without a structural tightening signal, those rallies continue to fade. Commercials remain opportunistic
buyers at low price levels, but not aggressive enough to alter the broader market tone.

So What? Wheat remains a market supported by positioning rather than fundamentals. Spreads continue
to encourage storage, cash markets show no tightening signal, and global competition remains intense.
While US export execution has improved year-over-year, it has not translated into stronger forward
demand or improved competitiveness. Unless winter wheat acreage surprises lower or Black Sea flows
are disrupted, Chicago wheat is likely to remain range-bound, with rallies capped by global supply and
weak export pricing power. The market is firming at the margins, but it is not tightening.

US Cash Market & Basis

Corn: River basis improved modestly this week amid stable water levels on the Lower Mississippi, though
colder weather introduced some logistical caution. Illinois River bids firmed to –18¢ under March, up 2¢
w/w, while the Lower Miss held near –40¢ to –43¢ under with steady drafts and traffic. Spot freight from
Cairo to Memphis eased to $19.00/ton, down slightly on quieter post-holiday movement. CIF Gulf basis
strengthened to +82¢ over March, up 1¢, bolstered by robust export inspections and Mexican buying.
FOB Gulf offers remained near +106¢ over March, reflecting solid replacement values. Processor bids in
Iowa and Nebraska stayed firm at +12¢ to +18¢ over, supported by steady ethanol economics. National
average corn basis was near –35¢ under, firmer by 3¢ on the week.

Soybeans: Processor bids in the Eastern Corn Belt held firm, aided by supportive crush margins and
consistent soymeal pull. River basis was mostly steady where barge logistics improved on the Illinois and
Lower Mississippi, but export demand from China stayed uneven despite recent flashes. CIF Gulf
soybean basis slipped to +100¢ over January, down 2¢, amid better freight availability but softer new
sales. FOB Gulf offers were near +102¢ over January, down 6¢ w/w. National average soybean basis
hovered near –72¢ under the March futures equivalent, slightly firmer from last week.

Wheat: HRW and SRW FOB Gulf values stayed flat near $6.56/bu and $6.23/bu, respectively, with export
basis unchanged amid quiet global tenders. Interior bids in Kansas and Oklahoma remained soft at –45¢
to –50¢ under March, influenced by storage incentives over spot consumption. Basis levels in the
Southern Plains milling market were steady as January coverage held firm. Domestic demand from flour
buyers was consistent but lacked fresh spot urgency, keeping interior values range-bound.

So What? Logistics remained stable with river levels supportive, allowing Gulf basis to hold firm in corn
while soy and wheat faced global competitive pressure. Export flow anchored corn cash strength,
processors provided the soy floor, and wheat stayed defensive without new demand catalysts. Basis tone
in corn and soy indicates demand is absorbing nearby supply without excess pressure. Absent stronger
China export confirmation or weather risks, domestic users will keep setting the nearby cash pace

International Cash & Trade Flows

Brazil: Brazil continues to lead global soybean and corn exports, with December loadings showing
strength despite the onset of early harvest pressures. ANEC reported December soybean exports near
3.02 MMT, while corn paced around 6.35 MMT, both solid but reflecting a seasonal slowdown from peak
months. FOB Santos soybean offers held near $422/MT ($11.49/bu), maintaining a competitive edge over
US Gulf equivalents into China. Corn FOB values stayed firm around $222/MT ($5.64/bu), positioning
Brazil as the preferred origin for Southeast Asia. Soybean harvest progressed to early stages in Mato
Grosso, with estimates around 5-10 percent complete amid generally favorable but locally wet conditions
that could delay logistics. Export corridors at Paranaguá and Santos remained efficient, with minimal
congestion reported.

Argentina: Up-River FOB prices were steady: corn offered around $218/MT, while soymeal traded near
$345/MT (47 percent protein basis), aligning with recent export trends. Farmer sales moderated post-
election stability, but dryness in southern regions raised concerns for corn and soybean development
during key growth phases. The Rosario Exchange noted corn crops in good condition overall, though
moisture deficits persist in parts of Buenos Aires and La Pampa. The peso weakened further in parallel
markets, with the unofficial rate near ARS 1,470/USD, adding to export incentives amid macro
adjustments. Wheat harvest advanced to nearly 93 percent complete, with production expectations hitting
a record 27.8 MMT under mostly dry conditions. Crushers maintained steady operations, drawing on

accumulated stocks.

China: Import focus shifted toward US soybeans amid state-led buying, with recent purchases pushing
totals near 10 MMT for the 2025/26 marketing year. US Gulf offers have remained generally less
competitive than Brazilian nearby export values, but the recent USDA flashes signaled improving US
traction for spring coverage. Crushers secured Q1 coverage via mixed origins, with confirmed US sales
including 336,000 MT reported early January. Dalian Jan corn futures settled around ¥2,400/MT
($6.80/bu equivalent), above import parity but reflecting domestic supply pressures. Market discussions
on long-term commitments (12 MMT in 2026 and beyond) continued, treated as directional amid tariff
stability.

EU / Black Sea: Russian FOB wheat offers for 12.5 percent protein firmed to $228–233/MT, supported
by reset export taxes and steady tender wins in Egypt and MENA. French wheat near $228/MT (Rouen)
stayed on the sidelines, with recent tenders favoring Black Sea origins. Ukraine sustained corn exports
via Danube and rail, with offers around $173–175/MT into Spain and Italy, up slightly on demand. EU
grain stocks improved post-harvest, curbing import needs. Russian and Ukrainian soymeal undercut US
offers into southern Europe, aided by freight and currency advantages.

Other Regions: Australia's harvest progressed strongly, with CBH receiving 23.5 MMT total (mostly
wheat), setting receivals records amid improved yields and protein. Canada maintained steady spring
wheat flows to Mexico and Asia. India stayed sidelined in global trade, with no policy changes. South
Africa reported no significant shifts.

So What? US exporters face ongoing challenges into Asia and MENA as Brazil and Russia hold price
advantages. China's pivot to US soybeans offers Gulf support, but execution lags. Argentina's dryness
adds risk premium potential, though not yet disrupting flows. Until FOB differentials narrow or demand
reallocates, US cash resilience will hinge on domestic pull and basis rather than global arbitrage.

Key Fundamentals & Reports (Dec 17 – Dec 30)

Crop Progress (USDA): No formal Crop Progress report is issued during winter, but state-level updates
and private assessments pegged national winter wheat conditions near 52 percent good-to-excellent, up
slightly from late December readings but still reflecting patchy dryness in the southern Plains. Emergence
was effectively complete, with Kansas reporting 60 percent good-to-excellent (vs 47 percent last year)
and Texas near 45 percent (improved w/w on scattered rains). Dryness in western Oklahoma and eastern
Colorado kept some stands stressed, but overall ratings held steady without widespread winterkill risks in
the forecast.

Export Inspections (USDA FGIS): Corn inspections hit 1.21 MMT (+8 percent w/w), outpacing last
year’s pace by 69 percent and pushing cumulative 2025/26 shipments to 885.5 mbu. Soybean
inspections slipped to 0.98 MMT (–12 percent w/w), the softest in three weeks, underscoring slower
China pull versus Brazil’s ongoing flow. Wheat inspections totaled 183k MT (–27 percent w/w), focused
on the Gulf but lagging seasonal averages. Overall, the data reinforces corn’s export strength, soy’s
shipment lag, and wheat’s mixed competitiveness.

Export Sales (USDA FAS): The next weekly Export Sales report (covering the week ended Jan 1) is due
Jan 8, with the market watching whether holiday-delayed bookings surface. Daily sales flashes kept
demand headlines active, including 336,000 MT of soybeans to China (Jan 2) and a 150,000 MT corn
sale to Mexico (Jan 6). Outside of the China business, there have been no confirmed large soybean
bookings, keeping attention on whether the upcoming sales report shows enough follow-through to
influence USDA’s export assumptions into the January WASDE.

Ethanol Production & Stocks (EIA): Ethanol output eased to 1.098 mbpd (–22,000 bpd w/w), reflecting
post-holiday adjustments and steady blending rates. Stocks built to 23.652 million barrels (+708,000 w/w),
consistent with seasonal patterns and no major disruptions. Implied corn grind hovered near 98 mbu,
supported by stable margins and firm processor pull in the Western Corn Belt.

CFTC Positioning (latest available Disaggregated COT snapshot): Commitments of Traders (as of
Dec 30, published Jan 2): Managed money held net short in corn (–53,192 contracts, little changed w/w)
and wheat (–70,991 contracts, minor covering), while soybeans stayed net long (+121,811 contracts)
after light additions. Open interest dipped modestly across the complex, signaling positioning was more
about Jan 12 prep than aggressive bets. Without official surprises, the data keeps funds as a potential
volatility amplifier if reports lean bullish, but not the primary driver.

NOPA Crush: No fresh data released in the window; the next monthly report (December) is due Jan 15.
Private estimates for December point toward another solid print above 195 mbu, backed by strong crush
economics and soymeal demand. Processor bids stayed active, confirming throughput remains a key
offset to softer soy exports.

So What? Data this week was mostly confirmatory rather than game-changing: corn exports and ethanol
use provide the supportive undercurrent, soybeans lean on flashes and crush to counter soft inspections,
and wheat’s execution stays decent but uninspiring. With no Crop Progress and the big USDA event
looming, physical signals (Gulf basis, inspections) are doing the heavy lifting. If Jan 12 validates export
upgrades or yield trims, funds have room to react; otherwise, the tone drifts neutral with South America
weather as the wildcard.

International Reports

Brazil: ANEC's early-January lineup data pegged soybean exports tracking around 2.8 MMT for the
month so far and corn near 5.2 MMT, in line with seasonal wind-down for old-crop soy but still reflecting
solid safrinha movement. FOB Santos soybeans were quoted near $422/MT ($11.49/bu), holding a
competitive $10/MT edge over US Gulf into Asian markets, while corn offers around $222/MT ($5.64/bu)
kept Brazil leading feed grain flows. Mato Grosso soybean harvest pushed to about 8 percent complete
by Jan 5, helped by generally supportive rains but with pockets of excess moisture slowing logistics in
northern regions. CONAB held off on a fresh bulletin this week, though trade sources anticipate a slight
bump to 2025/26 soybean output in the upcoming release. Port operations at Santos and Paranaguá
stayed efficient, without notable backups despite early wet-harvest chatter.

Argentina: The Buenos Aires Grain Exchange noted wheat harvest nearing 93 percent done as of Dec
30, lifting its production outlook to 27.7 MMT amid favorable late-season conditions in key zones.
Soybean planting advanced to 82 percent complete, with crops mostly in good shape but dryness building
in southern areas, while corn development held steady without major revisions. MAGyP's latest weekly
confirmed planting stability but flagged emerging moisture deficits in Buenos Aires and La Pampa. Up-
River FOB corn indications sat near $218/MT, soymeal around $345/MT, both unchanged w/w. The peso
slipped to about 1,470 per USD in informal channels, bolstering export edges amid ongoing macro
tweaks. Farmer forward sales picked up modestly post-holidays, with crushers tapping into earlier
stockpiles from incentive programs.

China: Amid renewed state-led US soybean purchases, CNGOIC's early-January grain update
highlighted steady feed consumption but emphasized plans to boost domestic soybean capacity, with
total grain feed/residual use seen near 287 MMT for 2025/26. No fresh tariff shifts were announced, but
crushers leaned into US origins for Q1–Q2 coverage, with bookings approaching 10 MMT ytd. Dalian Jan

corn futures closed around ¥2,400/MT ($6.80/bu eq), soybeans near ¥4,850/MT, and soymeal
¥3,200/MT, all firmer on the week amid import momentum. MARA and Customs issued no new reserve or
import directives in the period, though market buzz around long-term commitments (up to 25 MMT+
through 2028) kept demand sentiment afloat without firm confirmations.

EU / Black Sea: Russian 12.5 percent wheat FOB offers edged to $228–233/MT (+1 w/w) following a
duty cut to 97.3 RUB/ton from Jan 12, with further zeroing eyed mid-month, aiding tender wins in MENA
and beyond. Ukrainian corn offers via Danube held near $173–175/MT, finding takers in EU southern
ports. FranceAgriMer trimmed its 2025/26 soft wheat export forecast outside the EU to 7.6 MMT but noted
stable winter cereal conditions, with French wheat ratings around 68 percent good-to-excellent.

So What? Worldwide flows are still led by Brazil's corn/soy strength and Russia's wheat edge, with
Argentina's dryness injecting some yield uncertainty but not yet derailing competitiveness via currency
plays. China's US bean pick-up offers a demand lifeline without fully closing FOB gaps. Absent new
global revisions or logistics snags, international prices key off exporter rivalry more than tightness. Next
movers likely tie to Argentina's mid-Jan rain (or lack thereof) and China's follow-on buying, which could
either underpin US basis firmness or let seasonal pressure creep in ahead of WASDE.

Weather & Crop Conditions

United States
With no formal Crop Progress reports in winter, private and state assessments indicate winter wheat
conditions broadly steady, with national ratings near 40 percent good-to-excellent. Kansas remains
relatively strong near 60 percent G/E, while Texas sits closer to 45 percent after scattered showers.
Dryness persists in western Kansas and Oklahoma, where recent rainfall remains 15–30 mm below
normal, and warmer-than-seasonal temperatures have limited soil recharge despite low winterkill risk.

The Mississippi River system stabilized post-holidays, with modest gauge improvements at Memphis and
Baton Rouge supporting more normal barge drafts. Freight rates on the Cairo–Memphis stretch eased
toward $19/ton, though levels remain below seasonal averages and logistics margins elevated. Looking
ahead, NOAA’s 6–10 day outlook favors above-normal temperatures and below-normal precipitation
across much of the Plains and Midwest, suggesting continued river support but ongoing dryness risk in
HRW areas.

Brazil
Rains picked up in the Center-West over the week, boosting early harvest and easing logistics in core
zones. IMEA estimates show Mato Grosso harvest around eight percent complete as of Jan 5, with
scattered showers of 20–50 mm providing relief but creating wet spots in northern areas that could slow
trucking. Forecast models from INMET and NOAA point to more precipitation through mid-January, with
40–70 mm expected in central Brazil, sufficient to maintain crop momentum if consistent. Southern states
(Paraná, Rio Grande do Sul) remained mostly favorable after earlier wetness, supporting vegetative
stages without major stress.

Argentina
Dryness intensified in southern provinces, with BCBA flagging moisture shortfalls in Buenos Aires and La
Pampa that could start impacting soybean and corn yields during pod-set. Wheat harvest pushed to
nearly 93 percent complete under mostly dry skies, locking in strong output near 27 MMT. Forecasts
indicate limited thunderstorms in central and northern zones, with 15–30 mm possible through mid-month,
but southern dryness may persist. No widespread crop deterioration was reported yet, but risks are
building.

Global
In the Northern Hemisphere, winter wheat establishment advanced without major issues. Russia and
Ukraine noted typical emergence under mild fall temps, with stable soils. Europe saw steady progress, as
FranceAgriMer held French wheat ratings near 68 percent good-to-excellent, and Germany/Poland
benefited from adequate moisture for stands. Australia's harvest wrapped up mixed, with persistent
dryness in Western Australia capping yields, while New South Wales and Queensland got timely rains
improving quality but not totals. No notable disruptions hit India, South Africa, or Canada.

So What?
US winter wheat is in decent shape with low winterkill odds, but dryness in the Plains keeps recharge in
focus. Stabilized rivers have eased export flow without slashing freight, though costs linger high, limiting
basis upside. Brazil's rain recovery is the core global driver: harvest is underway, but steady moisture is
key to firming yield outlooks. Argentina's emerging dryness adds a risk layer, while Russia, the EU, and
Australia stay supportive overall, making South America's mid-Jan weather the main swing factor for
oilseed and grain pricing.

Synthesis & Outlook

The first week of January brought the grain complex into a classic pre-WASDE holding pattern, with
USDA's "January bundle" looming and physical signals doing the interim work amid light holiday trading.
After months of leaning on inspections, basis, and flashes for direction, the market now awaits official
tweaks that could endorse or challenge the export-led tone in corn and the crush/export split in soy.
Futures chopped in tight ranges through Friday, March corn near $4.45/bu, March soybeans at
$10.67/bu, and March Chicago wheat around $5.20/bu, as positioning stayed cautious ahead of the data
dump. Spreads held supported but not tight, with corn Mar/May at about 50% of full carry, soybeans
Mar/May near 50%, and wheat Mar/May close to 40%. Gulf basis firmed in corn, processors kept soy bids
engaged, and volatility stayed low.

The vibe was one of quiet anticipation, with traders prepping for potential balance-sheet shifts without
committing big. Speculative activity was subdued, as managed money held net short in corn (around -53k
contracts) and wheat (near –71k), while adding lightly to soy longs (roughly +122k). CME open interest
ticked lower but reflected a commercially driven market focused on rolls and hedging over outright bets.
The resumption of full data flow sharpens visibility, but it also amps up risk: after drawing on inferred
demand from Gulf values, ethanol, and China headlines, the complex will recalibrate to quantifiable
numbers. If WASDE backs export upgrades or yield trims, the recent firmness could evolve into
conviction; if it underscores global supply, the stability may reveal itself as fragile.

Globally, Brazil held export dominance in soybeans and corn, with Russia steering wheat trade. Santos
soybeans quoted near $422/MT ($11.49/bu), about $10 under US Gulf, and corn FOB around $222/MT.
Early Mato Grosso harvest hit 8 percent, with rains aiding but subsoils still thin. Argentina stayed
competitive via currency weakness, with wheat output near record 27 MMT. Russian 12.5 percent wheat
offers firmed to $228–233/MT without slowing tenders, and China's state buying pushed US soy
purchases toward 10 MMT. The world sheet thus remains ample, but with Argentina dryness brewing and
eyes on US reports, the tone moved from post-harvest drift to watchful prep.

Bullish triggers: The upside hinges on WASDE affirming US demand strength. Robust inspections,
steady Gulf basis, and ethanol output above 1.1 mbpd could support corn export hikes, slimming carryout
modestly. For soy, crush upgrades plus persistent China flashes (near 10 MMT bought) could offset soft
shipments, especially if Brazil/Argentina weather slips, lifting spreads and pushing March futures toward
the mid-$10.60s. Wheat could extend if seedings surprise low or Russian duties constrain flow, fueling
short-covering. With funds short in corn/wheat and vol muted, even mild bullish leans might spark sharp

moves.

Bearish triggers: Downside looms if WASDE highlights abundance and static demand. Holding corn
exports flat, sticking with big South American crops, or bumping world stocks could widen carries. In soy,
fading China momentum amid Brazil's harvest pressure might refocus on global oversupply and soften
Gulf values. Wheat stays exposed if Russia keeps undercutting tenders, sidelining US offers. That path
could drag futures to range bottoms, with corn near $4.35, soy around $10.45, and wheat back to the
$5.00s. Watchlist (Jan 8 – Jan 14): USDA January reports (Jan 12): WASDE tweaks, final 2025 yields,
Dec 1 stocks, winter wheat seedings Export sales catch-up (Jan 8) and any new flashes to China or
Mexico Argentina dryness updates in Buenos Aires/La Pampa and mid-Jan rain verification Gulf
basis/logistics flow and barge freight follow-through Russia/Black Sea tender pace, EU competitiveness,
and BRL/USD shifts

In summary: With the big USDA event front and center, the grain market is pausing after a demand-
stabilized close to 2025, ready for data to either confirm or upend the narrative. Corn holds the steadiest
footing via exports and ethanol, soy balances crush against shipment lags, and wheat's firmness leans
more positional than fundamental. The next days will test if those underpinnings endure or shift under
fresh intel. For the moment, the complex sits poised but patient, eyeing validation of recent hints.

Ethanol for America Act

The major barrier to a higher Renewable Fuel Standard (RFS) is the fuel versus food debate. Now is the
perfect time to move to E15. Corn prices are extremely low, and the latest USDA WASDE reported a record
crop and a record crop expansion.

USDA WASDE forecast corn production at 16.7 billion bushels, with the yield at 188.8 bushels per acre and
ending stocks at 2.1 billion. The season-average corn price dropped 30 cents to $3.90 a bushel. Most
importantly, 202526 crop year is expected to have 1.8 billion more bushels than 2024/25.

With current ethanol production consuming 5.6 billion bushels, a 10 percent increase in consumption would
consume an additional 560 million bushels. The reality is that year around E15 would add approximately
50 million bushels and regulatory streamlining would add another 50 million bushels. At some point over
the next ten years, the additional corn consumption could be 2.8 billion bushels minus lower fuel
consumption resulting from electric vehicles. The ethanol corn consumption increase is likely to be more
of a S curve as new compacity comes online.

With Brazil and the US corn and soybean production increasing, 100 million bushels of ethanol corn
consumption will barely move the needle. The ethanol production capacity expansion that would be three
to four years from now is when the corn consumption impact to the corn market would be felt.

=== REPORT END ===

Corn – Execution Strength Offsets a Firmer Board

Corn finished fractionally higher with March near $4.27, but
the bigger move was under the hood: inspections surged to
2.01 MMT for the week ending Feb 19 and Gulf basis firmed
to roughly +92H. Even with that demand signal, the March–
May spread widened to about an 11-cent carry (roughly 72

enough to prevent the board from slipping, even as the curve continues to pay storage.

Wheat – Carry Rebuilds as Competition Caps Upside
Chicago SRW futures recovered modestly, with March settling near $5.63 1/2, up 4 cents from February
19, while the March–May spread widened modestly toward a 10 cent carry, roughly 61 percent of full
carry. Structure remains in carry and consistent with comfortable nearby supply, but it is not signaling a
market fully rewarding storage. Weekly export inspections totaled 0.54 MMT for the week ending
February 19, keeping cumulative shipments modestly ahead of last year, but global competition remains
persistent. Russian 12.5 percent FOB values near $235/MT continue to anchor world pricing, limiting U.S.
export competitiveness at the margin. Gulf and interior basis levels were largely steady to slightly softer,
reflecting adequate coverage rather than urgency. Managed money maintains a sizeable net short
position, reinforcing a defensive tone. Without a fresh production shock or a shift in export
competitiveness, rallies remain constrained and structure continues to reflect a heavy balance sheet.

Soybeans – Domestic Demand Supports, but Brazil Caps the Board
Soybeans softened modestly this week, with March settling near $11.34 1/4, down 6 3/4 cents from
February 19, while the March–May spread widened to roughly a 16 cent carry, approximately 70 percent
of full carry. The wider carry structure reinforces comfortable nearby coverage and reflects seasonal
pressure as Brazil dominates the export window. Weekly inspections totaled 0.67 MMT for the week
ending February 19, below earlier peaks, and cumulative exports remain well behind last year as Brazil
controls early-season flow. January NOPA crush at 221.56 Mbu continues to underscore strong domestic
processing demand, though soybean oil stocks remain elevated. CIF Gulf basis softened slightly to +95H,
and interior processor bids eased, reflecting limited urgency amid accelerating South American harvest
flow. ANEC projects February soybean exports near 11.4 MMT, reinforcing Brazil’s seasonal pricing
ceiling. Managed money is net long in soybeans (roughly +92,000 contracts), adding a more constructive

acceleration. Broader risk sentiment remained mixed, with macro flows influencing tone more than
structure as grain markets continue to trade primarily on internal balance sheet dynamics.

Weather – Argentina Reenters Focus as U.S. Turns Warmer

NOAA’s 6–10 and 8–14 day outlooks favor above-normal temperatures across much of the central U.S.,
with a more active precipitation corridor extending into the eastern Midwest. In Brazil, widespread rainfall
continues to support safrinha development while occasionally slowing harvest logistics. Argentina’s
February rainfall remains below normal across key producing areas, and second-crop soybeans entering
critical grain-fill stages face renewed moisture stress, though official production estimates remain
unchanged at 48.5 MMT. With global weather largely quiet elsewhere, Argentina remains the primary
volatility variable.

Whether corn inspections stay above two MMT

Corn Market Deep Dive
Futures & Spreads: Corn futures firmed slightly this week, with March settling near $4.27 3/4, up 2 cents
from February 19. While flat price moved only marginally, the March–May spread widened to roughly an
11 cent carry, consistent with approximately 72 percent of full carry under the two-month storage
framework. The market remains comfortably in carry, signaling adequate nearby supply and stable
commercial coverage rather than tightening. Volatility remains contained, and price action continues to
reflect consolidation rather than directional momentum. The curve is stable even as the market continues
to weigh strong execution against an otherwise ample global supply backdrop.

Funds & Positioning: Managed Money holds a net short position near 42,313 contracts as of the
February 17 report, reflecting a defensive but not extreme speculative stance. The shift was driven
primarily by modest additions to gross shorts rather than aggressive long liquidation. While the net short
is meaningful, it remains below prior cycle extremes. Importantly, spreads have not collapsed in response
to the heavier positioning, suggesting that speculative pressure is being absorbed without structural
deterioration. Funds appear positioned defensively but not aggressively pressing the downside.

Cash & Basis: Cash markets strengthened modestly. CIF Gulf corn improved to +92H, reflecting firm
export demand and minor freight friction tied to draft constraints along portions of the river system. Interior

river bids firmed as well, while processor bids held steady. Basis is not surging, but the tone is
constructive. The physical market continues to signal steady movement rather than distress, with logistics
normalized enough to prevent bottlenecks but tight enough to keep freight supportive. Nearby commercial
flow remains orderly.

Export Sales & Inspections: Export inspections for the week ending February 19 accelerated to
approximately 2.01 MMT, a clear step higher from the prior week and reinforcing strong execution.
Cumulative shipments remain comfortably ahead of last year and continue tracking in line with USDA’s
export projection. Weekly export sales remain active, with forward commitments sustaining program
visibility into late winter. Shipments represent a large share of USDA’s export projection, underscoring
that demand performance remains the primary stabilizing force in the balance sheet. The export channel
continues to offset softer flat price dynamics.

Global Context: Brazil’s February corn export pace, while active, is running below last year’s level,
reducing immediate competitive pressure at the margin. Argentine production estimates remain steady,
though recent rainfall variability introduces localized uncertainty. Black Sea corn offers remain competitive
but not decisively undercutting U.S. Gulf into core Western Hemisphere destinations. Mexico continues to
anchor U.S. demand, and Latin American flows remain consistent. Global supply remains ample, but U.S.
origin retains competitive footing in key channels.

So What? Corn’s structure remains more stable than the board implies. Flat price has softened, and
speculative shorts remain meaningful, yet spreads hold near around 72 percent of full carry and basis has
firmed modestly. Export execution remains the anchor. Absent a deterioration in shipments or a renewed
surge in global competition, the market continues to trade within a carry-defined range. For now, structure
is steady, positioning is defensive, and downside momentum remains contained by physical flow.

Soybean Market Deep Dive

Futures & Spreads: Soybeans softened modestly this week, with March settling near $11.34. The
March–May spread widened to roughly a 16 cent carry, near 70 percent of full carry. The structure
remains clearly in contango but not at full storage economics, suggesting adequate nearby supply without
signaling pressure. Price action reflects stabilization rather than momentum, with the front end supported
but not urgent.

Products & Crush: Domestic processing continues to anchor the complex. January NOPA crush at
221.56 million bushels reaffirmed historically strong throughput, even as soybean oil stocks remain
elevated near 1.9 billion pounds. Crush margins continue to lean meal-driven, with soybean meal demand
providing the bulk of processing support. The product complex has not accelerated meaningfully, but
neither has it deteriorated. Domestic use remains the primary structural pillar for U.S. soybeans as export
competitiveness fluctuates.

Cash & Basis: Cash markets softened slightly at the Gulf, with CIF values easing toward +95H as
Brazilian harvest pressure builds. Interior processor bids slipped modestly as well, reflecting reduced
urgency amid improving South American logistics. The nearby market remains supported by crush pull
rather than export-driven tightening. Basis tone is stable but not strengthening, underscoring the
divergence between steady domestic demand and heavier global supply availability.

Funds & Positioning: Managed Money holds a net long position near 92,000 contracts as of the
February 17 report. Positioning is constructive rather than defensive, which helps explain why breaks
have been absorbed even as Brazil dominates the export window. The tradeoff is that length introduces
liquidation risk if South American flow stays smooth and demand fails to re-accelerate. In short, soybeans

are not set up like a crowded short market; they are supported, but more vulnerable to sentiment shifts if
the fundamental tape softens.

Export Sales & Inspections: Weekly export inspections for the week ending February 19 totaled
approximately 0.67 MMT, a noticeable step down from the prior week. Cumulative inspections remain
materially behind last year’s pace, reflecting Brazil’s dominant early-season window. ANEC projects
February soybean exports near 11.4 MMT, well above last year’s February pace and reinforcing South
America’s pricing influence. U.S. export flow remains functional but secondary in the global trade rotation.
The export channel continues to operate, but it is not the primary demand driver at this stage of the
marketing year.

Global Context: Brazil’s soybean production estimate remains near 178 MMT, with harvest advancing
under broadly favorable conditions across central regions. FOB Santos values remain competitive into
Asia, effectively setting the ceiling for U.S. pricing absent a freight or currency shift. Argentina’s soybean
outlook holds near 48.5 MMT, though renewed dryness across second-crop areas reintroduces weather
sensitivity. For now, Brazil defines the global supply narrative, while Argentina remains the volatility
variable rather than a confirmed disruption.

So What? Soybeans remain supported by domestic processing but capped by Brazil’s seasonal export
dominance. The board finished slightly lower on the week and the March–May spread widened versus
last week, reinforcing a carry-driven tone as nearby coverage stays comfortable and South America sets
the external ceiling. Export flow remains functional but secondary, while crush continues to provide the
structural floor. With managed money net long near 92,000 contracts, the complex is more sensitive to an
upside surprise in demand or South American weather than to a liquidation-driven break. Absent a
material shift in export competitiveness or a realized supply disruption in South America, price discovery
is likely to remain anchored by carry economics and the pace of Brazilian flow.

Wheat Market Deep Dive

Futures & Spreads: Wheat futures recovered modestly this week, with March Chicago SRW settling
near $5.63 1/2, up 4 cents from February 19. The March–May spread widened toward a 10 cent carry,
roughly 61 percent of full carry, reinforcing comfortable nearby supply without signaling a market fully
rewarding storage. The rally continues to read as more technical than fundamentally driven, with structure
remaining in carry and consistent with adequate coverage. Kansas City and Minneapolis followed a
similar tone, with no meaningful divergence within the complex.

Cash & Basis: Cash markets were largely steady. CIF Gulf SRW basis softened modestly toward +65H,
while interior levels showed little evidence of tightening. Plains HRW basis remained stable near -20H
despite localized weather variability, and Pacific Northwest values held firm, reflecting routine coverage
rather than scarcity. The absence of basis acceleration alongside widening carry underscores a market
that is adequately supplied. Physical flow remains orderly, and commercial coverage appears sufficient.

Export Sales & Inspections: Weekly export inspections for the week ending February 19 increased to
approximately 0.54 MMT, a firmer pace relative to the prior week. Export sales remain routine but not
accelerating, and cumulative shipments continue to track broadly in line with USDA projections. While
export flow is functioning, volumes remain insufficient to materially alter the broader balance sheet
narrative. The program is steady, but not dynamic.

Global Context: Russian 12.5 percent FOB values firmed toward $235/MT, offering modest support to

global pricing, and Euronext wheat rebounded nearly 3 percent on the week. However, Black Sea supply
remains structurally available, and competitive offers into North Africa and the Middle East continue to
anchor world values. Ukrainian exports remain active through established channels, and European origin
has re-entered select tenders. No logistical or policy disruption has emerged to materially tighten
availability. Global supply remains ample, even if short-term pricing has stabilized.

Funds & Positioning: Managed Money maintains a substantial net short position near 69,567 contracts
as of the February 17 report. While exposure is below prior extremes, it remains meaningful and
continues to reflect a defensive stance. The recent price recovery has not triggered meaningful short
covering, suggesting that speculative conviction remains intact. Positioning offers latent upside risk, but
absent a structural catalyst, funds appear comfortable maintaining exposure.

So What? Wheat’s structure has grown heavier even as flat price improved. The expansion deeper into
carry reinforces comfortable coverage, though still short of full storage economics. Export flow has
strengthened modestly, and global pricing has stabilized, but neither development has tightened the
balance sheet meaningfully. With funds still meaningfully short and spreads rewarding storage, the
market remains rangebound. Until a material production shock or export disruption emerges, wheat is
likely to trade within carry-defined boundaries rather than trend decisively higher.

US Cash Market & Basis

Corn: Corn basis strengthened modestly across primary corridors despite softer futures. CIF Gulf values
improved to approximately +92H, supported by strong export inspections and slight freight friction tied to
draft constraints along the Illinois River system. Interior river bids firmed toward +24H in St. Louis,
reflecting continued export execution and limited farmer selling. Midwest ethanol plant bids remained
stable, reinforcing steady grind demand. While flat price drifted lower on the board, the physical market
tone was constructive. The nearby cash market signals orderly movement rather than liquidation
pressure, with export demand continuing to underpin basis strength.

Soybeans: Soybean basis softened slightly at the Gulf as Brazilian harvest momentum increased. CIF
Gulf values eased toward +95H, reflecting steady but less urgent export demand relative to prior weeks.
Interior processor bids across the Midwest slipped modestly, indicating adequate nearby coverage amid
strong January crush but rising South American competition. River logistics remain normalized, reducing
transportation friction. The cash market remains supported by domestic processing, yet lacks the
tightening tone seen earlier in the month. Basis stability persists, though the directional bias has softened
marginally.

Wheat: Wheat cash markets were broadly steady. CIF Gulf SRW basis held near +65H, while HRW basis
across the Plains remained anchored near -20H. Pacific Northwest values were unchanged, reflecting
routine export coverage rather than incremental demand acceleration. Despite modest improvement in
futures, basis has not strengthened meaningfully, underscoring comfortable nearby availability. The
widening calendar spreads reinforce the view that inventory coverage is adequate. Physical wheat
markets continue to signal sufficiency rather than scarcity.

So What? Cash markets continue to trade with greater stability than flat price suggests. Corn basis has
firmed alongside strong export flow, soybeans show mild softening as Brazil accelerates harvest, and
wheat remains structurally comfortable. Logistics are functioning efficiently, and no corridor signals stress.
Physical markets remain balanced, with corn showing relative strength, soybeans steady but capped, and
wheat reflecting ample supply.

International Cash & Trade Flows

Brazil: Brazil’s export program continues to define global oilseed flow as harvest advances across central
regions. ANEC projects February soybean shipments near 11.4 MMT, materially above last year’s pace
and reinforcing South America’s seasonal dominance. FOB Santos values near $424/MT remain
competitive into Asia, effectively setting the ceiling for U.S. export participation. Corn exports are active
but trail last year’s February level, reflecting the seasonal transition between crop cycles. Interior basis
firmness and rising B3 values highlight domestic retention even as export channels remain functional.
Brazil remains the primary price-setter in soybeans and a stabilizing influence in global corn trade

Argentina: Argentina remains the principal weather-sensitive variable. Recent rainfall has been uneven,
with dryness returning across portions of the soybean belt entering critical grain-fill stages. Official
production estimates remain unchanged at 48.5 MMT for soybeans, though localized stress sustains
volatility risk. FOB offers remain present but lack the scale to materially disrupt Brazil’s leadership. Corn
harvest progress is underway in early-planted areas, with yield variability emerging regionally. Argentina’s
export flow continues, but its influence remains secondary unless weather conditions deteriorate further.

China: China’s import posture remains measured. Brazilian soybeans continue to dominate forward
coverage due to price advantage and harvest availability, while U.S. origin remains opportunistic rather
than primary. Dalian corn and soymeal futures softened modestly this week, reflecting adequate domestic
supply and limited feed margin expansion. No large new U.S. soybean purchase announcements
emerged during the window. China’s demand remains tactical and margin-sensitive rather than
expansionary.

EU / Black Sea: Black Sea origin continues to anchor global wheat pricing, though FOB values have
firmed modestly near $235/MT for Russian 12.5% wheat. Ukrainian corn remains competitively offered
near $232/MT, maintaining pressure on Atlantic Basin pricing. Euronext wheat rebounded nearly 3
percent this week, suggesting short-covering and stabilization rather than structural tightening. EU export
pace remains below last year’s level, and no major logistical disruption has emerged. Black Sea supply
continues to cap rallies even as pricing stabilizes.

Other Regions: Australia continues to move wheat into Asian markets following a solid harvest, while
Canadian spring wheat exports proceed in line with seasonal norms. No material trade restrictions, quota
shifts, or policy disruptions have emerged across secondary exporters. The broader international
environment remains stable and well supplied.

So What? Brazil is still the price-setter in soybeans, but this week the market’s attention shifted from
“crop size” to “how fast it can physically move,” with ANEC implying another front-loaded February
shipment pace. Argentina, meanwhile, is no longer just a volatility headline, deterioration is starting to
show up in the way estimates and ratings are being discussed. For wheat, Black Sea FOB values have
firmed a touch, yet they still define the ceiling for everyone else.

Key Fundamentals & Reports (Feb 12 – Feb 18)

Export Inspections (USDA FGIS – week ending Feb 19): Corn export inspections accelerated to
2,005,050 metric tons, lifting cumulative 2025/26 shipments to approximately 37.7 million metric tons,
maintaining a pace well ahead of last year and broadly consistent with USDA’s export projection. The
improvement reinforces corn’s position as the strongest executing program within the complex. Soybean
inspections totaled 669,865 metric tons, a noticeable slowdown from the prior week, with cumulative

shipments continuing to trail last season materially as Brazil dominates the early marketing window.
Wheat inspections reached 535,113 metric tons, a firmer weekly pace that keeps cumulative shipments
modestly ahead of last year but still insufficient to materially alter the balance sheet narrative.

Export Sales (USDA FAS – latest through week ending Feb 12): Weekly net sales reflected continued
forward engagement. Corn net sales totaled 1,482,300 metric tons, sustaining active commitment flow.
Soybean net sales reached 412,600 metric tons, steady but not indicative of acceleration amid strong
Brazilian competition. Wheat net sales totaled 348,200 metric tons, consistent with routine demand rather
than expansion. Cumulative commitments for corn now represent roughly three-quarters of USDA’s
export forecast, while soybeans and wheat remain broadly aligned with seasonal expectations. No
significant flash sales emerged during the window.

Ethanol Production & Stocks: Ethanol production for the week ending February 13 increased to 1.118
million barrels per day, up from the prior week’s 1.110 million. Stocks remain elevated near seasonal
highs, signaling steady grind but no acceleration in blending demand. Production remains supportive for
corn usage, though inventory levels temper enthusiasm for additional near-term upside in ethanol-driven
demand.

NOPA Soybean Crush: January NOPA crush at 221.56 million bushels remains the structural anchor for
soybeans, reaffirming historically strong domestic processing demand. Soybean oil stocks near 1.90
billion pounds continue to reflect ample product availability, reinforcing that crush margins remain meal-
driven. Domestic demand remains intact and continues to offset export weakness.

CFTC Positioning (as of February 17, released February 21): Managed Money positioning remains
broadly defensive across the grain complex. Corn funds held a net short of 42,313 contracts, soybeans a
net long of roughly 92,000, and Chicago SRW wheat a net short of 69,567. The key takeaway is that
speculative exposure is still positioned to fade rallies in corn and wheat, while soybeans carry a more
constructive long, but it is not stretched enough to imply forced liquidation without a catalyst. With
spreads holding in carry and cash markets largely steady, positioning looks more like “patience and
optionality” than a crowded bet. In practice, that keeps the complex vulnerable to event-driven volatility,
weather escalation in Argentina, a logistics hiccup in Brazil, or a shift in export competitiveness, but it
does not, by itself, signal a market on the edge of a structural unwind.

So What? The complex is getting more uneven. Corn is the only leg clearly outperforming on execution
(2.01 MMT inspections), yet funds are still leaning short. Soybeans keep leaning on crush for a floor, but
weekly inspections cooled again, which reinforces the seasonal Brazil cap. Wheat flow improved, but
carry rebuilt and basis stayed calm, a combination that usually reads as “stabilization,” not “tightening.

International Reports

Brazil: Brazil’s February production profile remains intact, with CONAB maintaining soybean output near
177.98 million metric tons and total corn production near 138.45 million metric tons. The safrinha crop
continues to account for the majority of projected corn supply, reinforcing expectations for substantial
second-half export availability. While harvest advances steadily across central regions, no official
downward revisions have emerged. The size of Brazil’s soybean crop continues to frame global oilseed
balance sheets, anchoring expectations for abundant exportable surplus into mid-year. Structurally, Brazil
remains the dominant supply reference point rather than a volatility driver at this stage.

Argentina: Argentina’s official production outlook remains stable, with soybeans near 48.5 million metric
tons and corn near 57 million metric tons. Rainfall variability persists across key regions, particularly in
second-crop soybeans entering critical reproductive stages, but no formal downgrade has been issued.

Farmer marketing remains measured amid currency dynamics and macro uncertainty, limiting aggressive
forward sales. While agronomic risk remains elevated relative to Brazil, it has not yet translated into a
measurable supply contraction. Argentina remains the primary weather-sensitive variable, but not yet a
confirmed structural shift.

China: China’s demand posture remains pragmatic. Routine soybean imports continue, largely supplied
by Brazil’s advancing harvest. No material policy changes or reserve interventions were announced
during the window. Domestic futures pricing suggests stable but not expanding crush economics, and
feed margins remain sensitive to livestock profitability. China’s role remains supportive but selective,
engaging opportunistically rather than driving expansionary demand.

EU / Black Sea: The broader European and Black Sea production outlook remains stable. No significant
revisions to wheat or coarse grain output were announced during the reporting window. Export corridors
from the Black Sea remain functional, and EU export pace continues to trail last year’s levels. The
absence of logistical disruption or policy intervention reinforces a supply environment defined by
continuity rather than constraint. At present, structural availability remains the dominant feature across
the region.

So What? International balance sheets remain defined by scale rather than shock. Brazil’s large soybean
and corn production frames global supply expectations, Argentina’s weather risk remains monitored but
not escalated, and China’s import behavior remains tactical. Across major exporting regions, no new
production revision or policy shift has emerged to materially alter global availability. The international
backdrop continues to reflect abundance, with volatility centered more on weather potential than realized
disruption.

Weather & Crop Conditions

United States
U.S. weather remains largely neutral from a production standpoint at this stage of the calendar. The
USDA national Crop Progress report remains on seasonal pause, with full updates resuming in April,
leaving the market to rely on state-level updates and winterkill monitoring. Snow cover across key HRW
wheat areas in Kansas, Oklahoma, and Colorado remains adequate, limiting immediate freeze risk
despite intermittent cold. NOAA’s 6–10 and 8–14 day outlooks continue to favor above-normal
temperatures across much of the central U.S., reducing logistical friction along river systems and allowing
freight conditions to function more consistently relative to earlier winter disruptions. With winter wheat still
in dormancy and no active fieldwork underway, U.S. weather remains an operational variable rather than
a balance-sheet catalyst.

Brazil
Brazil’s weather continues to reinforce production expectations, though logistics are beginning to feel the
pressure of record volume. National soybean harvest has advanced to roughly 25 percent complete, with
Mato Grosso leading near 47 percent and reporting strong early yields that sustain aggregate production
expectations near 178–180 million metric tons. Soil moisture remains favorable for safrinha corn
establishment across the Center-West, preserving the second-crop outlook. However, frequent rainfall in
Mato Grosso and Pará has narrowed loading windows along the Arco Norte corridor, extending barge
wait times along the Tapajós system. In the South and Southeast, heavy rainfall in Paraná and Santa
Catarina has introduced localized flood risk, potentially slowing loading operations at Paranaguá and
Santos. Logistics remain functional, but the system is stretched, and the risk has shifted from theoretical
to operational as export flows accelerate.

Argentina
Argentina has moved from monitored stress to measurable deterioration. Soybean conditions have fallen

to 40 percent rated Good/Excellent, down sharply in recent weeks, with the Rosario Grain Exchange
projecting production near 48 million metric tons and warning that losses in the most affected zones could
reach 20–60 percent. Corn conditions stand at 44 percent Good/Excellent, prompting the Buenos Aires
Grain Exchange to reduce its production estimate to 57 million metric tons amid persistent dryness and
thermal stress. Rainfall coverage has been uneven and insufficient to fully stabilize second-crop
soybeans entering grain fill, while late-planted corn remains highly sensitive to short-term precipitation.
Unlike Brazil, where weather variability has not altered aggregate expectations, Argentina’s production
profile is actively being trimmed, keeping downside supply risk elevated.

Global
Outside South America, global weather remains largely stable. Winter wheat across Russia, Ukraine, and
the European Union remains dormant under broadly normal conditions with no significant winterkill threat
reported. Black Sea logistics continue without disruption, and no secondary exporter has introduced
meaningful supply risk. As a result, global weather volatility remains geographically concentrated rather
than systemic.

So What?
Weather risk is no longer evenly distributed. Brazil continues to support global supply with strong harvest
progress despite tightening logistics, the United States remains neutral during dormancy, and Argentina
has become the primary volatility node with declining crop ratings and downward revisions underway.
Unless Argentine conditions deteriorate further or Brazilian port congestion materially disrupts export flow,
weather remains a developing driver rather than a confirmed balance-sheet shock, but the asymmetry
has increased meaningfully compared to prior weeks.

Synthesis & Outlook

The defining feature of this week was divergence beneath stable flat price. Corn finished modestly higher
with March near $4.27, soybeans edged lower with March near $11.34, and Chicago SRW wheat firmed
to roughly $5.63, yet the more important signal came from structure and flow rather than the closes
themselves. Calendar spreads across the complex widened week over week and remain in carry: corn’s
March–May spread sits near an 11-cent carry (roughly 72 percent of full carry), soybeans near a 16-cent
carry (about 70 percent), and wheat near a 10-cent carry (about 61 percent). That structure continues to
reflect adequate coverage and limited urgency, even as cash and export execution send more nuanced
signals underneath the board.

Corn continues to behave like a market in equilibrium, but this week the export channel did more of the
heavy lifting. Inspections surged to about 2.01 MMT for the week ending February 19, reinforcing a
shipment pace that remains comfortably ahead of last year and aligned with USDA’s forecast. Gulf basis
firmed toward +92H and interior river bids strengthened modestly, suggesting that physical demand
remains intact even as the curve stays comfortably in carry. Funds remain net short near 42,000
contracts, keeping a defensive speculative overlay in place, but the combination of stronger inspections
and firmer cash tone helped prevent the board from slipping despite a market structure that still pays
storage.

Soybeans remain bifurcated between domestic strength and global weight. January NOPA crush at
221.56 million bushels continues to validate the demand floor, even as soybean oil stocks remain
elevated and keep the product complex from driving a broader revaluation. On the export side,
inspections cooled to about 0.67 MMT for the week ending February 19 and cumulative shipments remain
materially behind last year as Brazil dominates the seasonal export window. With ANEC projecting
February soybean exports near 11.4 MMT and Brazil’s harvest advancing, the market continues to treat
South America as the pricing ceiling near-term. The March–May spread remains in a carry-driven posture
near 70 percent of full carry, consistent with comfortable nearby coverage. With managed money net long

near 92,000 contracts, the complex is positioned to react to either a weather-driven surprise in Argentina
or a clearer demand signal, but absent that, the export rotation keeps upside constrained even as crush
provides support.

Wheat remains the heaviest leg of the complex despite this week’s modest recovery. Export inspections
improved to roughly 0.54 MMT, but the market’s structure moved deeper into carry, with the March–May
spread widening toward 10 cents. That combination, firmer futures alongside a heavier carry, reads as
stabilization rather than tightening. Black Sea pricing remains the global anchor, with Russian 12.5
percent FOB values near $235/MT continuing to cap U.S. competitiveness into price-sensitive
destinations. Basis was steady to slightly softer across Gulf and interior markets, consistent with
adequate coverage rather than urgency. Managed money remains meaningfully net short near 69,500
contracts, creating latent upside risk through short covering, but that risk still requires a catalyst beyond
routine weekly flow.

Weather risk is increasingly asymmetrical. Brazil remains abundant but operationally stretched as harvest
advances and export systems absorb large volumes, while U.S. winter conditions remain neutral during
dormancy and logistics have improved relative to earlier winter constraints. Argentina, however, has re-
entered focus with renewed moisture stress during critical reproductive stages, and crop conditions have
deteriorated relative to earlier weeks. For now, the market is acknowledging that risk without fully
repricing it into balance sheets, but the distribution of weather risk has shifted meaningfully compared
with mid-February.

Bullish triggers: Upside momentum would likely require confirmation that Argentine deterioration is
balance-sheet relevant rather than localized, particularly if soybean production slips materially below
current estimates or corn losses expand beyond the latest trims. A move from “stretched” to disruptive in
Brazilian export logistics could tighten the near-term pipeline and force a short-term repricing of
availability. Additional support could come from continued strength in U.S. corn export execution, an
acceleration in ethanol grind beyond recent levels, or any disruption in Black Sea logistics that shifts
wheat competitiveness and triggers short covering in a structurally short market.

Bearish triggers: Downside pressure would build under a smooth, uninterrupted Brazilian harvest and
export program that reinforces South America as the seasonal pricing ceiling for soybeans and limits U.S.
participation into Asia. A meaningful stabilization in Argentine rainfall and crop conditions would deflate
the weather premium that is beginning to re-enter the narrative. Domestically, softening U.S. interior basis
would signal weaker nearby demand, while the complex remains vulnerable to renewed pressure if
spreads continue to widen and commercial coverage stays comfortable. In wheat, persistent Black Sea
competitiveness and the absence of a supply shock would keep structure rewarding storage and continue
to cap rallies.

In summary: the grain complex remains structurally comfortable but increasingly uneven beneath the
surface. Corn is outperforming on execution and cash tone even as the curve stays in carry, soybeans
remain supported by domestic processing but capped by Brazil’s export dominance, and wheat remains
rangebound under heavy global availability despite a modest rebound. Until supply is materially disrupted
or demand accelerates in a measurable way, price discovery is likely to remain governed by carry
economics, cash-market signals, and incremental flow rather than conviction-driven trend.

Ethanol for America Act

The major barrier to a higher Renewable Fuel Standard (RFS) is the fuel versus food debate. Now is the
perfect time to move to E15. Corn prices are extremely low, and the latest USDA WASDE reported a record
crop and a record crop expansion.

Executive Summary

Corn – Carry Holds as Exports Execute, Board Drifts
Corn futures softened through the holiday-shortened week,
with March settling near $4.27, down about five cents. The

of full carry), keeping the front end stable despite weaker flat
price action. Export inspections for the week ending
February 12 totaled about 1.49 MMT, with cumulative
shipments still running ahead of last year and broadly
aligned with USDA projections. Cash markets stayed firm,

contracts, well below prior extremes and no longer expanding. With global supply ample, steady
execution and stable spreads continue to limit downside momentum in the nearby structure.

Wheat – Heavy Balance Sheet Caps Rallies Despite Solid Flow
Chicago SRW futures weakened into midweek, with March settling near $5.47 under persistent global
competition. The March–May spread held near a 4.75 cent carry, well below full carry and consistent with
limited commercial urgency. Weekly export inspections totaled about 0.38 MMT, keeping shipments
modestly ahead of last year. Gulf basis and interior bids were largely unchanged, signaling adequate
coverage. Managed money remains heavily net short near 85,832 contracts, reinforcing a defensive tone.
With Russian FOB wheat still competitively offered and no fresh production shock emerging, rallies
remain constrained absent a shift in export competitiveness.

Soybeans – Crush Support Persists as Brazil Sets the Ceiling
Soybeans traded modestly lower, with March settling near $11.33. The March–May spread held near a 16
cent carry (72 percent of full carry), reflecting comfortable nearby supply alongside steady domestic pull.
Weekly inspections totaled 1.20 MMT for the week ending February 12, but cumulative shipments stand
near 24.35 MMT, roughly 32 percent below last year, highlighting Brazil’s dominant early-season export
window and limited U.S. participation in China. January NOPA crush reached 221.6 Mbu, a monthly
record, reinforcing domestic demand even as soybean oil stocks remain elevated. CIF Gulf held near
$12.36–$12.37 and interior processor bids stayed firm. Managed money remains net long near 115,896
contracts, elevated and vulnerable to liquidation if export momentum fails to improve. With South
American supply large, U.S. demand continues to anchor the front end, leaving the complex supported
domestically but capped externally.

Macro / Policy – Dollar Firms as Risk Tone Softens

The U.S. dollar firmed modestly through the week, adding incremental pressure to export competitiveness
at the margin, particularly for wheat and soybeans facing direct competition from lower-cost origins. Crude
oil edged higher toward the mid-$63 per barrel range, offering mild support to biofuel-linked demand but
not enough to materially shift margins. Equity markets softened in a broader risk-off tone tied more to
macro positioning than commodity-specific catalysts. Ethanol production remained firm at 1.08 million
barrels per day for the week ending February 6, while stocks increased to approximately 25.25 million
barrels, indicating steady grind but no acceleration in blending demand. Interest rate expectations and
currency stability remain background variables, but for now macro flows are influencing sentiment rather
than structure. Grain markets continue to trade primarily on internal balance sheet dynamics rather than
external policy shocks.

Weather – US Logistics Improve; Argentina Remains the Volatility Point

U.S. winter wheat remains in dormancy with no new national Crop Progress update issued during
February. State-level conditions across the Plains remain generally steady, and recent snow cover has
limited winterkill concerns. River logistics have improved from earlier winter constraints, reducing freight
friction without materially altering basis. In Brazil, harvest progress continues under broadly favorable
central weather, reinforcing expectations for a soybean crop near 177.98 million metric tons and
sustaining export momentum. Southern Brazil retains localized moisture variability, but no national
production revision has emerged. Argentina remains the primary weather-sensitive region, with soil
moisture deficits persisting in key soybean and corn areas. While localized stress remains evident, no
new official downgrade occurred during the reporting window. Outside South America, global weather
conditions remain quiet, leaving Argentina as the central volatility variable rather than an active disruption.

What’s Next (Feb 11 – Feb 18):

Export inspections: does corn execution stay ahead of last year

Export sales: any confirmation of soybean demand amid Brazil competition

CFTC positioning: stabilization vs renewed exposure

Brazil harvest progress and vessel lineups

EIA ethanol production and stocks

Dollar direction and macro risk tone
