Corn Market Deep Dive
Futures & Spreads: Corn drifted modestly lower through the holiday-shortened week, with March futures
settling near $4.27, down roughly five cents from the prior close. The March–May spread widened slightly
to around a 9.25-cent carry, representing roughly 61 percent of full carry under the Mar–May framework.
While the curve remains in carry, it is not pressing toward maximum storage economics. The structure
continues to reflect adequate supply but steady nearby demand, with no evidence of stress in either
direction. Volatility remained muted and price action contained, reinforcing a market that is consolidating
rather than trending.

Funds & Positioning: Managed Money maintained a net short near 57,493 contracts as of the February
10 CFTC report. The position remains meaningful but far from historical extremes and, importantly, is no
longer expanding. Gross shorts have stabilized while longs have edged modestly higher, suggesting a
defensive posture rather than an aggressive bearish build. With futures failing to break meaningfully lower
and spreads holding their ground, speculative pressure appears controlled. Absent a macro catalyst or
deterioration in export pace, funds appear comfortable maintaining exposure without increasing
conviction.

Cash & Basis: Cash markets held relatively steady. CIF Gulf values remained firm in the $5.26–$5.29
range, while interior river bids showed modest regional variation but no broad softening. Freight
conditions have improved from peak winter disruption, though draft limitations continue to influence barge
efficiency. Basis strength is not accelerating, but neither is it eroding. The nearby physical market remains
stable, signaling continued execution rather than liquidation.

Export Sales & Inspections: Export inspections for the week ending February 12 totaled approximately
1.49 million metric tons, maintaining a shipment pace ahead of last year and broadly consistent with
USDA expectations. Weekly export sales for the week ending February 5 totaled 2,105,500 metric tons,
reinforcing that forward commitments remain active. Cumulative shipments now account for roughly 60
percent of the USDA export forecast, indicating that the program remains on pace despite softer flat price
action. The export channel continues to provide structural support even as futures consolidate.

Global Context: Brazil’s export program remains active, though forward pressure has moderated relative
to peak harvest expectations. Argentine corn production remains stable on official estimates, with no new
material downgrade during the week. Russian and Ukrainian corn offers remain present but not
aggressively undercutting U.S. Gulf values into core destinations. U.S. origin retains a competitive
position into Mexico and select Latin American markets, though Brazil continues to anchor broader global
pricing expectations.

So What? Corn remains structurally stable. The board has softened, but spreads are holding near 60
percent of full carry, basis is steady, and export execution remains intact. Funds are short but not
pressing. Without a supply shock or demand deterioration, the market continues to oscillate within a
range defined more by carry economics than directional conviction. For now, structure remains firmer
than flat price suggests.

Soybean Market Deep Dive

Futures & Spreads: Soybeans softened modestly through the week, with March futures settling near
$11.33, drifting lower in holiday-thinned trade. The March–May spread held near a 16 cent carry,
maintaining a clear contango structure that continues to reflect adequate nearby supply. On a full-carry
framework, the spread sits near 72 percent of full carry, signaling neither tightening stress nor
burdensome pressure. The curve remains stable, and price action suggests consolidation rather than
directional conviction. While flat price lacks urgency, the structure has not weakened materially.

Products & Crush: Crush fundamentals remain the anchor. January NOPA crush totaled 221.6 million
bushels, a record for the month and confirmation that domestic demand remains historically strong.
Soybean oil stocks rose to approximately 1.9 billion pounds, up sharply year over year, tempering
enthusiasm on the product side. The crush margin remains meal-driven, with soybean meal demand
providing the primary support for processing economics. USDA’s emphasis on domestic use as the key
demand pillar continues to be validated by execution data.

Cash & Basis: Cash markets held steady despite softer board tone. CIF Gulf values remained near
$12.36–$12.37, reflecting continued export coverage even as Brazil’s harvest advances. Interior
processor bids remained firm across the Midwest, with limited evidence of aggressive farmer selling.
Basis is not accelerating higher, but it is not retreating either. The nearby market remains supported by
crush pull rather than export urgency, reinforcing the divergence between domestic strength and global
supply weight.

Funds & Positioning: Managed Money maintained a sizable net long near 115,896 contracts as of the

February 10 report, elevated relative to recent historical averages. While not at record extremes, the
positioning profile leaves the market more vulnerable to long liquidation than short covering. Gross long
exposure remains substantial, and absent a fresh bullish catalyst, speculative conviction appears tactical
rather than structural. The positioning tilt is constructive, but extended.

Export Sales & Inspections: Weekly export inspections for the period ending February 12 totaled
1,202,617 metric tons. Cumulative inspections for the 2025/26 marketing year now stand at 24,348,735
metric tons, compared with 36,019,599 metric tons at the same time last year — a decline of roughly 32
percent year over year. While weekly flow remains functional, the broader export program continues to
lag materially behind last season due to Brazil’s dominant early-season presence.

Global Context: Brazil’s 2025/26 soybean production estimate remains near 178 million metric tons,
reinforcing expectations for a large exportable surplus. Harvest progress continues without major
disruption in central regions, and FOB offers remain competitive into Asia. Argentina’s production outlook
remains near 48.5 million metric tons, with localized stress but no material downgrade during the
reporting window. Brazil continues to set the global pricing ceiling, limiting U.S. upside absent a supply
disruption or shift in trade flow timing.

So What? Soybeans remain domestically supported but externally weak. Record crush continues to
anchor the nearby structure, yet cumulative export inspections are running roughly 32 percent behind last
year, underscoring Brazil’s dominance in early-season trade flows. The curve reflects comfortable supply,
and funds remain meaningfully long. Absent a shift in export momentum or a South American production
disruption, upside remains capped while domestic demand prevents structural deterioration.

Wheat Market Deep Dive

Futures & Spreads: Wheat futures firmed through the holiday-shortened week, with March Chicago
SRW settling near $5.47, down roughly fifteen cents on the period. The March–May spread held near a
4.75-cent carry, maintaining a storage-led structure but without tightening. The curve remains comfortably
in contango, reflecting adequate supply and limited urgency from commercial buyers. Price action was
largely technical, with no fresh fundamental catalyst emerging. Kansas City and Minneapolis contracts
followed a similar tone, offering little structural divergence within the complex.

Cash & Basis: Cash markets remained broadly stable. CIF Gulf SRW values held near $6.23–$6.28 per
bushel, while interior basis levels showed regional variation but no meaningful tightening. Plains HRW
basis remained steady despite localized dryness concerns, and Pacific Northwest values were largely
unchanged. The absence of basis acceleration reinforces the view that end users remain covered. The
physical market continues to signal sufficiency rather than scarcity.

Export Sales & Inspections: Weekly export inspections for the period ending February 12 totaled
approximately 0.38 million metric tons, a moderate weekly pace. Export sales for the week ending
February 5 reached roughly 488,000 metric tons, consistent with routine demand but not indicative of
acceleration. Cumulative shipments remain aligned with USDA projections and modestly ahead of last
year’s pace, yet volumes remain insufficient to materially tighten the balance sheet. The export program
is functioning, but it lacks momentum capable of shifting broader sentiment.

Global Context: Global supply remains the defining feature. Russian wheat continues to anchor world
pricing, with export offers remaining competitive into North Africa and the Middle East. Ukrainian
shipments remain active through established corridors, and European origin has re-entered select

tenders where freight allows. Argentine supply remains available, adding incremental pressure. With no
logistical disruption or policy constraint emerging across major exporters, global availability continues to
cap rallies and limit speculative rebalancing.

Funds & Positioning: Managed Money maintains a substantial net short near 85,832 contracts as of
the February 10 CFTC report. While positioning is not expanding aggressively, it remains historically
large. Short covering has been limited, suggesting that funds remain comfortable leaning defensively in
the absence of a catalyst. The short profile provides latent upside risk, but without a supply disruption or
export acceleration, that positioning alone has not been sufficient to trigger sustained covering.

So What? February reaffirmed wheat as the loosest balance sheet within the grain complex. Domestic
stocks remain elevated, global inventories are comfortable, and structure continues to reward storage
over urgency. Export demand is steady but insufficient to tighten conditions, and cash markets show little
stress. Without disruption in a major exporting region or a material shift in global demand, wheat is likely
to remain rangebound, driven more by positioning and spread behavior than by fundamental tightening.

US Cash Market & Basis

Corn: Corn basis held steady across primary corridors despite softer futures. CIF Gulf values remained
near $5.26–$5.29 per bushel, reflecting continued export coverage. Interior river bids showed modest
regional variation but no broad deterioration. Freight conditions have improved from peak winter
disruption, though draft limitations still affect barge efficiency. Ethanol plant bids across the Midwest
remained firm, signaling consistent grind demand and limited farmer selling. The physical market remains
balanced, with no evidence of forced movement.

Soybeans: Soybean basis continues to reflect strong domestic crush pull. CIF Gulf values held near
$12.36–$12.37, indicating steady export coverage even as Brazil’s harvest advances. Interior processor
bids across key Midwest regions remained competitive, reinforcing spot demand. River logistics have
normalized relative to prior constraints, reducing transportation friction without materially loosening basis.
While the board softened, cash markets continue to reflect structural domestic demand.

Wheat: Wheat cash markets were largely unchanged. CIF Gulf SRW values remained near $6.23–$6.28
per bushel, while HRW basis across the Plains showed limited movement. Pacific Northwest bids were
steady, and interior markets lacked directional energy. With no export acceleration or logistical disruption,
basis remains stable but unresponsive. U.S. wheat continues to face competitive global pressure, limiting
tightening in nearby structure.

So What? Physical markets remain steadier than futures suggest. Corn basis reflects export execution
and ethanol demand, soybeans remain anchored by crush, and wheat signals sufficiency rather than
scarcity. Logistics have improved but have not altered structure. Absent a material shift in demand or
supply, cash markets are likely to remain balanced as futures consolidate.

International Cash & Trade Flows

Brazil: Brazil continues to anchor global trade flows through strong execution and competitive pricing.
ANEC projects robust soybean shipments through early 2026 as harvest advances. FOB offers out of
Santos and Paranaguá remain competitive into Asia, sustaining export momentum. Corn exports remain
supported by late-season availability and active vessel lineups across major ports. Brazil’s export
program remains the dominant structural force in global oilseed and coarse grain flow.

Argentina: Argentina remains the primary supply-side uncertainty. Soybean and late-planted corn

conditions show vulnerability amid uneven rainfall and soil moisture deficits, heightening sensitivity to
February precipitation during reproductive stages. FOB offers have adjusted modestly and remain
competitive where available. Export flow continues but lacks the scale to challenge Brazil’s dominance
absent a weather-driven disruption.

China: China’s import posture remains selective. Routine U.S. soybean cargoes have moved, but no
large new purchase announcements emerged during the window. Brazilian origin continues to be favored
due to pricing advantages and abundant supply. Domestic corn price support tempers incremental import
demand, while downstream feed margins remain pressured. China remains tactical rather than
expansionary.

EU / Black Sea: Black Sea origin continues to set the tone for wheat pricing. Russian 12.5 percent
protein wheat remains competitively offered on FOB terms, pressuring U.S. origin into price-sensitive
tenders. Ukrainian corn remains available through established corridors, while EU wheat export pace
trails last year. Lower-cost Black Sea supply remains the primary outlet for global wheat trade.

Other Regions: Australia continues to move wheat into Asian markets following a solid harvest, while
Canadian spring wheat exports proceed in line with seasonal norms. No material trade restrictions, quota
shifts, or policy disruptions have emerged across secondary exporters. The broader international
environment remains stable and well supplied.

So What? Global trade flows continue to reflect price leadership rather than disruption. Brazil dominates
soybeans and remains competitive in corn, Russia and Ukraine anchor wheat supply, and Argentina’s
role remains weather-dependent. China’s demand remains opportunistic. U.S. competitiveness is timing-
driven rather than structural, and absent supply shocks or narrowing FOB spreads, global flows are likely
to remain competitive rather than restrictive.

Key Fundamentals & Reports (Feb 12 – Feb 18)

Export Inspections (USDA FGIS – week ending Feb 12): Corn export inspections totaled 1,492,383
metric tons, bringing cumulative 2025/26 shipments to 35,726,976 metric tons, compared with 24,749,316
metric tons at the same point last year, an increase of approximately 44 percent year over year, keeping
the program ahead of the pace required to meet USDA’s projection. Soybean inspections reached
1,202,617 metric tons, with cumulative shipments at 24,348,735 metric tons versus 36,019,599 metric
tons last year, a decline of roughly 32 percent year over year, reflecting Brazil’s dominant early-season
export window. Wheat inspections totaled approximately 381,000 metric tons, with marketing-year
shipments modestly ahead of last season and broadly aligned with USDA expectations, though not at a
pace sufficient to materially tighten the balance sheet.

Export Sales (USDA FAS – latest through week ending Feb 5): Weekly export sales for the week
ending February 5 reflected continued forward activity. Corn net sales totaled 2,105,500 metric tons,
marking a strong weekly commitment. Soybean net sales totaled 377,600 metric tons, while wheat net
sales reached 498,000 metric tons. While soybean bookings remain moderate relative to prior seasonal
peaks, corn commitments continue to run firmly. No major daily flash sales were reported during the
window. Due to the Washington’s Birthday Federal holiday, the next U.S. Export Sales report will be
released Friday, February 20, 2026.

Ethanol Production & Stocks: Ethanol production for the week ending Feb 13 averaged 1.08 million
barrels per day (down from 1.11), while stocks remained high at 25.25 million barrels. This indicates a

slight seasonal dip in production while inventories continue to build, keeping a lid on immediate corn
demand upside.

NOPA Soybean Crush: January NOPA crush totaled 221.56 million bushels, a record for the month and
confirmation of historically strong domestic processing demand. Soybean oil stocks increased to
approximately 1.90 billion pounds, reflecting ample product availability even as crush margins remain
meal driven. The data reinforces domestic demand as the primary support within the soybean balance
sheet.

CFTC Positioning: As of February 10 (released February 13), Managed Money held a net short of
57,493 contracts in corn, reflecting stabilization rather than renewed bearish expansion. In soybeans,
funds maintained a sizable net long of 115,896 contracts, elevated relative to recent averages. Chicago
SRW wheat held a net short of 85,832 contracts, remaining the most structurally bearish position across
the grain complex. Positioning remains influential but is no longer accelerating in any single direction.

So What? Core U.S. fundamentals remain stable. Corn exports and ethanol grind continue to validate
steady demand. Soybeans are anchored by record crush despite competitive global supply. Wheat
execution remains routine but overshadowed by ample global availability. Speculative positioning remains
meaningful but is no longer expanding aggressively. Beneath the board’s consolidation, physical
execution remains intact as mid-February progresses.

International Reports

Brazil: Brazil remains the central driver of global oilseed and corn supply. CONAB maintains 2025/26
soybean production at 177.98 million metric tons, reinforcing expectations for a large exportable surplus.
Corn production under CONAB’s February update is estimated at 138.45 million metric tons, reflecting a
larger total supply outlook than earlier projections. The first crop remains steady, while the safrinha crop
accounts for the majority of the expected volume. This larger production profile reinforces expectations for
a significant second-half export presence and continued pressure on global FOB pricing once harvest
advances.

Argentina: Argentina’s production outlook remains stable on official estimates, with soybeans near 48.5
million metric tons and corn near 57 million metric tons. Weather variability persists, but no fresh
downward revisions emerged during the reporting window. Farmer selling remains measured, influenced
by currency dynamics and macro uncertainty, limiting aggressive forward flow. FOB values have adjusted
modestly but not sharply, indicating that export channels remain functional despite agronomic variability.
The market continues to monitor late-season rainfall, but risk has not yet translated into structural volume
constraints.

China: Chinese demand signals remained measured. No major new U.S. purchase announcements were
reported during the period, though routine soybean inflows continued. Domestic futures markets showed
modest firmness, suggesting stable crush economics, but downstream feed demand remains constrained
by livestock margin pressure. State support for domestic corn pricing continues to temper incremental
import demand. China remains active but tactical, providing support on breaks rather than driving
sustained upside.

EU / Black Sea: Black Sea origin continues to set the tone for global wheat pricing. Russian 12.5 percent
protein wheat remains competitively offered near $229 per metric ton FOB, reinforcing its role as the
global price leader. Ukrainian corn remains available through established export corridors, with offers
near $235 per metric ton FOB into select destinations. European Union wheat values remain elevated
relative to price-sensitive tenders, and EU export pace remains behind last year’s levels. With no new

policy or freight disruptions emerging, global wheat trade remains anchored by low-cost Black Sea
supply, limiting upside potential for alternative origins.

So What? International markets remain defined by availability rather than disruption. Brazil continues to
export aggressively, Argentina’s risk profile is monitored but not escalating, and Russia maintains pricing
leadership in wheat. China’s participation remains selective. Global supply remains ample on paper, and
until weather, logistics, or policy materially constrain exportable volume, international trade flows are likely
to remain competitive rather than restrictive.

Weather & Crop Conditions

United States
Weather remains a secondary driver for US grain markets, though short-term patterns have been
operationally supportive. Temperatures across much of the Midwest and Plains have trended near to
slightly above seasonal norms, limiting freeze-related logistics disruptions and allowing river systems to
function more consistently following earlier winter constraints. Snow cover across key HRW wheat areas
in Kansas, Oklahoma, and Colorado remains adequate, reducing immediate winterkill risk despite
intermittent cold shots. NOAA’s 6–10 and 8–14 day outlooks continue to favor moderation rather than
extreme cold, reinforcing dormancy stability for winter wheat and limiting headline-driven volatility. With
harvest complete and no active fieldwork underway, US weather remains neutral from a supply
standpoint, functioning primarily as a logistics variable rather than a production catalyst at this stage of
the calendar.

Brazil
Brazil’s weather profile remains broadly favorable for aggregate production. Central and northern regions
continue to receive adequate rainfall, supporting soybean development and sustaining expectations for a
large national crop near CONAB’s 177.98 million metric ton estimate. Harvest progress continues, though
fieldwork remains uneven in localized areas where rainfall has slowed operations. In southern Brazil,
moisture levels remain elevated in parts of Paraná and Rio Grande do Sul, contributing to localized
variability and increased disease pressure. However, no new national yield revision emerged during the
window, and export logistics continue to function normally. For now, Brazil’s weather remains a variability
story rather than a disruption narrative.

Argentina
Argentina’s crop profile remains the most weather-sensitive element globally. Soil moisture deficits persist
across key soybean and corn regions, and crop ratings remain below seasonal norms. However, during
the reporting window, no material downward revision to official production estimates was issued, with
soybeans near 48.5 million metric tons and corn near 57 million metric tons. Rainfall coverage remained
uneven, offering limited relief without reversing broader stress. With crops advancing through
reproductive stages, yield sensitivity remains elevated, but the market has not materially repriced supply
risk during the week. Weather risk remains present, though not intensifying.

Global
Outside South America, global weather risks remain limited. Winter wheat across Russia, Ukraine, and
the European Union remains in dormancy under generally stable conditions, with no new winterkill threats
reported. Black Sea logistics continue without disruption. Australia and Canada report no new production
concerns. The broader global weather profile remains stable.

So What?
Weather risk remains concentrated but contained. Argentina continues to face structural moisture stress,
while southern Brazil exhibits localized variability. Central Brazil remains productive, and U.S. conditions
remain neutral. At this stage, weather is being monitored rather than repriced. Until either Argentina sees

a measurable production downgrade or Brazilian harvest disruptions materially impact export flow,
weather remains a background variable rather than a directional catalyst.

Synthesis & Outlook

Futures softened through the holiday-shortened week, with corn settling at $4.27 per bushel, soybeans at
$11.33, and Chicago SRW wheat at $5.47. While flat price drifted lower, the underlying structure
remained stable. The March–May corn spread held near a 9-cent carry (60 percent of full carry),
soybeans near a 16 cent carry (72 percent of full carry), and wheat near a 4½–5 cent carry, confirming
adequate supply without structural stress. CIF Gulf and interior basis levels remained steady across all
three complexes. Managed money positioning remains meaningful, approximately –57,500 corn,
+115,900 soybeans, and –85,800 Chicago wheat as of February 10, but is no longer accelerating. The
divergence between flat price softness and steady physical execution remains intact.

Corn continues to reflect balance rather than stress. Export inspections totaled 1.49 million metric tons for
the week ending February 12, ethanol production held near 1.08 million barrels per day, and spreads are
holding near 60 percent of full carry. CIF Gulf values remain near $5.26–$5.29, and interior basis has
stabilized rather than strengthened. Futures remain capped by comfortable ending stocks, yet the front
end continues to resist disorderly widening. The market appears content trading carry economics while
awaiting a catalyst.

Soybeans remain structurally anchored by domestic crush. January NOPA crush totaled 221.6 million
bushels, a monthly record, reinforcing USDA’s emphasis on domestic use. Funds hold a sizable net long
near 115,900 contracts, introducing potential vulnerability to liquidation but also reflecting constructive
demand sentiment. Brazil’s soybean production estimate remains near 177.98 million metric tons,
keeping global supply expectations heavy, while Argentina’s production remains near 48.5 million metric
tons with weather risk present but not escalating. The structure remains firm at roughly 60 percent of full
carry, but flat price lacks urgency absent a shift in trade flow or supply outlook.

Wheat remains the most structurally heavy component of the complex. Export inspections totaled 0.38
million metric tons, consistent but not dynamic. Global supply continues to dictate pricing, with Russian
12.5 percent protein wheat offered near $229 per metric ton FOB. Spreads remain in carry near 5 cents,
basis is stable near $6.23–$6.28 CIF Gulf, and speculative positioning remains heavily short near 85,800
contracts without forced covering. Until global availability tightens or a weather event materializes, wheat
is likely to remain rangebound with rallies constrained.

Bullish triggers: Upside risk would likely require a measurable production downgrade in Argentina,
particularly if soybean output falls materially below 48.5 million metric tons or corn below 57 million metric
tons, or if harvest disruptions in southern Brazil begin to slow export execution. Additional support could
emerge from a sustained acceleration in U.S. corn export pace above the current 1.49 million metric ton
weekly trend, further strength in ethanol production above 1.08 million barrels per day, or meaningful
short covering in wheat triggered by a global supply disruption.

Bearish triggers: Downside pressure would build under a smooth Brazilian harvest with uninterrupted
export flow near 177.98 million metric tons, combined with stabilization or improvement in Argentine crop
conditions. Softening U.S. basis across interior corridors would signal weaker physical demand, while
long liquidation in soybeans, given the extended 115,900 contract net long, could amplify volatility.
Continued Black Sea pricing dominance near $229 per metric ton FOB would further cap wheat rallies
and reinforce the heavy global supply narrative.

Watchlist (Feb 19–Feb 26):

Argentina: Argentina rainfall distribution and crop ratings

Brazil: Brazil harvest progress and export lineups

Weekly exports: Weekly export inspections and sales consistency

Ethanol: Ethanol production and stocks trajectory

Positioning: Fund positioning shifts in corn and wheat

In summary: Corn remains supported by export execution and steady ethanol demand, with cumulative
shipments running 44 percent ahead of last year. Soybeans rely primarily on record domestic crush to
offset a materially weaker export program that is currently 32 percent behind last season. Wheat
continues to trade under the weight of ample global availability and persistent Black Sea competitiveness.
None of the complexes exhibit tightening stress, and until either demand accelerates or supply is
disrupted, price discovery is likely to remain governed by carry economics and incremental flow rather
than conviction-driven movement.

Ethanol for America Act

The major barrier to a higher Renewable Fuel Standard (RFS) is the fuel versus food debate. Now is the
perfect time to move to E15. Corn prices are extremely low, and the latest USDA WASDE reported a record
crop and a record crop expansion.

USDA WASDE forecast corn production at 16.7 billion bushels, with the yield at 188.8 bushels per acre and
ending stocks at 2.1 billion. The season-average corn price dropped 30 cents to $3.90 a bushel. Most
importantly, 202526 crop year is expected to have 1.8 billion more bushels than 2024/25.

With current ethanol production consuming 5.6 billion bushels, a 10 percent increase in consumption would
consume an additional 560 million bushels. The reality is that year around E15 would add approximately
50 million bushels and regulatory streamlining would add another 50 million bushels. At some point over
the next ten years, the additional corn consumption could be 2.8 billion bushels minus lower fuel
consumption resulting from electric vehicles. The ethanol corn consumption increase is likely to be more
of a S curve as new compacity comes online.

With Brazil and the US corn and soybean production increasing, 100 million bushels of ethanol corn
consumption will barely move the needle. The ethanol production capacity expansion that would be three
to four years from now is when the corn consumption impact to the corn market would be felt.

Executive Summary

Corn – Front End Firms as Basis Surges and
Execution Holds Pace
Corn edged higher this week, with March futures gaining
eight cents to close at $4.30. The March–May spread held
steady close to a seven-cent carry, reflecting approximately
50 percent of full carry. Export inspections reached a five-
week high at 1.51 million metric tons, lifting cumulative
shipments to 31.44 million metric tons, approximately 53
percent ahead of last year. Basis firmed sharply as river
freight surged; CIF jumped to +97H, interior bids held firm,
and barge costs increased due to ice-related delays.
Despite a 2.1-billion-bushel carryout, the front end remains

bid. Funds trimmed shorts modestly but held a net short of nearly 95,900 contracts. Futures remain
rangebound, but the structure continues to flash firmness the flat price has not yet justified.

Wheat – Winter Risk Adds Support, but Heavy Balance Sheet Holds
Wheat firmed on the week, with March Chicago SRW futures up 28 cents to 5.35 dollars, and the March–
May spread close to a nine-cent carry. USDA posted export inspections of 0.35 million metric tons, last
week, keeping cumulative shipments 18.2 percent ahead of last year. SRW Gulf offers held near +130-
140H while HRW basis firmed slightly on winterkill concerns. A midweek cold snap with patchy snow
cover sparked some speculative short covering, although managed money traders still hold a net short of
more than 113,500 contracts. Russian FOB wheat held firm near $229 per metric ton, maintaining
downside pressure on global pricing. While US flows are holding steady, the broader market remains
defined by ample global supply.

Soybeans – Domestic Pull Steadies Market, but Export Lag Persists
Soybeans closed at $10.74, up roughly 11 cents on the week, with the March–May spread holding near
minus 12 cents. CIF basis remained firm at 109 over March, while Midwest processors continued to post
strong bids. Weekly inspections totaled 1.32 million metric tons, led by China, and USDA reported a flash
sale of 192,350 metric tons to unknown destinations. Funds lightened net length to 14,624 contracts. With
harvest only five percent complete in Brazil and Argentina showing confirmed crop stress, US crushers
continue to anchor the front end. However, cumulative US soybean exports remain down 37.4 percent
year over year, underscoring Brazil’s advantage in early-season global shipments. Futures remain boxed
in by global supply prospects, but firm interior demand continues to set the tone.

Macro / Policy – Crude Slips, Dollar Steady, Freight Tightens

Crude oil softened throughout the week, with West Texas Intermediate closing near $62.39 per barrel and
Brent near $67.57, easing energy input costs across the supply chain. The dollar index hovered near 96,
softening modestly and providing slight tailwinds to US export competitiveness. Barge freight surged as
ice disrupted river movement on the Upper Mississippi and Illinois, driving seasonal highs in tariff rates.
With macro indicators largely stable, logistics and basis continue to guide market tone.

Weather – Plains Freeze, South America Dries

A second Arctic front swept across the US Plains, with subzero temperatures and uneven snow cover
heightening winterkill risk for hard red winter wheat. River ice buildup slowed barge flows along key
tributaries. In South America, central Brazil received consistent rainfall, while the southern states and
much of Argentina remained dry. Argentine crop ratings deteriorated sharply, raising the probability of
irreversible yield loss as February approaches.

What’s Next (Jan 29 – Feb 05):

USDA Export Sales covering January 22 (due January 29)

CIF and Gulf basis behavior under ongoing river constraints

Argentina rainfall forecast and crop condition update

Brazil harvest progress and vessel lineups

Flash sales activity following Lunar New Year

Corn Market Deep Dive

Futures & Spreads: March corn rose eight cents on the week to close at $4.30, holding near the upper
end of its January range. The March–May spread held steady at minus 7 cents, representing
approximately 50 percent of full carry. The curve remains in carry, though not at maximum, a reflection of
firm front-end commercial pull rather than any shift in the broader balance sheet. Volatility remained low
and open interest ticked higher, suggesting quiet accumulation rather than new directional conviction.

Funds & Positioning: Managed Money held a net short of 95,867 contracts as of January 20, trimming
exposure only modestly on the week. Gross shorts stood near 305,000, with longs just above 209,000.
While still elevated historically, the speculative short has stopped expanding. With futures failing to break
lower, the pressure to add new shorts has eased. In the absence of a fresh macro driver or shift in export
flow, funds appear comfortable maintaining a defensive bias without further buildup.

Cash & Basis: Cash markets tightened materially. CIF basis jumped to +97H as river freight constraints
intensified, and interior processor bids strengthened across key ethanol regions. River basis firmed in
response to limited barge capacity and ongoing seasonal delays. FOB Gulf offers held steady near
+109H for February, signaling persistent downstream demand. The basis strength is not speculative, it
reflects real logistics, confirmed execution, and continued slow farmer selling.

Export Sales & Inspections: Export inspections totaled 1.51 million metric tons for the week ending
January 22, the highest weekly volume since mid-December. Cumulative shipments for the marketing
year reached 31.44 million metric tons, 53.4 percent above last year’s pace. Two flash sales were
confirmed during the week, totaling 345,000 metric tons (split between Colombia and unknown). The
weekly report and flash sales align with USDA’s revised 2025/26 US corn export forecast and reinforces
the on-pace execution theme.

Global Context: Brazil’s January corn export projection was revised to 3.45 million metric tons, according
to ANEC, with FOB offers from Paranaguá near $205 per metric ton. The Argentine crop remains under

significant stress, though Up-River corn was still offered near $225 per metric ton as of late January.
Ukraine continued to offer into Europe near $235 per metric ton via Danube loadings, though Black Sea
flows remain seasonally light. US origin maintains a competitiveness edge into Mexico and into select
Asian destinations, but Brazil’s forward program continues to limit broader price responsiveness.

So What? The board remains subdued, but the physical market keeps flashing firmness. Execution is
consistent, basis is rallying, and spreads are stable. While speculative positioning is still short, funds are
no longer pressing aggressively. With logistics acting as a bottleneck and harvest in South America still
early, front-end corn remains bid, even if flat price optics remain heavy on paper.

Soybean Market Deep Dive

Futures & Spreads: March soybeans settled at $10.74, gaining approximately 11 cents on the week.
The March–May spread closed at a 12-cent carry, steady week over week. While the flat price remains
constrained by global supply prospects, the nearby curve still reflects meaningful domestic pull. Open
interest increased, and daily trading ranges narrowed, a sign of less directional conviction but ongoing
positioning around crush-led strength. Full carry percentages were not computed due to lack of published
storage assumptions, though the structure remains comfortably in contango.

Products & Crush: NOPA’s December crush, reported on January 15, totaled 225.0 million bushels, the
second-highest monthly volume on record. National totals likely exceeded 230 million bushels. Soybean
oil stocks rose to 1.642 billion pounds, a seven-month high, while soymeal demand remained firm. The
crush margin continues to be meal-driven, as soy oil faces policy-linked headwinds. These fundamentals
confirm USDA’s January pivot toward domestic demand as the primary source of use.

Cash & Basis: CIF basis held firm at +109H, supported by limited interior movement and ongoing barge
disruptions. FOB Gulf offers for February–March remained steady, quoted between +115 and 118H,
underscoring continued export interest. Processors across the Eastern and Western Corn Belts
maintained strong bids: central Illinois stayed near +5H, while select Iowa plants were reportedly paying
up to +10H. The national average basis firmed by 1 cent to -67H. Despite seasonally soft futures, interior
processor bids remain elevated, backed by firm soymeal demand and limited farm sales.

Funds & Positioning: Managed Money reduced net length to 14,624 contracts as of January 20, a
modest pullback from earlier January highs. Gross longs dipped to around 99,000 contracts, while gross
shorts ticked up to approximately 84,000. The positioning profile now looks lighter and more responsive,
not aggressively long, but still tilted constructively due to domestic demand resilience.

Export Sales & Inspections: Weekly inspections for the period ending January 22 totaled 1.32 million
metric tons, led by shipments to China. A daily flash sale of 192,350 metric tons to unknown was
confirmed by USDA on January 21. However, cumulative US soybean exports are now approximately
20.67 million metric tons, down about 37.4 percent year over year, reflecting Brazil’s dominant position in
early-season trade. While total commitments remain solid, the broader US export program continues to
lag.

Global Context: Brazil’s January soybean export forecast has been revised down to 3.23 million metric
tons, per ANEC, slightly below earlier expectations. FOB Santos offers hovered around $402 per metric
ton, still undercutting US Gulf equivalents into Asia. Brazilian harvest progress reached just under 5
percent, modestly behind average pace, slowing the short-term flow of new-crop supply but not yet
altering expectations for a heavy first quarter shipping window. In Argentina, dryness persisted and crop
condition ratings declined further, increasing sensitivity to mid-February rainfall. Chinese demand signals

remained intermittent, present enough to support minor bounces, but not broad or sustained enough to
reshape the structural outlook.

So What? Soybeans remain suspended between strong domestic crush demand and a looming wave of
South American supply. The board continues to reflect global caution, while cash markets, especially
processors, continue to define the tone through strong interior pull. Funds are paring back but remain net
long, betting on US interior strength. The coming weeks are critical: if Brazil’s logistics remain constrained
and weather setbacks persist, the front end could stay supported. But for now, the market is not trending,
it is stalling, waiting for confirmation.

Wheat Market Deep Dive

Futures & Spreads: March Chicago SRW closed at $5.35, up approximately 28 cents on the week. The
March–May spread held steady at a nine-cent carry, reflecting a storage-led market. Full carry
percentages were not computed due to lack of posted assumptions. The SRW curve remains sluggish,
anchored by ample global supply and limited urgency from end users. Price action responded briefly to
midweek winterkill headlines but lacked follow-through. Minneapolis and Kansas City contracts were
steadier, with modest HRW strength helping firm basis-adjusted tone.

Cash & Basis: FOB Gulf SRW offers held near +100H (equivalent to about $6.16 per bushel), while
HRW with 11 percent protein hovered around +110K. Interior Plains basis was mostly unchanged:
ordinary protein held near +90K and 12 percent protein near +106K. Pacific Northwest soft white wheat
remained steady around $6.55 per bushel. Overall, basis remains stable but lacks directional energy.
Export buyers continue to be selective, with US origin still priced out of competitive tenders due to Black
Sea pressure.

Export Sales & Inspections: Weekly inspections for the period ending January 22 totaled 0.35 million
metric tons, slightly behind the same week last year. However, marketing-year-to-date exports are 18.2
percent ahead of last season. Weekly sales figures for this window posted after we had written this
week’s edition, but the previous report showed a strong 632,000 metric tons booked, primarily to
Southeast Asia and Latin America. No daily flash sales were reported during the period, adding a little
more weight to the weekly report to support the recent export volume momentum, or to slow it.

Global context: Black Sea supply continues to set the tone for global pricing. Russian FOB wheat with
12.5 percent protein was offered near $229 per metric ton, with January exports tracking close to three
million metric tons, strong, though slightly below December levels. Ukrainian shipments via Danube and
rail remained undisrupted. French wheat held near €240 per metric ton (roughly $260), which continues to
limit EU competitiveness in global tenders. Argentine Up-River wheat was quoted near $210 per metric
ton. With no policy or logistical barriers in major origins, low-cost supply continues to cap upside.

Funds & Positioning: Managed money traders extended net shorts in Chicago SRW to 113,560
contracts as of January 20, adding to bearish bets by nearly 4,000 contracts from the previous week.
Gross shorts rose to approximately 199,000 contracts, while gross longs remained near 85,000. The book
remains the most asymmetrically short of the major grain complexes. Despite the position size, there is no
evident catalyst forcing broad fund repositioning.

So What? Wheat remains trapped in a familiar cycle, large speculative shorts, ample global supply, and
no structural break to change the tone. Basis is flat, spreads lean toward full carry, and US origin remains
noncompetitive versus Black Sea flows. Until a structural shock emerges, whether from weather, freight,
or geopolitics, this remains a defensive, short-led market. Futures are too bearish to collapse further

without a trigger, and too structurally expensive to rally without a shake-up. The complex is idling, waiting
for a reason to move.

US Cash Market & Basis

Corn: CIF Gulf basis surged to +97H for January barges, up sharply on the week due to ice-related river
delays. February CIF was quoted near +92H. FOB Gulf offers for February held steady around +109H.
River logistics tightened materially: Illinois River freight jumped to between 640 and 685 percent of tariff,
significantly higher than both the prior week and year-ago levels. Interior basis remained firm, the DTN
Corn Index averaged -35H nationally, up one cent week over week. Eastern Corn Belt processors,
particularly ethanol plants in Iowa and Illinois, were bidding as strong as +20 to 30H, underscoring interior
competition for spot bushels.

Soybeans: CIF NOLA spot barges held near +109H, with February quoted at +103H. FOB Gulf
premiums remained steady, ranging from +115H to +118H for February to March loadings. The national
average basis firmed one cent to -67H, supported by a strong interior crush pull. Central Illinois
processors continued to post flat or slight premiums to March, while other Midwest plants reportedly paid
+5 to +10H to secure ownership.

Wheat: FOB Gulf offers for SRW held near +100H (equivalent to approximately $6.16 per bushel), while
HRW with 11 percent protein remained around +110K. Interior Plains basis was stable: ordinary protein
held near +90K and 12 percent protein near +106K. Pacific Northwest soft white wheat bids were
unchanged around $6.55 per bushel. Export competitiveness remains limited, but basis structure is
holding firm in the absence of new disruptions.

So What? Despite heavy carryout projections on paper, physical markets remain firm across all three
majors. Corn basis is being driven by river constraints and aggressive ethanol demand, not speculative
positioning. Soybeans continue to reflect tight crush-linked pull, even as exports lag. Wheat basis is
stable but uninspired, propped up by interior structure rather than global demand. The cash market
continues to defy futures' bearish tone, until basis softens or logistical pressures ease, physical tightness
will remain the dominant front-end signal.

International Cash & Trade Flows

Brazil: ANEC revised its January export forecast to 3.23 million metric tons for soybeans, down from an
earlier 3.79 million metric tons, and 3.45 million metric tons for corn, citing faster loadings at northern
ports and stable carryover availability. FOB Santos soybean offers remained near $402 per metric ton,
retaining a pricing edge over US Gulf equivalents into Asia. Corn out of Paranaguá held firm at $205 per
metric ton, remaining competitive across Mediterranean and Southeast Asian markets. Vessel lineups
showed robust movement through Santos, Barcarena, and northern arc corridors. Early soybean harvest
progress was slow but stable, with logistical flows beginning to scale.

Argentina: Crop conditions continued to deteriorate. The Buenos Aires Grain Exchange cut soybean
good to excellent ratings to approximately 20 percent, down eight percent, and corn to approximately 15
percent, down 12 points. FOB Up-River prices firmed to approximately $225 per metric ton for corn and
$445 per metric ton for soymeal, underpinned by tightening supply outlooks and slow farmer selling. The
official exchange rate held near 390 Argentine pesos per US dollar, though parallel rates continue to
influence farmer behavior. Government discussions around export quota management are ongoing and
likely contingent on February rainfall outcomes.

China: No major US grain purchases were confirmed beyond routine soybean flows. However, Chinese

weekly US soybean imports approached 1.24 million metric tons, and January total imports are projected
near 7.0 million metric tons, up five percent year over year. Dalian January soybean futures rose
approximately 38 yuan per metric ton on the week, indicating marginally improved crush margins. Feed
demand remains under pressure due to weak hog profitability, but state reserves have been actively
purchasing domestic corn to support internal market pricing.

EU / Black Sea: Russian 12.5 percent protein wheat held firm at approximately $229 per metric ton FOB
Novorossiysk, with January shipments estimated near three million metric tons, in line with historical
averages but softer than December. Ukrainian corn offers hovered around $235 per metric ton, though
feed demand from European Union buyers remained subdued. French wheat, quoted out of Rouen, held
near €240 per metric ton, approximately $260, still priced out of recent global tenders. The European
Union wheat export license total reached approximately 16 million metric tons season-to-date, about 10
percent behind last year, as aggressive Black Sea pricing continues to pressure European
competitiveness.

So What? Global trade flows remain driven by low-cost origins, Brazil in soybeans and corn, and Russia
in wheat. Argentina remains price-competitive, but forward volume is now contingent on both weather and
farmer willingness to sell following recent tax adjustments. China’s presence in the soybean market adds
support during breaks, but its buying pattern remains episodic and insufficient to shift broader demand
trends. For now, US export competitiveness is situational, not structural, and will remain dependent on
timing advantages, logistical flexibility, or short-term disruptions in South America or the Black Sea. Until
global FOB spreads narrow or crop losses reshape available supply, the US remains a secondary origin
in the global price hierarchy.

Key Fundamentals & Reports (Jan 15 – Jan 22)

Export Inspections: For the week ending January 22 (published January 26), corn inspections totaled
1.51 million metric tons, the highest weekly volume since mid-December. Cumulative shipments reached
31.44 million metric tons, now 53.4 percent ahead of last year. Soybean inspections were 1.32 million
metric tons, slightly below the prior week, with China accounting for the majority of loadings. However,
cumulative soybean exports totaled 20.67 million metric tons, down 37.4 percent from a year ago. Wheat
inspections came in at 0.35 million metric tons, a modest decline week over week, but enough to keep
season-to-date exports at 16.33 million metric tons, roughly 18.2 percent ahead of last year and broadly
aligned with USDA’s 725 million bushel projection.

Export Sales: Weekly export sales for the week ending January 22 had not been published at the time of
writing, but daily flash activity during the window included 345,000 metric tons of corn (split between
Colombia and unknown) and 192,350 metric tons of soybeans to unknown. The prior week official sales
(ending January 15) showed aggressive net corn sales of 4.01 million metric tons, a marketing-year high,
and strong soybean net sales of 2.45 million metric tons, led by China. Wheat bookings totaled 632,000
metric tons, indicating healthy forward interest, particularly from Southeast Asia and Latin America.

Ethanol Production & Stocks: For the week ending January 16 (published January 24), US ethanol
production dropped to 1.119 million barrels per day, down 77,000 from the previous week’s record high,
but still up 1.2 percent year over year. Ethanol stocks rose to 25.74 million barrels, the highest level in
nearly a year and 11.5 percent above last year. Export estimates hovered near 218,000 barrels per day,
helping absorb some of the build. Cold weather and regional gas constraints prompted temporary
production cutbacks in parts of the Western Corn Belt, but overall grind remained historically strong.

CFTC Positioning: Managed money funds extended their net short in corn to 95,867 contracts, reflecting
a defensive but not accelerating stance. In soybeans, the net long position narrowed to 14,624 contracts,

as funds trimmed exposure amid export uncertainty. Chicago SRW wheat remained the most imbalanced,
with net shorts climbing to 113,560 contracts, driven by bearish sentiment around global supply and a
lack of bullish catalysts. Overall, positioning shows skepticism toward the board without strong conviction
either way.

So What? Core US indicators remain supportive, particularly for corn and wheat. Export inspections are
strong, ethanol grind is still historically high, and funds remain heavily short with no new selling
momentum. Soybeans are more mixed: inspections are solid, but the NOPA crush shows modest
seasonal softening, and exports remain far behind year ago levels. With logistics still tight and South
American weather unresolved, basis remains the better signal of market tone than futures, and structural
support is holding, especially in corn.

International Reports

Brazil: The key development was confirmation of ANEC’s revised January projections, 3.23 million metric
tons of soybeans and 3.45 million metric tons of corn, reinforcing that exporters still expect strong
execution even with harvest just under five percent complete. Northern ports and carryover stocks
continue to supply the early window, but the forward curve will hinge on whether southern crop stress in
Paraná and Rio Grande do Sul forces yield downgrades heading into February and March. Dryness in the
south has not impacted current loadings, but it could tighten March and April FOB availability, potentially
shifting global price floors into the second quarter. Brazil remains the origin of choice for Asian feed
buyers, but logistics, rainfall, and late-crop visibility are now critical swing factors.

Argentina: The weather issue has moved from stress into confirmed damage. Weekly crop ratings
collapsed, with corn good to excellent falling 12 points and soybeans down eight, as both BAGE and
Rosario signaled irreversible yield loss in early-planted fields. No new policy shifts were announced, but
reductions in export taxes, which dropped rates to 24 percent on soybeans and 8.5 percent on corn,
remain in place, encouraging farmer sales. The market focus is whether crop confidence is high enough
to trigger increased farmer selling. Argentina’s FOB offers remain competitive on paper, around $225 per
metric ton for corn and $445 per metric ton for soymeal, but forward volume looks increasingly uncertain.
February rainfall will be decisive, but attention is now shifting from price to physical availability.

China: US export flashes to unknown, 192,000 metric tons of soybeans and 195,000 metric tons of corn,
suggest continued buying interest, but there was no visible escalation beyond routine flows. No new
multi-cargo forward purchases emerged, and speculation around reserve replenishment remains
unconfirmed. Dalian soybean futures rose around 38 yuan per metric ton, hinting at slightly improved
crush margins, but feed demand remains weak due to poor hog profitability. Until China steps in with
volume across multiple timeframes or origins, the tone stays basis-supportive, not structurally bullish.

EU / Black Sea: Russian 12.5 percent protein wheat was offered near $229 per metric ton FOB
Novorossiysk, with January exports tracking close to three million metric tons, below December highs but
still robust. The corridor remains open, and no major freight or policy disruptions occurred. Ukrainian corn
held steady around $235 per metric ton FOB via Danube, though buyer appetite stayed light. French
wheat (Rouen) was quoted near €189 per metric ton, or roughly $222, in line with current Euronext front-
month futures. The US retains selective competitiveness into Latin America, but Black Sea origin
continues to anchor global wheat pricing and limits urgency to shift demand.

So What? Brazil’s execution is holding, but forward yield risks in the south are becoming harder to ignore.
Argentina now faces confirmed crop losses, not just stress, with February weather pivotal for salvaging
volume. Russia’s wheat flow continues to dominate, capping global upside, while China remains present
but not aggressive. The overarching theme is still abundance on paper, but with mounting evidence of

tightening supply windows. Market tone is beginning to fracture, South American weather and Chinese
buying behavior are now the key global pivot points.

Weather & Crop Conditions

United States
Lows dropped below 15 degrees Fahrenheit in parts of Kansas and Nebraska, and subzero wind chills
recorded across Iowa, Illinois, and Indiana. Snow cover remained variable, sufficient across the Central
Plains and Delta, but patchy in northern Missouri and eastern Nebraska, raising localized winterkill
concerns in hard red winter wheat zones. NOAA’s six-to-ten day outlook projected above normal
temperatures across the Southern Plains and Southeast, with colder anomalies lingering over the
Northern Tier and Great Lakes. Precipitation remained below normal across much of the Corn Belt, while
wetter anomalies were centered along the Lower Mississippi Valley. River logistics continued to tighten,
as ice buildup on the Upper Mississippi and Illinois Rivers slowed barge movement and pushed freight
rates sharply higher, though no closures were reported. Gulf loading remained steady under ongoing draft
limitations.

Brazil
Harvest pace improved modestly but remains behind average, estimated at approximately 5 percent
complete nationwide as of January 25. The north to south weather divide persisted, as Mato Grosso,
Goiás, and Tocantins received steady rainfall, supporting pod fill and early harvest, while southern states
remained critically dry. Rio Grande do Sul, Paraná, and western Santa Catarina recorded less than 30
percent of normal January rainfall, and crop scouts flagged irreversible damage to late-planted soybeans
and first-crop corn. Forecast models offered little relief through early February. While no port disruptions
have been reported, southern crop stress is now central to Brazil’s forward supply tone and export pace
heading into the second quarter.

Argentina
Rainfall deficits worsened across key production zones, notably Córdoba, La Pampa, and central Buenos
Aires. Temperatures stayed high, with frequent daily highs exceeding 95˚ Fahrenheit, accelerating crop
stress. The Buenos Aires Grain Exchange cut corn good to excellent ratings to 15 percent and soybeans
to 20 percent, marking the lowest levels of the season and confirming widespread early yield loss.
Scattered showers reached parts of Santa Fe and Entre Ríos, but coverage was limited and insufficient to
shift crop trajectories. With most fields entering critical reproductive stages, the next ten days are pivotal.
If February opens dry, markets may begin pricing in material production losses.

Global
Winter wheat across Russia, Ukraine, and the European Union remained in dormancy under generally
sufficient snow cover. A mid-January cold snap brought temperatures of -25˚ Celsius to southern Russia,
but insulating snow layers prevented significant winterkill. No new threats emerged across Europe.
Ukrainian logistics via Danube ports and rail corridors continued without disruption. Australia’s wheat
harvest is complete, and Canadian grain export infrastructure remains stable. Outside South America,
global weather is broadly stable, with no major production alarms in play.

So What?
South America is now the core weather risk. Brazil’s north supports record soybean potential, but
southern stress is hardening, and crop loss risk is climbing without rainfall. In Argentina, confirmed yield
loss is now the baseline, not just a risk. The United States remains weather-stable, with no current threats
to wheat or logistics, despite regional cold. As February approaches, weather’s role shifts from
background to price-setter, particularly for corn and soybeans. Direction from here depends less on
sentiment and more on rain gauges.

Synthesis & Outlook

Futures finished firmer this week, but the more relevant signals remain embedded in basis and spreads.
March corn settled at $4.30 per bushel, soybeans at $10.74 , and soft red winter wheat near $5.35.
Despite headline carryout projections that still lean bearish, calendar spreads remain short of full carry,
CIF and processor basis are steady to firm, and execution across ethanol and export channels continues
to outperform board tone. Managed money trimmed exposure modestly but remains deeply net short in
corn and wheat, with no indication of commercial pressure forcing carry expansion. The board still reflects
paper weight, the physical market does not.

Corn continues to show the clearest structural divergence. CIF basis surged to +97H, river basis firmed
on barge constraints, and export inspections reached 1.51 million metric tons, the strongest since mid-
December. Ethanol grind dipped from record highs but remains historically strong, with stocks rebuilding
seasonally. Soybeans remain bifurcated: interior crush demand and processor bids anchor the front,
while exports remain muted and forward tone hinges on Brazil’s harvest pace and Argentina’s weather.
Wheat is still supply-capped by external forces; Russian free on board wheat sits near $229 per metric
ton, and January shipments continue at pace, limiting upside despite stable US inspections and extreme
speculative shorts.

Bullish triggers: Southern Brazil remains critically dry, with reproductive-stage soybeans showing visible
stress and no significant rainfall in the ten-day forecast. In Argentina, crop condition ratings have
collapsed, confirming early-stage yield losses in both corn and soybeans. US domestic demand continues
to show resilience, processor bids are holding firm, and while ethanol production has eased from recent
highs, grind remains strong enough to support grain movement. Additional Chinese buying beyond the
recent flash sales could tighten calendar spreads quickly, particularly in soybeans. Further barge
constraints or surprise flash sales could accelerate nearby firmness faster than the futures curve currently
reflects.

Bearish triggers: The Center-West region in Brazil is still tracking toward record corn and soybean
yields, and if southern conditions stabilize or Argentina receives meaningful rainfall in early February, the
overall supply tone could quickly revert. US ethanol production did decline last week, and any sustained
slowdown or softening in CIF basis would challenge the firmness seen in nearby corn structure. In wheat,
global free on board offers, particularly from Russia and Argentina, remain aggressive. Without a material
pickup in US export activity, wheat upside remains structurally capped despite speculative positioning.

Watchlist (Jan 29–Feb 05):

Argentina: Do crop ratings stabilize or deteriorate further?

Brazil: Is harvest momentum improving or stalling amid southern dryness?

CIF and interior basis: Signs of softening or continued strength?

Ethanol: Can production rebound, or does slowdown extend?

China: Any new US soybean demand beyond flash sales?

Wheat funds: Are shorts covering or deepening?

In summary: The grain complex remains fundamentally two-tiered. Futures reflect large theoretical
surpluses, but basis, spreads, and physical flows suggest localized tightness persists, especially in corn.
Without a demand shock or South American weather resolution, the market stays rangebound but firm

=== REPORT END ===
